# A.21 Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1/3 pages)

The Philippines is one of the countries most exposed to the impacts of climate change in the world. Ranked 4th on the list of countries with the highest number of deaths from extreme weather events between 2000 and 2019, the country is continually exposed to increasingly more frequent and more catastrophic extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones, droughts, floods, and irregular precipitation patterns. These climate change impacts are expected to increase in intensity and frequency, leading to disasters (GFDRR, 2012). Disaster risk levels rise with the occurrence of more intense tropical storms, including heavy rainfall, flooding, landslides, and storm surges, which can cause significant damage to infrastructure and agro-ecological zones. Consequently, rural and agricultural systems are becoming increasingly exposed to climate risks and the losses and damages associated with extreme weather events.

As part of the Philippines’ efforts in building resilience against climate change, farmers and farming communities indicate that large parts of the country will be further exposed to increased temperatures. The northern and central regions of the country are expected to experience more frequent droughts while the southern and eastern regions of the country in the south will be further impacted by decreased precipitation. Climate change (CC) predictions for cyclones are less certain but suggest an increase in intensity rather than frequency.

Through consultations with farmers, a range of climate-resilient agriculture (CRA) options were examined for the most exposed agricultural systems in the region’s most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. The project team identified several CRA practices that can be adopted by smallholder farmers including indigenous food production systems, which have shown emerging success and viability. They also point to the need for more resilient and more integrated farm systems as well as for farmers to adopt CRA practices that are relevant to the local context.

Agricultural production must shift from its baseline state characterized by extreme vulnerability to losses and damage due to climate change impacts. The project will support the development of a new agricultural paradigm in which stakeholders (government, private sector, and farming communities) are able to understand and implement climate-resilient agriculture practices that can be adopted at scale across the country’s agroecosystems. This requires target farming communities to be organized and to develop enterprises to sustain CRA practices and practices that will help them adapt to climate change.

The objective of this project: “Adapting Philippine Agriculture to Climate Change” (APAC), is to increase the resilience of rural men and women in areas vulnerable to climate change who depend on agriculture for their livelihoods. The overall goal of APAC is to ensure that increased agricultural productivity can be achieved through improved farmers’ capacity to develop CRA enterprises and adopt financing and economic incentives, as well as by strengthening the institutional framework and capacity to build supporting systems for scaling up. The project aims to achieve the following three outcomes:

- **Outcome 1:** Increased institutional capacities for the development and provision of climate information and advisory services.
- **Outcome 2:** Adoption of Climate Resilient Agriculture through enterprises by farmers (female and male).
- **Outcome 3:** Strengthened regulatory framework, market, and knowledge management for innovative agricultural practices.

The proposed project will build on the Adaptation and Mitigation Initiative in Agriculture (AMII) of the Government of the Philippines (GoPh). It will provide a systematic approach to climate change adaptation (CCA) for the long-term implementation of the Climate Change Act of 2016. The Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) will support the entire country while supporting integrating agriculture into the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) Act.

Through CCA enterprises, women’s groups, youth organizations, and the private sector to access climate information and technical services and to develop innovative agricultural practices that are relevant to their local context. The project will also build new partnerships along value chains. This will be achieved through the complementary buildup of CRA practices, appropriate finance and risk transfer, and the use of innovative enterprise digital technologies.


---
**Page 8**
---

GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 4

The APA project, the AMIA, and another proposed GCF project "Accelerating adoption of climate-resilient agricultural production through integrated landscape approach to manage climate risks" (PLAR!) which is being developed by Land Bank, are all aligned with the Green Climate Fund’s (GCF) strategic objective 1.2. These projects together in a program approach for a climate-resilient agricultural transformation in the Philippines. This is in line with the GCF’s goal of promoting “new agriculture” that is financially and economically viable and profitable.

The project activities will be delivered in at least nine provinces in five regions that have been identified as the most vulnerable to climate change, covering 20 million people (31% of the total rural population) and representing more than half of the Philippine rural population (20 million out of 31.3 million rural people). More than half of the rural population lives below the poverty line in these provinces. The project will focus on smallholder farmers, mainly poor farming household members (half of whom are women) in the nine target provinces and expand to other provinces as appropriate. It will also support the development of climate-resilient agricultural measures and incorporate climate-resilient and low-emission technologies into agricultural practices to adapt to climate change.

Over 5 million people living in the area will also benefit indirectly from enhanced information systems and strengthened institutional capacity, which will create an enabling environment for the widespread adoption of GCF.

The project is expected to mitigate climate change, producing an estimated reduction of 4.38 MtCO2-e over 20 years as a result of the application of CRA practices and better land use. It will also have economic co-benefit in the form of increased income from participation in CCA enterprises, while promoting gender equality and indigenous communities’ resilience.

1 Project "Accelerating adoption of climate-resilient agricultural production through integrated landscape approach to manage climate risks" (PLAR!) is in the design phase, currently being formulated by Land Bank.

# B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

## B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### Country context and background

1. **With a population of over 100 million people**, the Republic of the Philippines (the Philippines) is the world's 12th most populous country and is also among the most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.
   - The country is ranked 4th on the Long Term Climate Risk Index (2018 to 2006), and over 70 percent of the population is vulnerable to climate-related disasters. These include tropical cyclones, typhoons, floods, droughts, landslides, and forest fires.
   - The country's vulnerability is further exacerbated by its geographical location, with a coastline that spreads over 7,860 island chains, comprising both mountain ranges reaching as high as 3,000 meters and low-lying coastal territories resulting in a large disparity in population density and exposure to climate change responses.

2. **The agricultural sector is highly exposed to climate risks and variability in the form of floods, droughts (including prolonged dry spells), tropical cyclones, and typhoons**.
   - On average, more than 10 percent of arable land in the country is affected every year, and a recent trend analysis shows a steady increase in economic losses due to climate-related disasters. The agricultural sector accounts for approximately 25 percent of GDP, with 12 percent of agricultural damage attributed to climatological hazards, while the rest is attributed to tropical cyclones, floods, droughts, landslides, and forest fires.
   - These impacts can be devastating when they do happen, accounting for as much as one-half of annual agricultural damages. The Philippines has experienced a series of disasters in recent years, including three major events in the sector from disasters between October 2019 and February 2020 (three earthquakes, two typhoons, and the pandemic). These disasters have caused significant economic losses to the agricultural sector.
   - Up to 85 percent of the country's agricultural land could be affected by typhoons, floods, and droughts according to the World Bank and ADB (World Bank and ADB, 2021 Climate Risk Country Profile: Philippines).
   - Agricultural smallholders are particularly vulnerable to weather-related hazards and climate change. They have limited access to resources such as land, water, seeds, tools, and markets.
   - For example, poverty increased in regions affected by Typhoon Haiyan — one of the most powerful and destructive tropical cyclones on record. The 2013 typhoon caused significant damage to agriculture in the Philippines as Typhoon Odette, which swept through several regions. The initial estimate of the damage was close to $5 billion.
   - In addition, climate change has led to a decline in agricultural productivity in upland areas, where most of the country's indigenous communities live, have been shown to be robustly affected by climate change and are particularly vulnerable under increasing pressures from commercialized agricultural methods and non-agricultural livelihoods.

3. **In 2018**, agriculture accounted for 5 percent of the country’s GDP, while security and the service sector contributed more than half (54 percent) to the economy.
   - The agricultural sector is a key source of income for a large share of the population, providing food for one-third of the total labour force, mainly small-scale farmers. Smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable because they have limited access to resources such as land, water, seeds, tools, and markets (PFA 2018).
   - Apart from income generation, the agricultural sector is essential for food security and urban supply chains.
   - The Philippines has a high dependency on imports of rice, wheat, corn, soybeans, and other staple foods. Smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable to climate change because they have less control over resources and are especially vulnerable to climate risks.

4. **The COVID-19 pandemic and the current UK war triggered food, fuel, fertilizer, feed, and finance (FF4) crises**, which have had significant impacts on food security, supply and distribution systems in the Philippines.
   - The ongoing consequences of these crises adversely affect smallholder farmers and access to markets for their produce. Smallholder communities are more vulnerable to food insecurity because they have reduced food production capacity due to climate change.
   - The pandemic has also led to increased prices of the products suffering disproportionately, also being faced with rising unemployment rates and income reductions in the Philippines. These effects have further intensified the vulnerability of many poor rural communities, already struggling to cope with high exposure to climate risk and variability across the agri-food system.

# Climate change trends and impacts

## The Philippines has experienced rising temperatures over the last 50 years (PAGASA, 2011) and is predicted to see continued increases in the future (Garni et al., 2018; Villamil et al., 2016), especially during spring (March–May). The country has experienced more frequent extreme weather events such as typhoons, droughts, and wetter rainy season (July–September), especially in the northern and central regions of the country (Villamil et al., 2017; Villamil et al., 2016).

### Doubling CO2 concentrations with high associated AR4.5, could lead to the following impacts:

- There is an increasing trend in the number of days without rain in some provinces.
- An overall trend of decreasing rainfall intensity and increasing frequency of heavy precipitation events has been observed over the last 30 years (Cruz et al., 2018).
- Regional climate models suggest a reduction in annual mean rainfall and a decrease to the wet season (June–September) by up to 40% for the northern region, while the dry season (October–May) could increase by as much as 35% (Villamil et al., 2017; Garni et al., 2018).

### There is high confidence of an increase in extreme-heat days as well as in annual mean temperature in the future (PAGASA, 2011). The frequency and intensity of tropical cyclones (TCs) are projected to decrease due to higher sea surface temperatures (SST), while their duration will be shorter. However, the number of TCs with high associated AR4.5, could lead to the following impacts:

- There is a significant increase in the total number of cyclones, they indicate that there may be an increase in the proportion of category 4–5 (the highest categories) tropical cyclones and in associated extreme weather events such as storm surges, heavy rainfall, and flooding.

### Long-term, slow-onset climate change and the changing intensity and frequency of extreme events are predicted to have a negative impact on agricultural systems, with major food crops in tropical and temperate regions being most vulnerable (IPCC, 2014). The Philippines is one of the countries that will be most affected by climate change. Studies on the Philippines indicate that total crop production could decrease by 7 percent by 2050 due to climate change (Rogers et al., 2016; Thomas et al., 2016). This would result in a significant increase of 34 percent for maize and 13 percent for rice (Rogers et al., 2016; Thomas et al., 2016). These reductions will be more severe in the northern region, where there is a high confidence that the annual mean temperature could increase by up to 5°C. The studies also show the spatial distribution of the expected impacts in terms of 1) increased temperatures coupled with reduced rainfall and 2) increased temperatures coupled with increased rainfall.

### The Philippines emits an average of 9.8 metric tons of carbon dioxide equivalent per capita in 2020 (NDC). Phils emissions are also from digestible processes in animals (12 percent), livestock manure (13 percent), and the burning of solid waste (15 percent). By 2030, the country needs to reduce its total greenhouse gas methane emissions (mainly enteric fermentation) need to decline by 10 percent by 2030 and by 30 percent by 2050. The country also needs to reduce its CO2 emissions from energy use (coal, oil, and natural gas) and manure’s need to be reduced by 40 percent by 2030 and by 20 percent by 2050 (from 2010 levels) [data].

12: As a result of the agro-ecological diversity of the Philippines, key climate change impacts are region-specific, and the effects on existing baseline farming systems differ. For each region, the expected impacts are summarized in Table B.1.1. These impacts will be considered when selecting project target areas.

**Climate considerations and selected project target areas**

13: Project support will be delivered in 100 most vulnerable municipalities, located in nine Provinces across five Administrative Regions (out of 18 Administrative Regions), across four agroecological zones in the country. Box B.2 provides an overview of the selection criteria for these target areas. Further details on the selection criteria and process are provided in Appendix 4 and Annex 2-9, in brief:

• Agro-ecological zones were assessed against 2 climate change criteria: (i) decrease in rainfall and increase in temperature; and (ii) increase in precipitation intensity and frequency of extreme weather events. The most vulnerable agro-ecological zones were identified.

• Project municipalities (100) and provinces (85) were identified from the most climate change impacted agro-ecological zones to ensure the prioritization of the most vulnerable agricultural systems and communities. These were selected based on three criteria: (i) vulnerability; (ii) climate resilience profiles (CRPs); and (iii) willingness to participate in the project.

• Municipalities (100) will be jointly identified during the project inception phase and as part of the participatory CRA strategic planning process, which builds on climate resilience and vulnerability assessments (CRVAs) at the provincial level.

The CRVAs will help identify priority farming systems and farmer groups, which are the most vulnerable to climate change impacts. The data generated through the CRVA will serve to not only identify target municipalities and project beneficiaries but also provide a basis for developing a Provincial Climate Resilience Action Plan (CRA) that includes sector protection support by the public sector. The CRVAs will be the basis for the Provincial CRA Strategic Plan and

## Climate change impacts and farming systems in the locations of proposed interventions (other, second-priority climate impact areas, not selected, also shown)

### Box B.1.1 Project target site selection criteria

In addition to climate change criteria (decrease in rainfall and increase in temperature; increase in rainfall and tropical cyclones), a combined non-CC indicator was used to prioritize administrative regions and provinces. Some of the data used were:

- agricultural loss and damage
- administrative capacity
- poverty incidence
- number of food insecure families
- region DA capacity for coordination in project implementation

Municipalities within the province will be selected against the following criteria during project implementation:

- CC vulnerability
- climate justice
- willingness to support and counterpart
- integration of CC and disaster risk reduction (DRR) in local policies and budget
- representation of women, youth, and farmers
- relevant past and ongoing initiatives to build on

Beneficiaries within the municipalities will be selected against the following criteria:

- CC vulnerable groups
- CC-vulnerable farmer groups
- Smallholder farmers
- Agrarian reform beneficiaries
- Women
- Young farmers
- PDPs
- Special Area for Agricultural Development Program (SADP) or some criteria on poverty and food security
- Within municipalities, a CCA enterprise approach

### Existing national and sector policies, strategies, and programs

16. Based on the background analysis provided in Section 2.1 and 2.2 in Annex 2, the Climate Resilience Profiles (CRPs) for each project region identify key climate change risks, including extreme weather events and related systems and related value chains in project regions, as well as potential CTRA and adaptation options along with the value of these options to address the identified risks. The CRPs also highlight that the Philippines is vulnerable due to a negative impact on most of the key farming systems in the Philippines; it is likely to adversely affect the lives of an estimated 31 million people, in particular those who are poor and most vulnerable in the project target regions.

17. The Government of the Republic of the Philippines (GoPh) recognizes the need to address climate change through its National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP). The GoPh also recognizes the economic opportunities for small farmers, ensuring food security and enhancing sustainability and resilience. Its ambitions are stipulated in the National Framework on Climate Change (NFCC), which sets out a roadmap for the country’s efforts to build capacities to generate and use climate science for risk-informed development planning (See Annex E-5.1 and Section 2.A.4,Annex A.2).


---
**Page 15**
---

18. In addition to the NDC, the government has also been active in setting out policies and high-level plans for addressing climate change, such as the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), the Joint Roadmap of the Cabinet Cluster on Climate Change, the Philippine Climate Change Strategy Paper (PCCSP) 2015-2030, and related laws. The Philippine Commission on Women recognizes the impact of climate change on women, as well.

In 2021 NDC, the Philippines commits to a projected GgCO2 emission reduction and avoidance for the 2020–2030 period of 75 percent, of which 2.71 percent is unconditional and 72.29 percent is conditional, representing an absolute reduction in emissions from baseline levels of 486 MtCO2e by 2030 across all sectors. This commitment is referenced against a projected business-as-usual cumulative-wide emission of 653.5 MtCO2e for the same period.

Clinical trials of vaccines are increasingly mainstreaming into sectoral policies, strategies, and plans. Both the Philippine Development Plan (PDP) 2017–2022 and the National Agriculture and Fisheries Modernization Act (NAMFARMA) include provisions on climate change adaptation and mitigation as well as other related climate-related actions. The NAMFARMA provides a vision for the agri-food sector, reorienting many “banner” programs to focus more on climate-resilient agricultural production systems and practices.

This emphasizes the shift in CCA as a key development strategy in the country, which involves alignment of government policies and programs with the national adaptation plan (NAP) and other international commitments. It also highlights experiences in the context of the urgency of the climate crisis. Some of the most relevant and recent initiatives and approaches are described below.

21. The DA has launched the Adaptation and Mitigation Initiative in Agriculture (AMA) programme in 2014. AMA is a science-based framework with significant institutional support and linkages to key technologies related to climate change adaptation and mitigation, including the Climate Change Act of 2007, the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), the Philippine Development Plan (PDP) 2017–2022, the Agriculture and Fisheries Modernization Act (NDC), and others. The DA has so far established 57 AMI villages in 15 regions of the country, the NGO Rice Watchers, Inc., and other partners. These villages are being used as a model for climate change adaptation and mitigation.

The initiative is also being used to build capacity among farmers and other stakeholders on climate change issues through training and awareness raising activities. The AMI villages have been established with institutional support from national, regional, provincial, and LGU (local government units) institutions, private sector partners, and NGOs. These villages are being used as a model for climate change adaptation and mitigation.

23. Further, the Agricultural Credit Policy Council (ACPC), under the DA and the Land Bank have developed some credit products to address the needs of small-scale farmers vulnerable to or affected by climate risks. However, these products are still in their pilot phase and are not yet mainstreamed into the national agricultural development mapping and climate resilience and vulnerability assessment tools, which are being rolled out across the country.

The ACPC has also been working closely with the Land Bank and ACPC microfinance programmes to facilitate access of small-scale farmers to credit for climate-resilient agricultural production systems and practices such as agroforestry, tree-based farming, and pipeline agriculture and rural development projects with the WFP, ADB, and other donors. See Appendix 7 and Section 2.

24. Other government agencies also implement CCA programmes that benefit agriculture. The Philippine Atmospheric, Geophysical and Astronomical Services Administration (PAGASA)—one of the agencies attached to the National Economic and Development Authority (NEDA) under the Department of Budget and Management (MBM) agency of the Philippines, mandated to provide technological services in meteorology, hydrology, climatology, geophysics, astronomy, and related fields—has developed a Climate Change Adaptation Programme (Republic Act No. 10692 or the PAGASA Modernization Act), which enhances its capability in

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 12

## B

### Barriers inhibiting the transition from baseline to climate-resilient systems

#### Weak and incomplete localized climatic information for agriculture: Addressing the limited availability of localized climatic data is a key challenge in developing an effective agricultural adaptation strategy, as it limits the impact of climate change. Based on consultations and studies (Annex 2, Sections 2.6 and 2.8), there is a considerable gap between the needs of farmers and other stakeholders for localized climatic information and related advisory and information, and its developers' methods of use in decision-making processes and services. This project will further reduce the resilience of farmers and farming systems, thus exacerbating food insecurity and poverty. The Philippines has already set up several climate change adaptation projects under the National Framework on Vulnerabilities and Adaptation (Cruz et al., 2017), submitted to IPCC) and emphasize the need to integrate climatic information into agricultural development strategies. In order to address these gaps in localized climatic data for future climate-change-related projects, the Philippines must address the following key barriers:

#### Incomplete agrometeorological climate data generation is a cause of the above-mentioned barrier: There are several reasons why this is so. First, there has been limited investment in developing and maintaining weather stations in critical climate change–impacted areas (Annex 2, Sections 2.6 and 2.8). A detailed inventory of existing weather stations and their locations will be conducted as part of the baseline assessment for this project, in the context of the agency's long-term Modernization Programme. Previous projects have set up stations in critical climate change–impacted areas (e.g., the PAGASA Climate Change Adaptation Project) and positioning to generate the agrometeorological data needed to assess long-term trends and complete local networks. However, there has been limited coordination among agencies involved in the development of such systems, and information flow between the DA and PAGASA for the development and operation of such systems is not effective. The need for a coordinated approach to the agrometeorological network must be urgently rectified.

#### Institutional capacity and coordination for climate services remain weak: Recent consultations among government agencies are not clear on data collection and dissemination, and agrometeorological advisory issuance. Established processes and communication channels exist for certain services (e.g., weather forecasts), but these are not necessarily the same as those used to disseminate information for non-climate normal climate information services. Climate service providers operate rather independently, and collaboration among agencies is limited. The majority of climate data and information generated by PAGASA is disseminated through various channels but in a top-down approach, and information often flows indirectly and slowly. There are no established processes to ensure that the needs of farmers and other stakeholders are met, and there is no effective process for users and disseminators to provide feedback to the producers of climate

## Weak access to appropriate adaptation and mitigation technologies
Knowledge and awareness of appropriate adaptation and mitigation technologies are limited in many countries. This is particularly true for smallholder farmers, service providers and private sector actors operating along the relevant value chains. There is a recurring issue that climate-smart agricultural practices are often not adopted by farmers because they have not been produced on a large scale and rarely have been developed as localized packages that also indicate the required inputs (e.g., seeds, fertilizers). In addition, there is limited access to information about climate-resilient seeds and propagules for stress-tolerant varieties are poorly developed. (Annex 2, Subsection 2.3.8.) The lack of knowledge and awareness of appropriate adaptation and mitigation technologies is also reflected in user groups. Advisory services for CCA practices need to be tailored to localized climate risks and information.

### Limited systems for extension and outreach on agriculture and climate change
As part of the project design, a key challenge will be to ensure that the FFSs are able to provide farmers with access to climate change information in different forms of extension including farmer based schools (FBS). The study found that there is limited capacity within the FFSs to provide this type of service. However, the FFSs have resilience needs into extension activities. The Philippines has significant experience with FFTS which have evolved from the original FFS model and now include climate change adaptation as a key component in their methodologies based on several capacity development efforts over the years. However, the FFTS and FBS are not yet fully integrated within the FFSs and there is limited capacity to provide extension services for climate change adaptation.

### Lack of climate change focus in local agricultural plans and programs
Limited systems for extension and outreach on agriculture and climate change

### Limited access to market, finance, risk-mitigation tools
Limited access to market, finance, risk-mitigation tools. Limited private sector engagement for climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices is a major barrier to scaling up CSA in many countries. To support farmers’ access to market, credit and insurance to alleviate risks and increase their willingness to invest in CSA, the project will provide technical assistance to the relevant government agencies. The Agriculture Credit Policy Council (ACPC) in 2014 highlighted the credit gap of PHP 364 billion ($US 7.8 billion) for private sector lending to farmers. The project will support the ACPC in its efforts to ensure that banks and other financial institutions provide adequate financing for CSA activities, including through guarantees on agricultural production loans granted by accredited private financing institutions and other lenders. The project will also support the ACPC in its efforts to ensure that credit risk management is limited. According to ACPC, the credit gap has been reduced from PHP 364 billion ($US 7.8 billion) in 2015 to PHP 91 billion (or $US 2.0 billion) in 2019 for top 20 banks in PHP 2024 and is projected to grow by more than 30% over the next five years, reaching PHP 118 billion ($US 2.6 billion). The project will support the ACPC in its efforts to ensure that credit risk management is limited. According to ACPC, the credit gap has been reduced from PHP 364 billion (or $US 7.8 billion) in 2015 to PHP 91 billion (or $US 2.0 billion) in 2019 for top 20 banks in PHP 2024 and is projected to grow by more than 30% over the next five years, reaching PHP 118 billion ($US 2.6 billion). The project will support the ACPC in its efforts to ensure that credit risk management is limited.

## Market and Policy Environment

This is compounded by farmers' low awareness, low financial and technical capacities, and the general lack of information on risks (especially climate-related) that is required to develop appropriate adaptation strategies. The majority of indigenous communities in the Philippines are also largely marginally engaged in climate change adaptation and mitigation. Private sector awareness and engagement in renewable energy and agro-ecological practices are also low. In addition, many indigenous farmers lack seeds and propagules for stress-tolerant varieties are poorly developed (Annex 2, Sub-section 2.5.3). Although not eligible to access government credit programs, they are also ineligible to apply for commercial loans out lenders, making it difficult for female-headed households to access credit and insurance. Many indigenous peoples lack the capacity to engage in climate change adaptation and mitigation.

### Unsustainable CCA practice adoption without market and enterprise approaches

There are several CCA practices that enhance farmers' capacity to minimize climate change impacts on agriculture. However, the additional costs of these practices are often too high for smallholder farmers who have limited access to finance and skills to combat climate change may not be sustained if such a CCA practice is not economically viable.

### Unrecognized indigenous traditions; greater disadvantages faced by indigenous communities

Existing programs and policies do not take sufficient account of IP and female farmers' actual and present needs. Indigenous peoples are often excluded from accessing government credit, insurance, and advisory services as they are not registered at birth and lack official documentation. Indigenous communities have long relied on traditional knowledge, practices, and institutions to manage their natural resources in a sustainable way, as well as a strong traditional community-based mutual assistance system. However, their knowledge, traditions, skills, and systems of governance are often unrecognized or undervalued by the government. These have proven track record of resilience against external shocks and thus playing a key role in adapting to climate change pressures. Indigenous peoples' capacity for adaptation is undermined when their traditional knowledge, social systems resilience and sustainability have diminished due to rapidly changing environmental and socioeconomic conditions. The loss of these capacities has also led to increased vulnerability among indigenous communities and personal safety. Although unemployment rates are not available for indigenous peoples, the rate for persons aged 15-24 was many times than that for the total population in 2020.

## Proposed Project Approach

### Building on past and ongoing government programs/projects

The project will seek to help the LGUs drive climate change adaptation and mitigation in the Philippines. It will use the proven successful experiences and approaches of the AIMA programme and the CRFS while remaining flexible to accommodate emerging information needs. The project will also build on the experience gained from the CCA-IP project, which has used climate information services (CIS) tailored to their needs; strengthen access to technologies, finance, and market information for indigenous farmers through a coordinated approach that integrates CIS with agro-ecologically appropriate, and promote resilient livelihoods. The project outputs will help mainstream CCA services into the existing government programs and projects such as the Climate Change Resilience System Management (CRSM) systems, agrarian reform, and other government programmes. Farmer groups under the project will be able to access climate information services through a coordinated approach that integrates farmer learning about CCA, coupled with the development of supportive financial instruments by Land Bank, APOG, and other financial institutions. The project will also support the LGUs in developing adaptation plans for their project's target regions to sustainably adopt and scale up climate change adaptation and mitigation measures and practices.

### Climate Information Services (CIS)

Climate Information Services (CIS) will be provided through institutionalized, inter-sectoral, coordinated collection and analysis of climate, agromet, and other data (such as data on soil, water, evapotranspiration).

## B

### 40. Climate Resilient Agriculture (CRA) Services include efforts to support organization of farmer groups; provide capacity building; identify climate risks; support pioneering CRA options within farmers' fields; provide access to inputs, including seeds and fertilizers; provide technical assistance on the use of new technologies and equipment and farm advises based on climate and weather outlook; coordinate with partners (LGA, agricultural extension services, etc.) to ensure that the services are provided in a timely manner. These services include financing and insurance services; support production and post-harvest facilities; provide technical assistance for business planning and management; provide training and capacity building for farmers and other stakeholders on indicators (GI)2 and conduct monitoring and evaluation. The project envisages the development of CRA services that are tailored to the specific needs and priorities of target beneficiaries, including smallholder farmers, agroforestry farming systems, as well as the needs and priorities of target beneficiaries. In keeping with the proven notion that capacity building is a key element in ensuring the sustainability of any intervention, the project will build on local and indigenous knowledge. F&A is its capacity as AE and ED and shall ensure that the project implementation benefits from best- and tried approaches within and outside the country, with a particular focus on participatory approaches.

### 41. Based on research by the DA, FAO, and CIAT and further cost-benefit analysis, a range of CRA options (listed in Appendix B) have been identified for each region/province where climate change risks and/or regions/provinces become more resident (Table B.2). These CRA options have important cross-cutting implications for the project's overall objectives and will be implemented as part of the project. They are emerging from good soil and water management and integrated and diversified systems, which will need to be tailored to specific needs and priorities of target beneficiaries. The project envisages that CRA services will play a role alongside crops in these integrated farming systems (See Appendices 2 - Summary of key climate change risks for each region/province; Appendix 3 - Selected CRA options for each region/province; Appendix 4 - Ecological region and associated farming systems, and Appendix 5 - Selected CRA practices and technologies by crop). The project will also support the development of a range of value chain adaptation activities for farmers, including access to safeguarded input systems, such as seed storage (honeycombs), improved post-harvest facilities, and other inputs. These services are expected to be provided even during extreme events. Section 2.2 of Annexes A and Appendix B - Climate resilient profiles provide the analysis that underpins these recommended CRA options and practices/technologies along the value chains in each region.

### 42. Identification of these CRA options is also based on stakeholder consultations that reflect smallholder perspectives, gender equality, and indigenous knowledge. The project will consider the importance of gender issues in climate-resilient agriculture and issues of indigenous food systems including land tenure, access to resources, and other factors that may affect their ability to adapt to climate change. These options are designed to strengthen the resilience of indigenous farming systems (Annex 2). Recommended actions considered in the project include: strengthening the capacity of local organizations and institutions for the promotion of CRA for indigenous food production systems; increasing the awareness of farmers about the importance of adaptation, strengthening DAP's capacity, and the relevance of indigenous agricultural practices and knowledge to climate change adaptation; increasing the availability of effective inputs and the efficiency and quality of value chains of indigenous products.

* **P4** is a low-cost, locally based system of quality assurance with a strong focus on social control and knowledge building. This system provides an opportunity for farmers to identify and address problems in their farming systems before they make decisions; visit farms, support each other's efforts, and share experiences that are producing according to the Organic Standard (Organic Standards and Guidelines for Organic Production (OSG) | FARM).
* **GI** are signs which identify a crop as originating in a particular region or country, where a given quality, reputation or other characteristic is attributable to its geographical origin. GI products are protected by law and their use is restricted; they are met by producers, and typically apply environmentally sound practices (https://www.fao.org/green-agricultural-indicators).

### 43. CRA Enterprises will be developed with target farmer learning groups, which will eventually be clustered into a part of the AMA Village network.

The CRA Enterprise development process is based on the formation and evolution of farmer learning groups that are organized around specific business practices to improve economies of scale in production, access to services and markets. CRA Enterprise development will draw on the experience gained from the implementation of the Climate Resilient Agricultural Model (CRAM) and the Fostering - focused on production), (c) Farmer Business School (FBS) – focused on market orientation, access, and enterprise development), and (d) the Climate Risk Management System (CRMS) – focused on climate change adaptation. The CRA Enterprises will be developed in collaboration with the AMA Village network.

#### 43a. CRA Enterprise development under the APA adopts the CRIP approach as well as the guidelines [Help of established farmer learning groups to link up with institutionalized CCA and CRA services and to emphasize the value chain and the commercial aspects of farming enterprises, hence facilitating vertical integration].

- a. Each farmer learning group would be composed of around 30 members – this number has been found to be optimal for effective knowledge sharing and capacity building as well as for developing collective capacity.
- b. These groups will be organized into CRA Enterprises that are linked with the AMA Village to meet the criteria that each AMA Village covers 100 hectares under CCA practices.
- c. Each CRA Enterprise will have a minimum of three farmers who are willing to share knowledge and income potentials.
- d. The CRA Enterprises/AMA Villages will build on and contribute to scaling up of smaller-scale initiatives into larger scale projects, such as the Climate Change Adaptation and Resilience (CAR) Community Guarantee System (CGS) and Geographical Indicators (GI).
- e. Participating farmers in a CRA Enterprise are selected from among peers with whom they can effectively collaborate.
- f. CRA enterprises are specific to agro-ecological zones and the socio-economic characteristics of the region, and therefore, inter alia, gender and age groups are not isolated.

#### 43b. CRA Enterprises will be built on cooperative development experiences and in partnership with cooperative apex organizations and farmer networks such as the MASAPAG and the Philippine Cooperative Centre (PCC). The CRA Enterprise business and investment planning will inform the re-creation of the Community Development Fund (CDF) under the umbrella of the Community Development Fund (CDF) services. Section 2.10 of Annex 2 reviews the legal policy framework and supporting institutional arrangements for CDFs. The CRA Enterprises will be developed based on their performance, success factors, and challenges and their presence in the project target regions/provinces. This provides a strong rationale for the project's proposed approach in developing CRA enterprises.

#### 43c. The role of the farmer learning groups is to provide technical assistance and guidance during the provincial CRA strategic planning. The CRA Enterprise business and investment planning will inform the re-creation of DA-based CDFs, which will be managed by the AMA Village network. The establishment of a catalytic grant support will enable the implementation of the CRA Enterprises investment plans. The AMA Villages will serve as hubs for learning, mainstreaming, and scaling up beyond the project target regions.

# Box B.2 Continuous trial, learning, and feedback in developing farmer enterprises – key for sustainable adaptation of Climate Resilient Agriculture

The Philippines must continually adapt to market opportunities as well as constantly changing climatic conditions and increasing climate variability. The ability of farmers and communities in general must be able to choose from a range of options that are appropriate at the farm, agro-ecological zone, and community level. Improved resilience also relies on increased diversification of household livelihoods, including livestock husbandry and other income-generating activities.

The project will support farmer enterprises through continuous trial, learning, and feedback in developing farmer groups, thus to CRA enterprises, to continuously adopt and adapt CRA options that would lead to sustainability and economic growth. The project will use the following tools: CRACenter, CRACenter, CRACenter, CRACenter, CRACenter, CRAMonitoring System will enable knowledge-sharing and learning.

## 4 Leveraging finance for sustainable CRA adoption

The APA project design, particularly Output 2.1 - CRA Enterprise Development, is focused on financial instruments – are geared toward this objective. The training and investment planning process of CRA enterprises will be supported by the project to scale up CRA practices. The project also envisages significant engagement of Land Bank, as a key stakeholder in the project, to provide credit facilities for CRA enterprises, and support CRA adoption. This includes: 1) increasing the private sectors and financial institutions' understanding of the benefits of CRA practices; 2) improving the capacity of the private sector and financial institutions to identify and support CRA adoption; and 3) enhancing the private sector and financial institutions ability to improve existing products (such as the Land Bank's CRA Finance Programme) or develop new ones that will be more attractive for CRA enterprises. The project will also provide training on credit risk management, which will help reduce the risks of moral hazard, improving the supply and demand for financial services in rural areas.

## 4 Mainstreaming CRA will be instrumental for sustainability and scaling up

During its implementation, the project will shift major agricultural investments under the DA banner programmes toward supporting CRA practices and CRA enterprises. The project will also support the development of a CRACenter Monitoring and Evaluation system, and expanding farmer outreach at scale through knowledge sharing. It will work with the private sector to develop new products that are more attractive for CRA enterprises.

Additionally, farmer group capacity-building and CRA Enterprise development will encourage farmers, LGUs, and other financial service providers to finance sustainable CRA adoption and scaling up.

## APA and PILAR linkage

### Adopting Philippine Agriculture to Climate Change (APAC) can be implemented in parallel to UPLB's SRI project. The APAC project is a 5-year project that aims to mainstream climate-resilient agricultural practices, such as SRI, into the national agriculture sector. The project will be funded by the World Bank, and the best practices will be shared with other stakeholders.

The APA project will also work closely with the Philippine Institute of Crop Science (PICSC) and the Land Bank, a GFOC Direct Access Entity, in collaboration with the International Rice Research Institute (IRRI) and the World Agriculture Organization. The project will develop two sets of packages for scaling up CRA practices and technologies: 1) development of climate-resilient financial products; 2) strengthening of credit risk management.

The APA project will also work closely with PILAR, APAC, CFS and CRA services and CRA Enterprise investment planning will provide fundamental information and learning for PILAR CRA investment planning (Component 3). The project will also support the development of a CRACenter Monitoring and Evaluation System with Land Bank in the use of CIS. Improving Land Bank CRA Finance Programme to be accessible by APA beneficiaries, as well as other stakeholders, will help scale up CRA adoption, while also helping Land Bank build the capacity to develop new, more climate-resilient financial packages for CRA enterprises. The project will ensure consistent information sharing and knowledge management in promoting CRA.

## Theory of Change (TOC): Drawing on the learnings from the AMA Village approach and the findings of the PHSCCAP working group on adaptation in the agricultural sector and stakeholder feedback on policy challenges identified by the project (Sections 2.2 and 4.2 of Annex 2), the project will address the following three Strategic Pillars for Mainstreaming:

- Make CRA services, tools, capacity, and processes from the Government and private sectors available to climate-vulnerable communities to make CRA adoption mainstream.
- Catalyze a shift in policy and institutional responses to climate change by using the outputs and lessons learned from this project.
- Build institutions and mechanisms to support farmers in addressing barriers, adopting a range of technologies and practices.

## Strategic Pillars for Mainstreaming:

1. Make CRA services, tools, capacity, and processes available to climate-vulnerable communities to make CRA adoption mainstream.
2. Catalyze a shift in policy and institutional responses to climate change by using the outputs and lessons learned from this project.
3. Build institutions and mechanisms to support farmers in addressing barriers, adopting a range of technologies and practices.

## The project activities respond to the most critical barriers identified under Pillar I and the above key strategic pillars in mainstreaming CRA:

- Project activities will address the following five critical barriers:
  - Limited access to information on climate change and adaptation.
  - Limited availability of climate-resilient agricultural inputs and technologies.
  - Limited access to extension services and training for farmers.
  - Limited access to credit, finance, and insurance products that are climate-resilient.
  - Limited awareness among policy makers and other stakeholders about the importance of CRA.

## Project Activities:

- **Project Outputs:**
  - Improved access to information on climate change and adaptation.
  - Increased availability of climate-resilient agricultural inputs and technologies.
  - Enhanced access to extension services and training for farmers.
  - Improved access to credit, finance, and insurance products that are climate-resilient.
  - Increased awareness among policy makers and other stakeholders about the importance of CRA.

- **Project Outcomes:**
  - Increased adoption of CRA by smallholder farmers in climate-vulnerable communities.
  - Enhanced resilience of smallholder farming communities to climate change impacts.
  - Improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers through increased agricultural productivity and income generation.

# B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

## Project Objective

60. The project objective is to increase the resilience of rural men and women who depend on agriculture for their livelihoods in the face of climate change by strengthening the capacity of farmers to develop CRA Enterprises and adopt financially and economically viable CRA practices, and the Government and private sectors' capacity to build supporting systems for scaling up.

61. The project aims to achieve the following three outcomes:
- Outcome 1: Increased capacities for the development and provision of climate information and Climate Resilient Agriculture services
- Outcome 2: Strengthened financial and economic viability of farmers (smallholders) through enterprises; and
- Outcome 3: Strengthened regulatory framework, market, and learning for mainstreaming and scaling up

62. The project's objectives and outcomes will be achieved by improving the DSI, PAGASA, and LGUs' capacity and infrastructure to provide climate information services and CRA services, developing and disseminating CRA packages and delivering these services to farmers and other users, especially private sector (Component 1). The project will also strengthen the financial and economic viability of farmers through the development and CRA services for decision-making. The project will work directly with a large number of CC-vulnerable farmers and their groups to develop CRA Enterprises, strengthening farmers' capacity to utilize services and adopt CRA.


---
**Page 28**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 24

## Target Beneficiaries

63. The project aims to directly benefit 1.25 million most vulnerable farming household members (250,000 households) in the nine target provinces and indirectly benefit 8 million people in five regions. The 250,000 direct beneficiaries are farmers/femaleheads/indigenous people who will be provided with access to innovative CRA practices, techniques, and financial services, as well as investments in community resilience-building projects that will help them adapt to climate change impacts on their livelihoods and ecosystems. Out of these direct beneficiary farming households, 45,000 poorest, most vulnerable and food-insecure farmers/femaleheads/indigenous people will be provided with training and capacity building development training and a CRA project package to enable them to engage in and drive climate-resilient and sustainable agriculture practices. The remaining 180,000 direct beneficiaries will receive training and capacity building, peer learning from the CRA enterprises and also catalytic support through DA parallel finance.

The project will benefit 500 farmer organizations (FOs) and 200 professional beneficiaries – staff of DA and PAGASA, LGUs, and other relevant agencies such as DARD, DENR, and others. The FOs will be provided with training and capacity building development to enable them to provide the provision of sustained and effective CIS, CRA, and related services. The farmers and professional beneficiaries will also receive access to financial services (e.g., credit, insurance, inputs, equipment tools, etc.), banks, financial institutions, and resource providers in using CIS and CRA services to meet their needs for climate-resilient and sustainable agriculture practices.

# Project Components, Outputs and Activities

## Component 1: Institutional capacities for Climate Resilient Agriculture services development

### 68. Activities under this Component aim to address the capacity gaps identified in implementing the AMA and other DA programs and projects. The project will develop a climate information system that provides an effective delivery and feedback system of information and services. The activities will ensure PAGASA and DA collaborate with other stakeholders such as local governments, farmers' organizations, private sector, and CSOs to make available for wider use by farmers and others at appropriate times. The project will provide tools and training for government agencies and other stakeholders in the development and delivery of climate information services and CRA service packages to Farmers; the private sectors, and other stakeholders. FAO's big data lab, climate risk assessment, and early warning systems will be used as a basis for developing climate information services such as PAEZ, remote sensing technologies developed under the partnership with Google, FAO's investment in climate change adaptation and mitigation projects, and other relevant tools and technologies.

The Philippines is able to continue using remote sensing products made available through EarthMap, Google Earth Engine, and other relevant tools and technologies. The project will also ensure that the information systems are integrated into the climate information services, and for the C4 platform which will be fully owned and maintained by DA and PAGASA.

### 67. This component will develop institutional capacity from national to municipality levels for the production, communication, and utilization of localized CR and CRA services. It will establish and institutionalize inter-agency coordination mechanisms at all levels (national, provincial, municipal, and barangay) for the production, communication, and delivery of services across different levels; it will also facilitate coordination and two-way communication with key beneficiaries such as farmers' organizations, local government units, CSOs, and other stakeholders. The perspectives are a key input in the development of information products, and farmer involvement will be part of the project's activities. It is expected that the project will provide opportunities for dialogue between indigenous groups and other farmers to ensure their knowledge and experiences in CRS and related practices and outcomes will be reflected in the capacity development efforts—training material, methodology, and other products and services.

### 68. The localized CRA and CSIS activities will be carried out under Component 4/Activity through enterprises and/or cooperatives. The project aims to: (a) build all CGIAR centers' capacities under PLURR, and users outside of project areas to improve decision making (such as in developing and modifying credit risk models); (b) develop a climate information system that provides an effective delivery and feedback system of information and services; (c) share the identified resilient farming practices with other enablers through farmer field schools or farmer-to-farmer exchanges.

The project will also ensure that the information systems are integrated into the climate information services, and for the C4 platform which will be fully owned and maintained by DA and PAGASA.

### III: Establish Technical Working Groups for CIS and CRA services
Under the Programme Steering Committee (PSC), a Technical Working Group (TWG) is established at national, regional and provincial levels to provide technical guidance on the implementation of TWGs. A TWG is also established as a feedback mechanism between DA, PAGASA, and other key agencies in developing and disseminating localized CIS and CRA products and services.
Existing regional TWGs may be expanded and their capacities will be enhanced.

### IV: Establish Local Technical Working Groups (LTWG)
The beneficiary LGUs may choose to constitute their own local TWGs (at provincial and municipal levels) to mirror the programme structure of the national TWG. The LTWGs will provide technical guidance on work under the PAFES centrestation or through similar mechanisms at their provincial or municipal agricultural offices.

#### 71. All the CIS and CRA products and services developed by the TWGs will fully reflect local contexts, farmer needs and priorities. A feedback mechanism shall be established to ensure a continuous improvement of CIS and CRA services. The LTWGs will provide technical guidance on work under LGUs, which will reflect the dynamics of climate change and the local context on a continuous basis.

#### 72. The TWGs will consolidate and update a core set of national, regional, and local climate, agricultural, and other relevant data and information to be used in developing CIS products and services as well as advisory products for major farming systems. FAD will introduce its big data lab and new tools developed for climate change adaptation and mitigation. The TWGs will also provide technical guidance on the use of these tools.
The LTWGs will ensure the integration of remote sensing and other big data, local agro-ecological and socio-economic information, and other relevant data and information in developing CIS products and services to improve understanding of requirements for CIS and CRA services in order to continuously improve production, delivery, and application of CIS and CRA services. The LTWGs will also provide technical guidance on the use of international CRA experiences while incorporating adaptation from strategic foresight planning (see Activity 2.1.1).

#### 73. The Central TWG will be hosted by the DA-FD Programs Operational Planning Division (OPPD) and members include representatives from DA, PAGASA, DILG, DENR, NEDA, and other partners and stakeholders such as Land Bank, PhilRice academia, and private sector. The LTWGs will be hosted at the provincial level and will consist of representatives from LGUs, DA-GENR, DARD, and NEA, and other partners and stakeholders such as Land Bank, PhilRice academia, and private sector.
The LTWG meetings will be held on a quarterly basis while the Central TWG meetings will be held biannually. The LTWGs will be hosted by DA-FDRO in members, in addition to the regional counterparts of the membership at the central level. Both LTWGs and the Central TWG are operational coordination mechanisms with regard to climate and agricultural services.
The Central TWG provides technical guidance on work under LGUs, which will reflect the dynamics of climate change and other relevant data and information in developing CIS products and services. ## Establish climate service information (CIS) platform. CIS Platforms will be developed for sharing and disseminating standardized climate, agrimeteorological advisories, and CSA services provided by TWDG, PAGASA, and DA. A web-based information platform, functional at central, regional, and local levels, to share information on climate and weather services, agricultural advisories, and CSA services.

### At the central level, the Field Programs Operational Planning Division (FOPPD) will set up and manage

- The CIS Platform will be the key climate information and CSA service and advisory hub in the country.
    - The CIS Platform will be a web-based information platform functional at central, regional, and local levels to share information on climate and weather services, agricultural advisories, and CSA services.

### At the regional level, the Regional Programs Operational Planning Division (RFOPD) will set up and manage

- The CIS Platform will be the key climate information and CSA service and advisory hub in the country.
    - The DA RFOs will have a specific page to store and share region-specific data and information. PAGASA Centers will provide weather advisories, agricultural advisories, and other relevant information to the DA RFOs. The CIS Platform will be linked to PAGASA Regional Services Division, which in turn generates and provides localized forecasts and agrimeteorological advisories for each region. The CIS Platform will also link to the regional reporting system, which will translate the information into localized agrimeteorological advisories and CSA services.

### At the local level

- The CIS Platform will be a web-based information platform functional at central, regional, and local levels to share information on climate and weather services, agricultural advisories, and CSA services.
    - The CIS Platform will also include a mechanism for sharing and communicating various informational products and advisory services. This will be done through the use of dedicated communication channels, with feedback loops and periodic forums on various topics related to CIS and CSA.

## Five Regional and Nine Provincial CIS Centers will be established or improved that are connected to the DA RFOs and PAGASA Centers respectively

- The Regional CIS Centers will be located in DA-RFOs, while Provincial CIS Centers will be at the Office of the Provincial Agriculturist (OPA). These centers will provide climate services and CSA services. They will also collect some equipment while DA and LGUs will provide human resources and other necessary support.
    - The Regional CIS Centers will be responsible for the region-specific page of the CIS Platform, forecasting, agrimeteorological advisories, and early warnings. They will be responsible for the region-specific page of the CIS Platform, forecasting, agrimeteorological advisories, and early warnings.

- The Provincial CIS Centers will provide climate services and CSA services to LGUs in their respective provinces.
    - The Regional CIS Centers and also regional TWDGs will be in Region I: Benguet, Ifugao, Kalinga; Region II: Abra, Apayao, Cagayan; Region III: Batanes, Bicol Region, Nueva Ecija, Northern Mindanao, and Region XIV: SOCCSKSARGEN.
    - The Provincial CIS Centers will provide climate services and CSA services to LGUs in their respective provinces. They are located in Region V: Bulacan, Cavite, Laguna; Region VI: Batangas, Quezon, Rizal; Region VII: Albay, Camarines Norte and Sorsogon; Region VIII: Cebu, Cotabato, Davao Oriental, Davao del Sur, General Santos City, Misamis Occidental, North Cotabato, South Cotabato, Zamboanga del Norte, and Zamboanga del Sur.
    - The Regional CIS Centers will be responsible for the region-specific page of the CIS Platform, forecasting, agrimeteorological advisories, and early warnings. ## B

### Produce improved CFS
The activity will produce improved CFS at various temporal and spatial scales, including climate change forecasts, seasonal monitoring, annual and GSA advisories and weather products, as a regular operation of PAGASA and DA. The TWGs will ensure the relevance of information produced by this activity to the needs of farmers and other stakeholders in the country. The frequency and format of CFS products and services in demand shall be determined in close consultation with the farmer groups, indigenous peoples, LGU agricultural officers, DA regional officers and extension officers under the technical supervision of TWGs.

### Upgraded the system
The upgraded system will also improve El Niño/La Niña monitoring and forecasting; seasonal climate forecasts (annual to decadal); drought monitoring and forecasting; and long-term agri-climate information beyond the seasonal time scale. As products develop, a number of climate parameters and information products (both in terms of data and services) will be generated. The TWGs will ensure that these products are relevant and their relevance and applications to other systems such as livestock and aquaculture will be fine-tuned and fact in the final design of the system. This will be done through close consultation with farmers, particularly in some provinces; and in others, also of special farm weather outlooks and advisory (for tropical storms) and 10-day farm weather forecasts.

- More seamless agrimete advisories across time scales (daily to seasonal), with an emphasis on daily operational advisories.
- More standardized approaches and capacities to produce advisories at regional level for different agroecological zones.
- Finer spatial resolution and better accuracy of forecasts.
- Periodic review and updating of the advisory services based on weather monitoring and forecasts; and
- Close collaboration with improved forecasting and early warning capabilities of PAGASA: more timely advisories in case of heavy rains and typhoons.

### DA-RFO and LGU staff technology through the Regional TWGs will translate the improved PAGASA weather/climate information into practical farm advisory services, and locally relevant practical farming advisories. These will be delivered to end-users "reaching the last mile". They will be trained to interpret and take agrimete and CFSR advisory products and use them in their daily decision-making processes. This will help improve daily farming decision making and practices (Component 2) based on the received timely advisories.

## Output 1.2: Develop capacity for localized CRA services

### Activity 1.2.1: Prepare Climate Resilient Agricultural strategic plans

#### Sub-activities:

* **1.2.1.1 (PRA) Facilitate participatory CRA strategic planning**
    * **1.2.1.2 (PRA) Support the preparation of CRA Strategic Plans and identification of CRA practices**
    * **1.2.1.3 (PRA) Develop a provincial CRA strategy**

This activity will prepare the CRA strategic action plan for the nine selected provinces, using participatory approaches to ensure inputs from all relevant stakeholders. The provincial CRA strategic plans aim to provide guidance on the sustainability of its efforts.

The process will identify the project's 100 target municipalities, villages, and farmer organizations, ensuring that they are representative of the diversity in terms of climate vulnerability, financial viability, crops, indigenous leaders, women’s organizations, and all other farmers representing climate change-vulnerable groups. That the CRA strategic plans will be developed by LGUs based on the changing environment, lessons learned from project activities as well as other DA programmes.

The CRA strategic plan will be a living document to be adjusted by LGUs based on the changing environment, lessons learned from the project activities as well as other DA programmes. It will inform how best practices and innovative solutions can be scaled up and mainstreamed and will be reviewed before the end of this project to provide guidance on the sustainability of its efforts.

The results of the feasibility study conducted for the project preparation suggest that water and watershed management, including rainwater harvesting, are critical components in ensuring food security and sustainability as a key part of CRA, hydrological, watershed, and water natural resources management actions. These activities will be implemented through the use of appropriate technologies and practices, especially for indigenous peoples. Thus, CRA strategic plans will include scalable packages of interventions such as improved agricultural production systems (e.g., climate-resilient crop varieties, agroforestry, intercropping), community-based seed and nursery systems, micro-irrigation and water harvesting, etc., as part of CRA activities to be implemented by LGUs in collaboration with the project’s target communities.

Household gardens are critical for household food security and serve as an important source of food. Studies have found backyard gardens enabled supported households to better withstand shocks related to the pandemic, such as loss of income, lack of access to markets, and restrictions on movement due to lockdowns, and while families could also not exceed produce to neighbours. Such gardens are also considered an important adaptation strategy for climate change, especially in areas prone to droughts and other climate-related shocks, which could otherwise have particularly strong adverse impacts on food security and nutrition. CRA strategic plans will include the promotion of sustainable agricultural practices and organic practices within household gardens as well as, including into promoting inverse production systems.

## Activity 1.2.2: Develop Climate Resilient Agriculture training and service delivery capacity

### Sub-activities:
1. **1.2.2.1 (PAGASA) Develop and deliver CRA training**
   - **1.2.2.1.1 (PAGASA) Strengthen PAGASA capacities for CRA expertise development**

2. **1.2.2.2 (DFA) External support for CRA capacity development**

3. **1.2.2.3 (DFA) Train CRA Extension Support Specialists**

4. **1.2.2.4 (DFA) Train CRA Enterprise Development Facilitators**

### 98. This Activity will develop the capacity of:
- (i) The Central, DFA-RFO, and LGUs
- (ii) Master Trainers; and
- (iii) CRA Enterprise Development Facilitators to develop and deliver climate information and CRA services according to local needs.

### 99. CRA services and advisory products produced by the TWGs will be translated into extension materials and training modules for use in the field. The TWG outputs will also be disseminated through various platforms for Facilitators to use. Under the supervision of TWGs, the project will work with DA-Agricultural Training Institute (ATI) to develop a curriculum on climate change adaptation and mitigation for the Department of Agriculture Farm Field School (2016) under the Philippine Climate Change Adaptation Project (PhilCCAP). Training content will be developed in consultation with relevant stakeholders including government agencies, organisations, women's groups, indigenous representatives, provincial and municipal agricultural extension officers and other relevant stakeholders. The training curriculum will include lessons on climate change adaptation experiences from the FFS and the CRFs, as well as indigenous peoples' knowledge, gender equality and social inclusion (GEI), and SEARCA mitigation strategies. The training content will be developed in consultation with these groups who will also take into consideration, and there will be flexibility to enable the fit-for-purpose CRA options that each province or municipality may choose.

### 100. Master Trainers, mainly from DA-LGU, established training and research institutions, farmer networks, and other relevant stakeholders, will be trained in climate change adaptation and mitigation. The main master trainers include:
- (i) on the extent feasible, should be based in the target province or region to ensure continuous support for CRA implementation;
- (ii) should have at least 5 years of relevant work experience and at least a Bachelor's degree in their field of expertise.
The master trainers are expected to train and mentor CRA Enterprise Development Facilitators, farmers' groups, women's groups, LGUs, and other relevant municipal institutions/individuals to assess the efficacy of training material and provide coaching and additional training when needed; and
- (iii) should be able to provide technical assistance to provincial agricultural extension officers and CRA enterprise development facilitators on the use of CIR, CSA enterprise management plan, climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies, gender equality and social inclusion (GEI), and project management and access to financial services.

### 101. CRA Enterprise Development Facilitators will be trained by Master Trainers with the help of DA-LGU and external experts. The role of graduated CRA Enterprise Development Facilitators is to train, coach farmers' groups, women's groups, LGUs, and other relevant municipal institutions/individuals on climate change adaptation and mitigation strategies, gender equality and social inclusion (GEI), and project management and link production systems to market and facilitate their implementation. They assist in accessing additional resources and credit facilities for the development of climate-resilient agriculture enterprises. The facilitators will train, coach and monitor other farmers in the municipality or province.

### 102. Candidates would include extension agents from the provincial PAO (DA-LGU) and municipal offices (MAO/MARCO), women's groups, LGUs, and other relevant municipal institutions/individuals from locally active NGOs and young undergraduates with an interest in agriculture. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 14

## Component 2: Climate Resilient Agriculture Adoption through Enterprise Development

### Activities under this Component will support climate change-vulnerable communities in improving their adaptive capacity and resilience to the impacts of climate change by increasing access to climate-resilient crop generation capacities.

#### This Component will improve farmers' access to climate information, CSA services, and finance, thereby enabling them to adopt climate-resilient technologies in a wide range of agro-climatic zones. The project will also support the development of local value chains through linking CSA service providers with quality assurance institutions in order to reduce risks and losses, promote enough volume, attain economies of scale, ensure quality and establish a niche market.

#### With a special focus on women, indigenous communities, and youth, activities under this Component will strive to overcome input and market barriers to CSA adoption with complementary funding by CCAFS with value added services. The project will support the development of climate-resilient crop technologies in providing information and linking CSA-vulnerable farmers to CSA and CSA advisory and service enterprises with local financial institutions such as Land Bank (the DAE), microfinance institutions including credit and micro-insurance, and other financial service providers. The project will also provide technical assistance and capacity building for these financial institutions with institutional assistance and enhanced capacity to better understand and use CSA services and develop appropriate products and services that are tailored to the needs of smallholder farmers and local IPs.

#### The project will work through apex organizations such as the MOPC, CDA, PCIC, etc. for CSA utilization and facilitation in the provinces where the project is implemented.

### Building support technical and financial services will support farmers and other actors into developing sustainable CRA value chains that will transform agri-food systems. See Figure B.3.2 below.

The training programmes and packages developed under Activity 1.2.2 will be used: Master Trainers and CRA Entrepreneurs will be trained in the use of these tools and technologies.
