### 1.4.The following process and capacity-building substance for the development of CRA Enterprises are envisaged:

- Establishment of baseline information on beneficiary socio-economic status and agricultural assets, as well as their level of awareness about climate change and adaptation practices, which will be collected at household level. This will also support beneficiary selection based on the established criteria.
- Identification of crops and other agriculture-based livelihoods with market and income potential. This is important for CRA enterprises to identify the most suitable crops or farming systems.
- The CRA Enterprises group elect their leader and treasurer. Each group opens a bank account with their leader as the account holder. The CRA Entrepreneurs will be trained in how to open these bank accounts so they can leverage access to financial services, in particular group loans and insurance.
- Capacity building of CRA Entrepreneurs on: (i) market linkages; (ii) value chain development; (iii) CRA practices with market prospect; (iv) developing value chains (production, processing, and marketing); (v) business planning and management; (vi) financial literacy; (vii) gender equality and social inclusion; and (viii) coordination; (ix) facilitation of access to financial resources from rural financial service providers including microfinance institutions; (x) risk management; (xi) climate change adaptation practices, gender equality and social inclusion; and (xii) SEAH prevention and risk mitigation.
- Vulnerability assessment of the CRA Entrepreneurs’ groups in order to support the adoption of CRA practices and the necessary farming system diversification.

1.15.The envisaged training provided to project beneficiaries will be demand-driven and market-driven, i.e., based on the needs and priorities of farmers. The Master Trainers will identify what particular crops or farming systems would be both profitable and suitable with climate change. Following the formation of the CRA enterprises, with technical support from the Master Trainers, each group will identify their training needs and priorities. The training content will be on identification of appropriate technologies and practices for the identified crops or farming systems; market linkages to markets for inputs, outputs, group management, gender equality and social inclusion, and SEAH prevention and risk mitigation. The training will also include field visits, demonstration plots, and practical sessions with priority topics, and prioritized knowledge. Farmers’ learning will also include field trials, demonstration plots, and post-harvest handling of the crops or farming systems they have adopted to support each group with needed agriculture inputs and equipment for CRA field learning and demonstration.

1.16.This will provide farmers with peer-to-peer learning and a testing ground so that they can use agricultural technologies and practices in their own farms. This will also help them to identify climate-resilient and economically viable crops. It will also raise farmers’ needs for additional information and delivery mechanisms, such as mobile phones (e.g., the M-Pesa service in Kenya), radio, television, and SMS (e.g., Box 83.5), market, and finance. The CRA enterprise development learning will already continue to reduce crop failure risk through the use of climate-resilient crops and practices. However, the inclusion of specific modules in the training that increase financial and literacy, such modules would cover cost-benefit analysis, budgeting, record keeping, and marketing. This will help farmers to better understand their vicinity and the products they offer.

Appendix 1.2.2 lists the totally selected CRA practices and technologies, which will be licitabale and prioritized based on CRA as mentioned above. This analysis of CRA technologies used the Compendium of Climate-smart Agriculture Technologies & Approaches in Indonesia (Lubis et al., 2019), which summarizes data on AMA’s CRA practices and technologies. The list is not exhaustive, but all the technologies identified at the start of the project; the applicability of the practices below will be reviewed or alternative CRA practices and technologies may be considered.

# Box B3.4: Local seed systems for CRA enterprise development

## Background

Seeds are one of the bulk of cash incomes used by both CRA and traditional users. In potato production, Wang (in d) noted that the use of certified good-quality seed varieties can increase yields by 20–50%, as compared to traditional varieties or even hybrid varieties. However, in many cases, farmers have no access to improved seed varieties because of high costs, lack of information, crop rotations and intercropping. Access to improved seed varieties remains a critical challenge for the great majority of smallholder farmers in Africa. The limited availability of good-quality seed varieties is one of the major constraints that smallholders highlight challenging. Furthermore, climate resilient seed varieties and the associated farming systems have not been widely adopted by smallholder farmers.

The project addresses the mentioned impediments through the following interventions:

1. In collaboration with the noted senior seed grower association, the CRA enterprise development approach will include the use of local seed systems to establish CRA enterprises in the country. The project will work closely with the seed growers’
   . The training will include explanations and demonstrations of varietal characteristics in relation to forecasted climate change scenarios. This will be followed by a field visit to the seed grower association’s farm where the performance data of these varieties under specific field conditions and agro-ecological conditions. These will be analyzed by the farmers and the project team to determine the best performing varieties for each CRA enterprise in relation to
   . The training will also include demonstrations on how to produce good-quality seed using locally available resources.

2. To ensure that local seed systems foster CRA enterprises (number of 100), the project will develop the required economies of scale to enable certified seed production among smallholders. The project intends to establish CRA enterprises in the country with a focus on climate resilient seed varieties and other high-yielding varieties for use by smallholder farmers
   . The project will also provide training on how to produce good-quality seed using locally available resources, including the use of local seed systems. This will be followed by a field visit to the seed grower association’s farm where the performance data of these varieties under specific field conditions and agro-ecological conditions.
3. The project intends to build on experiences from past and ongoing country seed system projects in the country

### Digital Agriculture as part of DA approach

The project will use digital agricultural data that are collected and archived near real-time from stations, then shared for analysis through its CIST platform. Remote sensing (e.g., in meteorology, farm weather, climate change) will be used to develop early warning systems and crop monitoring models to tease meteorological forecasts and compile aggregated information and climate-related risks to agriculture.

Aggregated information and advisories are communicated in various ways adapted to the needs of diverse users in the food value chain. The project will use a variety of communication channels, including social media, mobile apps, and videos, with the aim that the relevant data ultimately reach farmers for timely on-farm decision-making.

### GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 41

#### Preparatory of Investment Plan for Special Groups

**13a. The project will support the targeted poor farmers and beneficiaries of existing or emerging social protection programmes in leveraging safety nets and the project’s technical support in order to invest in, develop, and implement climate-resilient agricultural enterprises (CRA) that are sustainable, productive, responsive social protection and the pilot test of the graduation approach by the Asian Development Bank (ADB) have demonstrated that CRA investments can provide a pathway out of poverty for poor farmers by increasing their skills and capacities and build resilient livelihoods. Examples of such social protection programmes are provided below:**

- **The Climate Resilience Programme through Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation and Disaster Risk Reduction:** The program is supported through the CFW, which is a short-term intervention that empowers beneficiaries to build their communities. Through the CFW, farmers will be able to access climate information and early warning systems, training on climate-resilient crop planting, communal gardening, and other similar activities. At present, the program is being implemented in 12 regions.

- **The Kabahayang Kusog ng Kababaihang Katutubong (KKK) Project:** This project aims to provide assistance to poor farmers through a multi-sectoral approach that includes social protection services through targeted Community Needs Assessments (CNAs) and Special Area for Agricultural Development (SADs). The program will also support the development of climate-resilient agricultural enterprises, especially female-headed households and upland IPs in 30 of the poorest provinces of the country. The project will support the implementation of the Social Protection and Livelihood Enhancement Program (SPEP) and the Agri-Disaster Preparedness and Response Plan (ADDPORP).

**Box B.3.7 Leveraging social protection to promote poor and vulnerable households’ CRA adoption and strengthening livelihoods**

Since 2017, FAO, together with other UN agencies, has been supporting the development of adaptive shock-response mechanisms for smallholder farmers in the Philippines through the Social Safety Net Program (SSNP) under the National DRM Council. Frameworks to scale up major social protection schemes, such as the Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program (4Ps), have been developed.

Between 2018 and 2020, the DSWD, with support from ADB, also piloted the “graduation approach,” providing 1,500 beneficiaries of the 4P with productive assets and training through its Kabahayang Kusog (wellbeing) program. This aimed to provide the participants with a pathway out of poverty by building their capacity in agriculture and other livelihoods. The interventions included: (i) a one-time asset transfer, such as livestock, seeds, tools, and equipment; (ii) training on climate-resilient crop planting techniques, coaching and life skills training covering social and health issues; (iii) co-training in business and financial management; (iv) access to credit facilities for the purchase of inputs and other needs; and (v) a conditional cash transfer. The interventions were layered up by the government’s 4P cash transfer program benefits: paid every 2 weeks based on meeting health and school attendance requirements, and provided during the COVID-19 pandemic. During the COVID-19 pandemic, the pilot achieved an average graduation rate of 71%. The approach strengthened household resilience to shocks and adverse events by providing a pathway out of poverty through improved livelihoods and mental health. Beneficiaries of the pilot fared significantly better than those who received only the government cash transfers.

## B.3.8 – Funding CRA Enterprise Investment Plans

The expected budget for on-farm CRA implementation per farmer will be around USD 375. This budget is based on the enterprise-level investment plan.

This budget will be used to provide 6,000 farming households with a comprehensive CRA support package, covering inputs, equipment, service, and capacity building, on a cost-sharing arrangement. The DA co-financing, the farmer contribution (at least USD 125 per farm), and other financial sources (e.g., LGUs) will be used to cover the balance of costs.

Diversify on FAD crops and improve access to agricultural livelihoods; the project investment will also include: commodity beneficial, community-based small natural resources management assets that will reduce climate disaster (such as flood and drought); and other climate-smart agriculture practices (e.g., water harvesting, community-based watershed management) in support of the climate-resilient agenda. Each CRA enterprise, when developing their investment plans, shall identify the specific needs for women-headed households and IPs ensuring gender- and indigenous-appropriate CRA investment plans.

GCF finance will provide services and catalyze new technologies and practices, especially those with potential to produce climate change mitigation and adaptation benefits. The project will also include a capacity building component that will provide the necessary inputs (seeds, fertilizer, and equipment), which will be created toward supporting CRA. The partnership between GCF and DA will ensure that the financial resources provided by GCF are channeled directly into Government programmes, thus ensuring GCF’s continuous support of farmers in the transition from conventional to climate-resilient agriculture.

The CRA enterprises will be supported to mobilize resources from DA regular budget, rural development and natural resources management programme. LGUs as well as private sectors for implementation of the identified community-based NRM assets.

This project will also address the specific needs of women-headed households and IPs ensuring there are gender- and indigenous-appropriate considerations in CRA investment plans.

### Investment from the Project for CRA addition will be provided to CRA Enterprises/AMAs Villages when they have:

1. Submitted a flexible business investment plan;
2. Demonstrated potential in establishing partnerships with value chains; and
3. Project technical staff (FAO and DA) will review the investment plans and make investment decisions.

The project will support CRA Enterprises/AMAs Villages in tapping into other resources (e.g., LGU funding, other programmes in the area) and improving access to credit. This process will also address the specific needs of women-headed households and IPs ensuring there are gender- and indigenous-appropriate considerations in CRA investment plans.

Based on CRA Enterprise Investment Plans, DA and FAO will work closely with CRA Enterprises/AMAs Villages to identify the specific needs for women-headed households and IPs ensuring there are gender- and indigenous-appropriate considerations in CRA investment plans. The project will also provide a mix of sources of finances as per the above-mentioned financial model.

The CRA investment items to be supported by DA co-financing (total budget of USD 4,625 million), as agreed with GCF, will be procured and provided by both FAO and DA/DA-GIs at the provincial level. They will be transitioned as a part of DA’s budget items, primarily under its Banner programmes for non-crop enterprises. The remaining CRA investment items (total budget of USD 6 million) will be procured and provided by both FAO and DA.

FAO technical standards (for seeds, fertilizer, and other equipment), as well as tools, e.g., IPM, water harvesting, community-based watershed management) will be applied, in harmonization with Government/DA

## Facilitating access to financial services

### Conducting financial literacy training among target farmers: preparing them for each of them to open a bank account, and promote the use of microfinance institutions (MFIs) and other financial service providers (FSPs) such as credit unions, cooperatives, etc. through programmes under the ACPC and others that provide relevant financial products for either working capital or investment purposes.

### Marketing: Organization of agri fairs & expos

#### Group marketing
- **Marketing costs**

### CREA enterprises development

142. During the design and investment planning process of CREA enterprise development, the project will conduct financial literacy training among target farmers; prepare each of them to open a bank account, and promote the use of microfinance institutions (MFIs) and other financial service providers (FSPs) such as credit unions, cooperatives, etc. through programmes under the ACPC and others that provide relevant financial products for either working capital or investment purposes.

143. The project is also expected to work with MFIs, FSPs, and other financial institutions to identify and mobilize public and private sector funds so that Enterprises (AMA Villages may implement CREA investment plans, especially critical ecosystem-based Rural Roads, Water Supply and Sanitation, etc.) will be able to access these funds. The project will also ensure that information is properly monitored; information will be stored in the CRA M&E system (developed under Activity 3.1.3) and documented and published on the project website and other public domain.

144. As part of the project Monitoring, Evaluation, Accountability and Learning (MEAL) systems will be developed to facilitate FOAMMA Villages' monitoring and reporting on mobilization of finance, implementation of the CRA M&E system, and information sharing among stakeholders. The MEAL system will also monitor climate change mitigation and contribution to the enabling environment. FAO tools such as Resilience Index Measurement Assessment (RIMA) and other relevant tools will be used in developing a Climate Change Mitigation and Adaptation Based Solutions in agricultural landscapes, and digital Knowledge Management System, will be used in developing the MEAL system.

145. The project will help CRA Enterprises/AMA Villages keep the CREA enterprises active under the DA's banner programmes and LGUs' plans. This will in effect accelerate the shift from the existing conventional seed and fertilizer distribution systems to a more sustainable and climate-resilient agricultural production model, while continuing providing these support services beyond project closure, and in other climate impact provinces and regions.

### CREA peer learning and mainstreaming

146. CRA mainstreaming has been considered a necessity for CRA adoption of scale in order to cope with the projected changes in climate and land use. The project will implement a combination of Ongoing Support (OS) approach throughout the country: CRA awareness-raising, raising the capacity of CREA facilitators for OS approaches through the country; Participatory learning and experience-sharing among existing and new CREA extension workers across and beyond provinces; provision of technical assistance to those involved in agricultural farming systems development and management. The project will also provide a comprehensive package of support from CRA enterprises development and aims to play a key role in said knowledge- and experience-sharing. This package includes: (i) capacity building for facilitators, (ii) training on climate change adaptation and mitigation; and thematic workshops with relevant government agencies (DA, DAR, NGOs, etc.) and the private sector will be facilitated by the project. The project will also provide a comprehensive package of system support that can holistically address the building blocks of agri-food systems – including land use planning, soil health management, water resource management, crop production, livestock production, processing, marketing, nutrition and food safety, food waste and loss, agro-ecological practices, and others. The project will also provide a comprehensive package of support to CREA enterprises in other countries as a package geared toward adaptation in different socio-ecological contexts. In order to facilitate an agri-food system transformation toward inclusiveness and climate resilience.

### Component 2: Mainstreaming Climate Resilient Agriculture

149. This Component will also address the need for sustainability and scaling up of CRA. While Component 2 will directly target farmers within 100 climate-vulnerable municipalities, this Component looks at project impacts on a scale beyond that of individual farmers. It will mainstream CRA into national and local government policies and approaches into Govt/PFs agricultural and rural development programmes, at the same time raising awareness of both public and private sector actors about the benefits of CRA and how to implement it in their own development practices. The project will help: private sectors; apply and use the CIS and CRA services developed under Component 1.

150. This component will also raise awareness, mainstream localisation CIS and CRA services, and enhance the extension capacities of non-target LGUs, DAs, and other local actors through working with other national programmes and projects that are already implementing climate-resilient agricultural practices (e.g., PAGASA's capacity building systems [PABS] that are being piloted, supported by Provincial CIS Centers, which provide non-project areas extension services to farmers in the project area). This will be done through:

Output 3.1: CRA mainstreamed into national and LGU programs

151. The success of mainstreaming CRA will depend on the institutionalization of CIS and CRA services in non-project target areas. This will be pursued by making CIS and CRA services available at the national level with under-utilized resources, such as those from other government agencies and regional development authorities.

Activity 3.1: Increase farmers' awareness and understanding of Climate Resilient Agriculture

Sub-activities:

### Activity 3.1.1 (D): Implement CRA awareness raising campaigns and peer learning

#### 3.1.1.1 (D) CRA Awareness Campaigns

#### 3.1.1.2 (D) Support CRA strategic communication

#### 3.1.1.3 (D) CRA communication by DA

152. This activity will develop EIC materials that present CIS and CRA information generated at national and regional levels, as well as from the learning farming communities. The activity will support the development of printed media, regular radio and TV programmes, mobile phone apps (e.g., for weather and agronomic information), and other communication tools to disseminate information on climate-resilient agricultural practices directly to reach many, especially smallholder farmers. This will include developing models to make these materials easily accessible to farmers, including the appropriateness of the material, and encouraging leadership and interest from the community. The activity will also develop a mobile app that can be used by farmers to access information on how to use smartphones to create video, which in turn can be easily shared through social media. Non-indigenous and indigenous languages will be used for CRA awareness raising campaigns, in addition to quantifying demonstration effects and other CRA-related enabling environment services.

153. The use of community videos is a fairly new methodology, successfully applied in India (e.g., dGreen Digital) and other countries. This project will develop a similar approach by creating a video library that includes an expanding repository of community video-clips on CRA practices in local languages. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 47

## Activity 3.1.2: Mainstream Climate Resilient Agriculture practices and enterprise development into national and LGU programmes

### Sub-activities:
- **3.1.2.1 (TA) Training and facilitation for CRA mainstreaming**
- **3.1.2.2 (PAC) Policy advisors and technical support for CRA mainstreaming**

This activity aims to scale up CRA adoption and enterprise development beyond the project target areas through (i) strengthening capacity of DA-RDFs, LGUs, and other stakeholders in climate change adaptation and rural development programmes; and (ii) developing capacities of DA-RDFs, LGU staff members and of stakeholders for mainstreaming CRA into national and local policies.

### Mainstreaming:
Mainstreaming shall be done mainly through the following:

- Knowledge sharing with and training of DA-RDFs and LGU HAT on the integration of CRA enterprise development in the Philippine Climate Change Action Plan (PCAP).
- Training of LGU staff on the use of CRA results in updating or enhancing Local Climate Change Action Plans (LCCAP) and other relevant local policies.
- Development of models for CRA enterprise development with different partners (LGU, CDO, SUC, or private sector).
- Continuing provision of support services by the DA to identify or priority climate-resilient value chains.

The project will facilitate knowledge-sharing in non-target areas (regions, provinces, and municipalities). A key element of mainstreaming CRA enterprise development is convincing policy makers and practitioners by showing them how to integrate CRA into their existing policies and programmes. This will be done through training and -documented workshops and exposure visits to successful CRA enterprises developed by the project. The CGI Philippines will also provide technical assistance in developing LCCAPs for non-target areas of the population.

The project will also maximize the use of DA and PAGASA’s events to train non-target DA-RDFs and LGUs on strategically using CGI and CRA services to plan and support CRA practices and enterprise development.

**Page 52**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 48

161. With the new fiscal developments subsequent to the “Manilara ruling” it is likely that LGUs will have increased flexibility in their revenue and budget for development. Mainstreaming of CCA into planning and budget allocation by Provincial and Municipal LGUs, including non-target LGUs as part of the project’s implementation will be a key objective. The project will also facilitate harmonization of objectives and activities related to climate change adaptation to promote resilience-focused interventions amongst different programmes. This will be pursued by linking existing projects with CCA components and through the development of new CCA-specific projects and G&R programmes, through collaboration between the respective agency and LGU Planning coordinated programme implementation. The project will also support the mainstreaming of CCA into planning for the important target group in many project target areas. DA offers farmers a number of potentially complementary interventions to address climate change impacts on agriculture. In order to ensure that recently funded DA projects financed by the World Bank and IDA (especially for integrating CCA with agriculture cooperation) are not duplicated, the project will work closely with existing programs, identify partner proposals with high relevance for mainstreaming CCA, by means of:

* **Identify potential coordinators:**
    * Training on how to use the participatory CCAA;
    * Development of a database of best practices of CAA options; and
    * Conduct of techno-demo or community participatory action research (CPAR).

## Activity 2.1: Facilitate information sharing and dialogue among CNA value chain actors
### Sub-activity 3.2.1 (PAS) FACILITATION OF INFORMATION SHARING AND DIALOGUE AMONG CNA VALUE CHAIN ACTORS
#### 3.2.1.1 (PAS) Information-sharing and dialogue among CNA value chain actors

**Outcome:** The project will include baselines, mid-term, and end-line surveys of communities and farmers' situations in terms of resilience, food security, and other key project outcomes, following impact evaluation methodology to be validated by the CGIAR. The information collected from these surveys will be monitored for efficiency and ownership. Progress on local planning and budget commitments, project implementation, and reporting will be monitored through regular meetings with CCAFS, NEDA, and DEDA, documenting the project's contribution to water national programs, policies, and aims.

**Output 3.2: Enabling financial mechanisms and value-chains for sustainable CCA adoption**

### Activity 3.2.1: Facilitate inclusive Climate Resilient Agriculture value-chain development

#### Sub-activity 3.2.1 (PAS) Information-sharing and dialogue among CNA value chain actors
##### 3.2.1.2 (PAS) FAC(IAL) Support for dialogue and PPP establishment

**Under this activity, the project will map key value-chain actors and service providers (at municipality and provincial levels) using the prioritized CNA co-facilitation systems. This is expected to identify the capacities and engagement of key private sector actors in terms of their ability to provide services such as extension, pricing structures and dynamics as well as processes and requirements to access the products and services offered by the public sector. The project will also map the demand for services from both the public sector and directly feed into the financial inclusion activities for CCA enterprises development under Output 2.1.**

**The projects intend to bridge the knowledge and information gap between farmers and the private sector value chain actors, including: (i) relevant climate change information and data; (ii) relevant demand-side systems, CIS, CRA practices, farmer/CRA enterprise demand for services and demand cycles; (iii) relevant agricultural value chain service providers, the type and modality of services provided, price dynamics and processes, and requirements to access these services; (iv) relevant climate change information and data forecasts on climate-related events and parameters, which can inform the development of products and services that will be offered by private sector actors. The project will also map the demand for services from both the public sector and directly feed into the financial inclusion activities for CCA enterprises development under Output 2.1.**

**The analysis of the information and demand provided for supply and service among farmers and the private sector, the key subjects for engagement with the private sectors and other value chain actors will be CC impacts on farm productivity, climate change adaptation strategies, and the role of public and private sector actors in interpretation of climate information and agent data for business continuity planning, resident investment decisions, and risk management. The project will also map the demand for services from both the public sector and private companies and value-chain actors will be able to analyze climate information and use CIS and CRA advisories as well as other relevant information to make informed decisions on their investments in agriculture. The project will focus on farmers' evolving adaptation needs, while developing partnerships with farmer organizations/MAV Villages and relevant stakeholders to provide support to CC activities.**

**The analysis of the information and demand provided for supply and service among farmers and the private sector suppliers of medium-to-long term climate change trends and forecasts, as well as future needs for stress-tolerant seed varieties, improved agricultural practices, and other technologies and inputs will be used to inform the development and supply of CRA technologies and inputs. Collaboration with agricultural input and machinery suppliers will also be established to ensure that they are aware of the demand for CC-related products and services. CC risks by businesses, making them more climate-resilient, in line with the project-promoted CRA practices. Similarly, collaboration with agricultural input and machinery suppliers will be established to ensure that they supply technologies, and machinery. | LANDBANK Climate Resilient Agriculture Financing Program | * Conduct a feasibility study on the Climate Finance Programme in the project target areas and identify localized barriers to climate-resilient agriculture financing.\

* Engage Land Bank local offices and LGUs in the CRA enterprise investment plans (Activity 2.1.3) by developing viable financing models and securing the necessary approvals from the Department of Agriculture, the DA or MAs.\

## Objective: Provides financing for CRA-CCCA initiatives towards climate-resilient agriculture

### England: Climate Change Adaptation Finance Programme that utilizes climate-resistant technologies (e.g. seedling nurseries, irrigation systems, and water harvesting) endorsed by the Department of Agriculture and the locality's Municipalities/Local Government Units (LGUs). This includes rainwater harvesting, terracing and system of rice intensification.

### Conditions of facilities (reservoirs, controlled irrigation system for greenhouses, hydroponics, aquaponics), farm-to-market roads, etc.

### Resilience projects such as crop-based farming systems that diversify income sources, agroforestry, integrated pest management system, growing vegetables in floating gardens, and perennial crops. This includes the use of climate-resilient varieties, harvest losses during typhoons or periods of rain (e.g. mini rice cultivation), and other climate-resilient practices.

### Financing: 80% of Land Bank and 20% Borrower with a repayment term from 1 to 3 years depending on the project.

## Objective: Support to the national government's Food Staples Sufficiency Program

### Scope: Provides credit assistance for paddy farming for small farmers who are not members of Land Bank's accredited cooperative societies or other financial institutions.

The successful applicants have reached P11.82 billion with outstanding balance of P7.23 billion involving a total of 17,949 small farmers and cooperatives.

## LANDBANK Sikat-saka Program

Based on the CRA Strategic Plan and CRA enterprise investment plans (Activity 2.1.3), identify specific social groups, i.e., as a finance source complementing other financial institutions.
Develop a proposal to implement a graduation approach (see Preparing for Graduation: A Practical Guide, World Bank, 2015)

The project will explore ASENSO to support agri-MiME development and implementation of the CRA enterprise investment plans.

## Insurance Products

### Objectives
- Provide 0% interest loans to finance capital requirements of agri-fishery-based micro and small enterprises.

### Eligibility
- Micro-enterprises can avail of loans up to P1,300,000 for income-generating activities such as production, processing, or marketing of agricultural products, or production and sale of agri-fishery income-generating activity and non-farm activities.

### SURE (Agricultural Climate Risk Insurance)
- Provides immediate assistance by natural and other calamities, animal disease outbreaks, and other disastrous events to help them regain their capacity to earn a living.
  - **ACPC Survival and Recovery Loan Assistance (SURE) Program**
    - Supports APCIC to explore the use of SURE for providing insurance coverage in selected LGUs and projects area utilizing CGI and CIE systems.
    - Provides immediate assistance by natural and other calamities, animal disease outbreaks, and other disastrous events.

### Information Sharing and Collaboration
- Facilitates better understanding of risks, availability of better climate and agronomic data, and how these can be used to speed up insurance underwriting. Over the last few years, FAD has been leading Anticipatory Action (AA) in the Philippines and has been working with APCIC to develop a framework for AA.
- Early warning systems are critical tools that can help farmers prepare for disasters by providing early warnings of potential hazards such as droughts, floods, typhoons, etc. These systems can also be used to identify triggers for early action, before an anticipated disaster (drought, flood, typhoon, etc.). For example, in 2019 AA such as the provision of ducks and goats to women's cooperatives – to produce eggs, milk, and meat; and to provide seeds and tools to farmers to grow vegetables at home – to boost nutrition; and cash-for-work for cleaning local water canals had high return on investment.
- The Philippines has been leading Anticipatory Action (AA) in the region since 2015. This document provides a list of Early Warning Early Action Records.

### Insurance Products
- Information sharing and collaboration with APCIC and micro insurance companies will help them better understand climate risks, availability of better climatic data, and how these can be used to speed up underwriting.
- Over the last few years, FAD has been leading Anticipatory Action (AA) in the Philippines and has been working with APCIC to develop a framework for AA.

### Early Warning Systems
- These advancements provide opportunities to further develop index-based insurance which has been implemented by LGUs and cooperatives. This type of insurance is based on pre-defined triggers that are set at the level organizations (LGUs, MFIs, and cooperatives) from damage from extreme weather events that would halt their operations.
- In such a scenario, index-triggered payments can be made to an LCU or cooperative according to their agreed terms. This type of insurance is particularly useful for smallholder farmers who lack access to credit and have limited financial literacy but are able to participate in crop insurance (for the entire municipality in Balaoan, La Union).
- The project will help its 45,000 target beneficiary farmers through application or enrollment, availability of insurance products such as the commodity insurance packages from APCIC as applicable to their farm enterprises, and by providing training on how to access these services at the micro level.

## B.A. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

### Accredited Entity

#### 176. FAO will serve as both the Accredited Entity (AE) and an Executing Entity (EE) for this project. FAO will be the AE for all aspects of the project, including the overall management of the project, including: (i) all aspects of project appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial, and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation; (iii) environmental monitoring and reporting during project implementation; (iv) quality assurance and quality control monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting to the GCF; and (v) project closure and evaluation. FAO will also serve as an EE for this project in accordance with the terms and conditions set out in the Memorandum of Agreement (MOA) between FAO and GCF.

#### 176. FAO role as AE will be delegated to the relevant offices and divisions in FAO located/operated within the Rome, Bangkok, and Phnom Penh Sub-regional Offices and the Pacific FAO Office located in Bangkok, Thailand; and our Country Representation Office for Project Country (FAO-PLOCs). In order to fulfill its AE functions, FAO will serve as an Executing Entity (EE) for this project. The EE will be responsible for: (i) overall management of the project; (ii) oversight and supervision of the Budget Officer (BO), Lead Technical Officer (LTO), Funding Liaison Officer (FLO), HDQ Technical Officer and other relevant staff in implementing the project; (iii) ensuring that all project activities are carried out according to the project and assist project to ensure fulfillment of the AE function on the ground.

#### 176. A Lead Technical Officer (LTO) will be appointed in the regional office and will be responsible for the technical supervision, monitoring, and reporting of the project implementation. More specifically, the LTO will have overall technical responsibility for the implementation of the project. The role of the LTO is central to FAO’s ability to effectively perform its AE functions as well as to ensure that all project activities are carried out according to the project and assist project to ensure fulfillment of the AE function on the ground (EE). The LTO will oversee and carry out technical backstopping for the project implementation. This LTO will be supported by a team of FAO staff, including: (i) Budget Officer; (ii) Funding Liaison Officer; (iii) Lead Technical Officer; (iv) HDQ Technical Officer; (v) Project Coordinator; and (vi) other relevant staff as required to support the project. The LTO will be responsible for the Annual Work Plan and Budget (AWPB), including work plan and budget revisions. The LTO is responsible for ensuring that all project activities are carried out according to the project, and assisting in the preparation of the EE’s. In addition, the LTO will provide technical supervision (may also call on other experts to participate and advise) during the implementation phase of the project and assist in the project evaluation. The LTO is responsible for quality technical outputs. The LTO will coordinate the provision of appropriate technical support from the PTF to respond to specific Project Country Development Priorities.

#### 176. The PTF will remain independent from the Executing Entity functions also performed by FAO (see Project execution section below). In line with the GCF policy on fees adopted through GCF Board Decision B/03/09, alternative arrangements may be made for the provision of technical assistance to the project. These arrangements may and effectively perform AE functions listed in the “GCF General Principles and indicative list of eligible costs concerning the provision of technical assistance” (see page 157).

### Project Co-financing and Parallel Finance

#### 180. The Government of the Philippines acting through DA and PAGASA will provide co-financing in the form of in-kind contributions, including: (1) US$23 million for the provision of inputs required to implement the project; and reported during project implementation:
- These contributions are made up of (i) in-kind contribution of staff time, office and travel expenses, directly attributable to project execution and management; and (ii) US $ 22.68 million for the provision of inputs required to implement the project.
- The in-kind contribution will be used by DA to support the project’s implementation during its development/implementation year and to implement the CRA investment plans as per the financial model. Co-financing will be executed by DA as monitored by FAO in its AE capacity. In kind co-financing is measured against the total amount of in-kind contributions, including office and travel expenses, directly attributable expenditures. In addition, DA will provide parallel finance estimated at US$ 10.625 million as mentioned in paragraph 177.

#### B

The co-financiers are also the Executing Entities for their respective co-financing: Therefore, the Government of the Philippines acting through the Philippine Department of Agriculture (DA) and the Philippine Atmospheric Geophysical and Astronomical Services Administration (PAGASA) and the Philippine Bureau of the Treasury (BTr). DA is a government agency responsible for the administration of agriculture in the country. PAGASA provides meteorological, hydrological, and support services needed for domestic and export-oriented business enterprises. PAGASA was established on December 19, 2008 to provide weather forecasts, climate change information, disaster risk reduction, and support services needed for the well-being, economic security of all the people, as well as to promote national progress by undertaking scientific research in meteorology, hydrology, geophysics, astronomy, atmospheric sciences, oceanography, and space sciences. PAGASA serves as one of the Scientific and Technological Services Institutes of the Department of Science and Technology (DOST). The Bureau of Treasury is a government agency responsible for managing the principal custodian of all national government funds, manage the cash resources of the Government, perform banking functions in relation to receipts and disbursements of national funds, and maintain accounts of the financial transactions of the Government. All transactions involving the use of public funds require necessary approvals, authorizations, licenses or warrants are required in order for the Accredited Entity to receive the relevant instructions from the Bureau of Treasury. The receipt of all transactional documents and the transmission of disbursement instructions from the Accredited Entity are duly received by the Government of the Host Country. Additional information on the Bureau of Treasury can be found at <http://www.treasury.gov.ph>.

As an EE, FAO, though its Representation in Philippines (Phil-FAH), will deliver technical assistance and capacity-building to complement the activities of DA and PAGASA, drawing on FAO's comparative advantage and in-house expertise. The project will also provide support services for the implementation of the project by the government and stakeholders to sustain the process of CRA transformation introduced by the project.

## Project Governance Structure

187. As a mechanism for joint governance with the PILAR, a Programme Steering Committee (PSC) will be established. The PSC will provide overall guidance and strategic orientation to the project, ensuring it remains closely aligned with the GCF’s objectives of promoting climate resilience, enhancing development effectiveness and sustainability – and ensuring the synergy of PILAR and other sister projects under the programme. The PSC will be composed of representatives from the following organizations: the GCF Secretariat (GCF), the World Bank (WB), DOF, NDAF, FAD (as EE), and the EEs of the PILAR project. Taking into consideration the distinct implementation arrangements for each sister project under the programme, the PSC will be co-chaired by the WB and GCF Secretariat. The PSC will participate in an advisory observer (non-voting) capacity. For this project, the following will be invited: selected representatives from the Ministry of Finance (MOF), the National Development Authority (NDA), the National Regional Coordination Committees, representatives from other selected government agencies (NCIP, NIA, DARI), and representatives from the private sector.

188. The PSC will have the main functions of:
- ensuring political coordination, guidance, and advocacy;
- and reviewing and approving the Annual Work Plan and Budget (AWPB) and Annual Report (AR). The PSC will be subject to the GCF’s approval AWPB and Annual Performance Report (APR) for official submission to the GCC and to co-financers.

189. The PSC will meet at least two times per year or more frequently as required by the majority of its members. The PSC’s meetings may also be convened in case of any urgent or important matters. The co-chairs will have the authority to invite other experts as needed. Minutes of PSC meeting will be made publicly available and circulated to all members.

190. The GCF Secretariat will serve as the secretariat of the PSC, providing overall technical and administrative assistance to the PSC and facilitating its meetings.

### Sub-national/Regional Level Governance Structure

198. The Regional Coordination Committees (RCCs) will be convened in each of the five administrative regions to:

   * b) monitor project implementation at the regional level and provide feedback on progress to the Project Office.
   * c) serve as an advisory body for the Regional Project Officer (RPO).
   * d) be chaired by the RPO, who will be supported by a team of officers designated by the heads of LGUs (i.e., governors and mayors), and representatives from beneficiaries or elected members in their respective local contexts.
199. The RCCs shall meet at least once every quarter or when requested by the majority of its members. The secretariat of the RCC is the Regional Project Office (RPO).

### Project Management Office and Technical Assistance Team

The DA will host the Project Management Office (PMO), which will be physically located at the central DA office in Quezon City. The PMO shall manage all project activities for which the DA is the EE.

The PMO will consist of a range of operational and technical staff including:
* **Project Manager:** A senior official who is Climate Change Adaptation Specialist. She will be the head of the PMO and will be responsible for day-to-day project operations and coordination with other government agencies.
* **Administrative Officer:** National Administrative Officer, National Finance Officer, IT Assistant
* **Technical Officers:**
    * National Technical Operations Officer (project-reqired, full-time) – assists the National Project Coordinator in all operational arrangements and supervision of progress in the project region
    * National Climate Change Adaptation Specialist (project-reqired, full-time)
    * Climate Resilient Agriculture Office (CRAO)
    * Field Monitoring Team (FMT)
    * Project Monitoring Service (PMS)
    * Agricultural Extension Officer (AEO)
* **Subject matter specialists (local recruit)**
    * Agricultural Extension Officer
    * Administrative Assistant

103. The PMO will report to FAO-PH and the Field Operation Service (FOS) – Field Programs Operational Planning Division (FOPD). It will also coordinate closely with the DA Climate Resilient Agriculture Office (CRAO), which is responsible for the implementation of the project activities in the field. This coordination will be a between-and among central units: field offices, attachages, bureaus, corporations), providing guidance in departmental operations and ensuring that all project activities are properly implemented.

104. PAGASA will establish its own Project Execution Team (PET) to lead day-to-day delivery of activities for which PAGASA is the EE. PET will work closely with other PAGASA technical experts and service delivery staff not directly involved in the project implementation, but who have a role in supporting the project.

105. FAO-PH will establish a Technical Assistance Team (TAT) that will work closely with PMO and PET. TAT will have the following national officers:
* **National Project Coordinator:** The DA-FAO PH focal point for the project who will perform technical backstopping, coordinate technical assistance activities, and represent FAO-PH in technical discussions.
* **National Operations Specialist**
* **National Social Inclusion Specialist**
* **National Environment and Social Safeguards Specialist**

106. TAT will also have a team of subject-matter technology (TA) experts to provide technical guidance, support, and capacity-building for the project implementation. These experts will also be responsible for providing backstopping services during the project. They will include:
* **International Climate Information Services Expert**
* **International CRA Planning and Training Expert**

## Other Project Partners
* The Land Bank of Philippines (Land Bank) is one of the primary state financial institutions for agricultural development and the formulator of the PILAR project as a DA of the GCF. Land Bank will work as a key implementation partner of the project, particularly in activities pertaining to farmers’ access to credit. The project will provide technical assistance to the Land Bank on its business model, available to the Land Bank and provide necessary training on this information, all of which will help the bank design its outreach strategy for the project’s target beneficiaries. The project will also support the development of credits and other financial programmes to reduce risk from CCR and natural disasters. Any complementary or additional credit facilities provided by the Land Bank will be subject to the terms and conditions set out in the agreement (See Appendix 4 for mapping of complementarity with other GCF projects in the country).

* Agri-Financial Institutions (AFIs) are financial institutions that provide access to credit, insurance, and other financial services. The project will support AFIs to develop their own credit policies and programs in support of its priority goals. It will be involved in developing strategies to increase and improve access to credit for the target beneficiaries through AFIs. The project will also provide technical assistance on innovative financing schemes for adoption of CRA practices and technologies by small farmers, including the development of a financial model that can be used as a reference. These tools then will be made available to the project’s sites and beneficiaries as a finance stream. The project will tap into its expertise in credit risk management and financial inclusion to support the development of AFIs, NGOs, organizations, and rural financial organizations, as well as to advocate and disseminate activities to promote and generate greater awareness and understanding for agri-credit programs.

* Department of Agriculture (DA) is a key implementing partner of this project through components 2 and 3 and through planning key activities on CRA to fit the needs of agrarian reform beneficiaries identified in the

## Flow of funds and contractual arrangements

202. After the signature of the FAA, FAO will enter into a Project Agreement (the Subsidary Agreement) with the Host Country (HC), which will be signed by the Government entities involved in the project, including the Philippine Department of Agriculture (DA), the Philippine Atmospheric, Geographical and Astronomical Services Administration (PAGASA), and the Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP). The Project Agreement shall contain provisions on the Privileges and Immunities of the Specialized Agencies; 2) provisions with respect to the rights and obligations of the HC and the DA, PAGASA and BFP in relation to the project; as well as the respective roles and responsibilities of the Accredited Entity and the Host Country for the implementation of the Project.

203. In line with the project fund flows and contractual arrangements outlined in Figure 8.4.1, GCF proceeds received by FAO in its capacity as Accredited Entity will flow to FAO as well as to DA and PAGASA, passing through the Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP) as per Section 70.2 of Manual on Subsidary Agreements. Through the BTU, it will be responsible for the channelling of the grant proceeds from the Accredited Entity to the Executing Entities. The GCF funds received by DA and PAGASA will be used in accordance with the following: (1) BTU receives from GCF the relevant tranche(s) from GCF proceeds for DA and PAGASA. These funds are transferred to the Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP), which is responsible for disbursing these funds to DA and PAGASA, who will credit the Executing Entity sub-account within the Bureau of Treasury (DA) and PAGASA sub-accounts provided by BFP. The GCF proceeds received by DA and PAGASA will be used in accordance with DA and PAGASA, to pay vendors as per project needs. GCF proceed will be converted from US Dollar to Philippine peso at the prevailing exchange rate on the date of disbursement.

204. Procured parties will be contracted in accordance with FAO rules and regulations for procuring goods and services as set out in Section 70.1 of Manual on Subsidary Agreements.

205. FAO will execute the project in accordance with FAO rules, regulations, policies and procedures. Financial management and procurement under this project will be guided by relevant FAO-rules and regulations as relevant provisions are applicable to the project. The GCF funds received by FAO for this project shall be subject to all rules and regulations were reviewed by GCF and deemed satisfactory by the GCF Secretariat and Accreditation Panel as part of the accreditation process.

## B. Justification for GCF funding request (max 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

206. GCF financing through this project will leverage investments by the government and private sectors to support climate change adaptation in agriculture. The project will be implemented in a phased manner, including a progressive shift towards meeting the demands for support for farmers' climate actions. Specifically, it will: (1) provide technical assistance to farmers and other stakeholders on climate-smart agricultural practices; (2) assist in the development of an integrated framework for climate-resilient agriculture; and (3) support CRA enterprise investment plan implementation – to systematically adjust their package of goods, technical assistance, and financial support services to enable farmers' CRA adoption, initially in the project sites and later on at scale.


---
**Page 64**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

B

201. Similarly, GCF financing will allow PAGASA to adjust its CIS delivery as well as to mobilize budget for sustainable land management and climate-resilient agriculture projects in the country.

202. Similarly, GCF financing will also leverage additional investments from finance facilities, such as from the Agricultural Credit Policy (ACP) Trust Fund, which is expected to provide funding for the government to scale up and roll out climate credit and loan programs for vulnerable farmers; or from Land Bank’s Climate Resilient Agriculture Financing Program, which provides financing for private LCA projects for transition towards climate-resilient agriculture.

203. As added value, GCF financing through this project will produce public goods accompanied by institutional capacity building in the country to ensure that investments are made in a way that supports farmers and other stakeholders in achieving climate change resilience goals but also the day-to-day farming and investment decisions made by millions of rural households. In so doing, the project will underpin household-level investments for climate change adaptation.

204. This project is designed to sustain impact in demonstrating GCF’s ability to scale up CRA enterprises. The project will do this through a combination of (i) scaling up and replicating the use of PAGASA as a CRA service provider, (ii) strengthening the capacity of government agencies, local governments, and other stakeholders for budgeting, knowledge-and information-sharing systems, and capacity-building for scaling up CRA enterprises in agriculture and rural livelihoods, and (iii) building on existing public-private partnerships to increase farmers’ capacity to prepare CRA investment plans and link them to the resources of much larger programmes and financial institutions. The project will also seek to leverage additional investments from other development financing institutions (IFIs) (e.g., the World Bank, IAFD, ADB) and lend its focus on infrastructure, production, and value-chain development.

205. To ensure that these investments are made in a way that supports farmers and other stakeholders in achieving climate change resilience goals but also the day-to-day farming and investment decisions made by millions of rural households, the project will underpin household-level investments for climate resilience.

206. The combined value of investments in agriculture projects and programmes from the government and development partners is well over USD 1 billion, with outreach extending to over 50 million rural people (Feasibility Study, 2019). This project will provide a platform for scaling up the level of investments for more climate-resilient development.

207. Due to its emphasis on an enabling systems and capacities for climate change adaptation, the project will make significant contributions, including in capacitating a CCA or CRA service provider in the private sector, while laying out a foundation for the country to scale up such investments.

208. The GCF project will focus primarily on geographic areas with agriculture and rural livelihood systems that have the highest poverty rates (Appendix B). It will also include some pilot projects and proposals in the Philippines focusing on urban and coastal systems (see Appendix E). Poverty rates in the project areas are higher than national averages, and many of these communities would not be able to adopt CRA and transition towards climate-resilient livelihoods as many of DA banner programmes have failed.

209. Furthermore, the COVID-19 pandemic has had severe impacts on agriculture and further exacerbates vulnerabilities. GPHR has spent considerable resources to manage disruptions to the local food supply chain, including through the implementation of a national food security programme (NFS). The GCF project will provide a supplementary budget of P50 billion to help farmers and agribusinesses. The GPH’s existing debt-to-GDP ratio is 48 percent in FY2019/2020, which is projected to increase by an additional 3-5 percent from 54-6 percent in 2020. This would limit the country’s ability to invest in climate change adaptation.

B. Exit Strategy (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

214. Sustainability of the project outcomes will be ensured by CCA enterprises to be adopted in DA programmes. The project will link agriculture production with CRA practices to value chains to generate sustainable income, at the same time developing institutional capacity of the government and private sector to provide CRA services, including creating blended finance mechanisms and enabling enterprise development.

## The project approach and activities are fully in line with government policies and initiatives, which should ensure that the project will be institutionalized through the One SA Framework.

### 215.
The project design process estimated the GIM requirements for infrastructure, equipment, and services and all other costs associated with the implementation of the project. The project team has provided detailed estimates on GIM requirements and other recurrent costs to be incurred following project closure and is able to pay PAGASA through the existing budgetary framework. This is in line with the provisions set out under the PAGASA Modernization Programme. Thus, the enhanced CIS capacities and augmented agrometeorological observational capabilities will be sustained.

### 216.
The CIS Platform and Regional CIS Centers are embedded into the DA's existing operations at the national and regional levels, with DA hosting them from the outset and providing staff to be transferred/assigned to the project. The DA has also established a Project Management Unit (PMU) under the PAGASA Provincial CIS Centers and provide staff for operations.

### 217.
Asset transfer is governed by FAO Handbook 503.6 (Asset Management – Disposal of Assets: Transfer to Final Recipient). The project will be implemented in accordance with this handbook, subject to beneficiaries, governments or other UN organizations. In case of projects financed from voluntary contributions transferred to the DA, the terms and conditions set out under the Memorandum of Understanding (MOU) or agreement (project documentation) contains other provisions. [..] The Responsible Officer will ensure that a Project Implementation Unit (PIU) is established within the DA to manage the project. The PIU will be representative of the recipient organization at the time the property is handed over." This may apply to technological equipment, such as weather stations, climate change devices, and the like.

## Mainstreaming CRA into existing services and programmes

### 218.
The DA has already started to mainstream and institutionalize climate adaptation and mitigation in its various services and programmes. The following are examples of these: (a) The Department of Agriculture to Agriculture and Fisheries Transformation, which operationalizes the NFMIP; provides 12 key strategies. Of these, three are specifically related to climate change: (i) Climate-smart agriculture; (ii) Climate-resilient Crops/Beefaring Agri Clusters through Sustainable CRA services; (b) Province-led Agriculture Extension Services (PASES); (c) The Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR) – the DENR has a Strategic communication support, including through agreed and CIS. In particular, the project will be implemented also by the DA's Climate Change Division.

### 219.
The project will be a catalyst for the scaling-up and mainstreaming of new strategies, approaches, and technologies employed by DA. Through its activities and mainstreaming of CRA, the project will transform existing agricultural practices in the Philippines to climate-resilient ones. The project will also provide climate-smart crops, which will provide co-financing starting from three year of project implementation and reinvest in packages in support of climate change adaptation and mitigation. This will ensure that the climate change activities are rolled out and scaled up through government budgeting and funding processes.

### 220.
The project is conceptualized such that by design, it is aligned with climate change policies (Appendix I) and can be mainstreamed into existing services and programmes of the DA. ## Financial viability of CRA models and enterprises

The financial viability of CRA models and enterprises has been assessed based on a detailed analysis of the cost-benefit ratios for each model or enterprise identified during project preparation. The analysis shows that all models and enterprises are financially viable, with some having higher returns than others.

### 2.3 Financial viability of CRA models and enterprises

All the technologies identified during project preparation have been subject to financial cost-benefit analyses. The results show that all the technologies are financially viable, with some having higher returns on investment compared to others. This analysis will be used as a basis for selecting the most appropriate technology for each producer group.

### EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s “Impact Investment Framework.”

#### D.1 Direct potential (max. 600 words, approximately one page)

##### Direct beneficiaries

225. The Direct Beneficiaries of the Project are 1,230,000 vulnerable and resource-poor persons (250,000 farmer households) in 100 municipalities in nine provinces of five administrative regions of the Philippines. These are the most vulnerable people in the country who will be applying climate-resilient technologies and developing CRA enterprises with the support from the project. This group includes women-headed households, indigenous peoples, and persons with disabilities. They represent over 1% of the rural population in the Philippines – among the most vulnerable to climate change in the country. The Project will provide them with the necessary knowledge, skills, resources, and services that they need to significantly increase their income. Furthermore, the farming system diversification efforts as part of CRA practices are expected to improve food security and reduce poverty levels for these groups.

226. The project activities are expected to ensure that all direct beneficiaries – who live in areas of the Philippines that are (or will be) among the most negatively affected by climate change – have more resilient livelihoods at the time they exit the project.

##### Indirect beneficiaries

227. In so doing, the project will make important contributions to:

- Reduced emission and increased resilience: ARBA - Vulnerable people and communities; ARBA - Health, well-being, food, water security, and MDG; Forest and land use.
- Enhanced institutional capacity: GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for low emission climate resilient development pathways in a country-wide context. These include the development of policies, strategies, plans, guidelines, standards, dissemination, development or transfer and innovation; and Core Indicator 8 - Degree to which GCF investments support effective knowledge generation and learning processes, and use of good practices, methodologies and standards.

228. For further information on direct and indirect beneficiaries number, see Section 2.9 of Annex 2.

### METHODS AND ASSUMPTIONS

* Data on the number of households by municipality and overlaps of climate impact by province, for both priority provinces and non-priority provinces in terms of CC (impact see Section 2.9 of Annex B for targeting information).
* Assumptions: CRA interventions will be implemented in all targeted municipalities; average participation rate of farmers joining CRA enterprise development training and investment planning; and CRA enterprises will be established.
* High level of engagement and activity of women in rural livelihoods, agriculture, and CRA enterprises (hence the inclusion of women in the analytical work and field consultations).

## Climate change mitigation impacts

The project is also expected to achieve a reduction in GHG emissions of 4.38 MtCO2e over 20 years through the application of climate-resilient and low-emission agriculture practices on 250,000ha by 750,000 farmers.

# D2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## The project aims to be a catalyst for a broader shift in agriculture across the Philippines, from its baseline state of extreme vulnerability—terms of high damages and losses from extreme climate events and low adaptive capacity—to one where farmers, local government units (LGUs), private sector, and farming communities are able to understand and monitor short-term and longer-term climate-related risks.

### 233. The proposed project will make crucial contributions to the process of transforming Philippine agriculture toward climate resilience—a clear indication of its potential to produce impact beyond the one-off investments made by the GCF in this project. This is because it will build on existing work, including the projects supported under the GCF programs addressing urban and coastal climate-related challenges, thus contributing to a more holistic adaptation approach across the country.

#### 234. Institutionalizing provision of CIS and CRA services

- The project will consolidate the flow of information from PAGASA's networks of agrimeteorological stations and from GIA, together with institutional mechanisms and capacity building for farmers, LGUs, and private sector actors to access this information in a timely manner, both from the national to local level. This project will put in place national and local mechanisms to ensure continued collaboration between PAGASA and other government agencies, as well as with the private sector, to create an information system to enable agriculture sectors across the country to execute climate-resilient planning and decision-making.

- Strengthening extension and service delivery capacity: Drawing on the outputs of the CIS system, the project will enhance the capacity of DA's agricultural extension services at all levels (national, regional, provincial, LGU) for the development of the CRA training programs and IEC materials. This will enable DA to train more people, particularly young farmers, women, and smallholder farmers, in climate-resilient agriculture practices through the use of CIS and CRA enterprise development facilitators. DA will also be able to create more accessible CRA services that are tailored to the needs of different types of farmers (e.g., young farmers, women, smallholders) by strengthening extension and advisory services—those which are key determinants of the patterns of agricultural development in the country.

- Developing new business model and demonstrating successful adaptation at scale: The project will help farmers, LGUs, and private sector actors to access more climate-informed services through improved service delivery systems, and CC challenges through direct farm-level investments and support to resilient supply chains. These efforts will be supported by a strong enabling environment for the development of new business models that are carefully targeted and budgeted plans but also through key capacity-building of extension and other technical support and training programs. The project will also help farmers, LGUs, and private sector actors to access more climate-informed services at scale, as well as on, as well as on CRA practices and support mechanisms for different types of farming systems.

#### 235. Mainstreaming CRA into core government services, programmes, planning, and budget: Learning from the project's investment in CRA enterprises will help DA and GoPh integrate the project approaches and CRA content into its core services, programs, plans, and budgets. This will be achieved by building the capacities of regional and local agriculture offices, LGUs, NGOs, and finance services to gather, analyze, and use climate information for planning and decision-making at all levels.

- The project will facilitate continuing support for and collective learning among concerned government staff and citizens' groups in the development of new business models that are tailored to the needs of different types of farmers. This is supported by exchanges between different but comparable regions and provinces in the country. The ultimate aim is to ensure that these new business models become a sustainable market (including a substantive shift in the way considerable government expenditures are used).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 69

## Developing more resilient CCA supply chains and support. Through the CIS platform, the project will engage agri-enterprises, especially MSMEs and financial actors in its target areas, promoting the use of CIS for resilient supply chain management. The project will also promote the development of public-private sector partnerships/agreements/partnerships in support of CCA enterprise investment plan implementation. Also note that as a result, other projects may be able to leverage this platform to better understand and respond to the increasing demand for climate-informed supplychainservices from farmers (as per the above point), and be incentivized to respond.

### Working with Land Bank, ACPC, and other financial institutions to improve financial services to continue supporting farmers' adaptation. The project will work with Land Bank, ACPC, and other financial institutions across the Philippines to develop a more nuanced understanding of climate change risks and price their financial products accordingly. This will help them better understand how to support farmers in adapting to climate change and be more confident in their CCA practices. To start with, the project will work with a selected number of existing clients who are already using CIS services. The project will also provide training for financial institutions and organizations face in accessing these services and to facilitate group farming. The learning will inform the development of a business case for scaling up this model across other financial institutions. This will lead to further scale-up financing in partnership with the GCF, drawing on Land Bank's status as a Direct Access Entity.

### Establishing feedback mechanisms to refine the above systems. Mechanisms will be put in place to facilitate learningbetween among regions, provinces, and farmer groups for scaling up CCA. These feedback mechanisms will be used to improve the CIS platform and other tools. The project will also work with farmers to ensure that it is important for the long-term process of transforming agriculture in the Philippines. They can also support the acquisition of land by smallholder farmers through a pilot program.

#### D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

235. The project will contribute to climate change mitigation by reducing greenhouse gas emissions, primarily through alternate wetting and drying (AWD) technology for rice culture and also better land-use practices and management of water on land in line with a sustainable approach to the economic and financial benefits.

236. The project will also generate environmental co-benefits such as improved biodiversity (by increasing tree planting), agroforestry systems, more effective use of irrigation, strengthened vegetation, and enhanced micro-watershed management; soil quality, water quality and availability, among others. Adopted technologies and practices will also contribute to the achievement of other Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) including ecosystem benefits, including through reduced inputs and more integrated agricultural systems (SDG15). Where appropriate, the project will work with relevant stakeholders such as local government/unit, national government/unit, water agencies and on a province-by-province basis.

#### Social co-benefits

237. Although the GCF funds will be used for CCA concerns, they will have a positive impact in terms of social development. The project will provide training to women and indigenous people, who seldom provide adequately for the needs of women producers in the Philippines, special training and approaches will be considered to improve their farming livelihoods and food security, and reducing poverty. The main aim is to ensure that women are not left behind as a result of climate change. Women are often the primary targets of marginalization and concerns about access, including to safe and nutritious food; as well as the availability of staples from their traditional diets. The project will take into account and make specific efforts to address these concerns.

238. The project will pay special attention to women and indigenous people, providing them with appropriate training and approaches to improve their farming livelihoods and food security, and reducing poverty.

The project will generate general social benefits from the explicit inclusion of women in technical trainings, CRA consultations, and other activities. Women's participation in these processes will also have a positive impact on women in terms of diversifying production systems and income, while also strengthening the resilience of production systems to climate change-related natural hazards and extreme weather events, and changing precipitation patterns. CRA adoption also has important implications for women’s health and nutrition.
