240. The project will conduct gender-responsive extension services and project outreach that consider women's needs and priorities in all aspects of the project. Gender-sensitive training materials will be developed to ensure that CRA practices consider the differentiated needs and contexts of men from diverse socio-cultural backgrounds, including indigenous peoples (see Section 3.1.2.1), pastoralists (see Section 3.1.2.2), women (see Section 3.1.2.3) and youth (see Section 3.1.2.4). The project will also target men and women in rural areas, including young people and GAPP Annex 8).

240. The project will conduct gender-responsive extension services and project outreach that consider women's needs and priorities in all aspects of the project. Gender-sensitive training materials will be developed to ensure that CRA practices consider the differentiated needs and contexts of men from diverse socio-cultural backgrounds, including indigenous peoples (see Section 3.1.2.1), pastoralists (see Section 3.1.2.2), women (see Section 3.1.2.3) and youth (see Section 3.1.2.4). The project will also target men and women in rural areas, including young people and GAPP Annex 8).

241. The project is also expected to generate positive impacts for both men and women. The project recognizes that indigenous peoples' rights are safeguarded through the implementation of an Indigenous Peoples Plan (see Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework in Chapter 6 of the ESMP located in Annex F). It will further leverage these safeguards by ensuring that CRA practices are integrated into existing community plans, which are dynamic and ever-evolving. Stakeholder consultations indicated that some indigenous peoples are already using CRA practices to improve their livelihoods. The project will provide incentives for indigenous peoples to share their knowledge with other stakeholders through the use of extension services, agriculture and biodiversity information, farming practices, and community plans for agricultural development. As indigenous peoples' rights are safeguarded by the implementation of an Indigenous Peoples Plan, and as CRA is an integral use of natural resources, the project is well positioned to generate diverse benefits for them as long as it does not create new barriers or exacerbate existing ones. The project will also address any existing barriers, and ultimately applies an inclusive and participatory process that considers the differentiated contexts, priorities, needs, and rights of indigenous peoples.

242. Economic: The overall objective of the project is to increase the climate change resilience of the most vulnerable households through developing CRA enterprises and by its adoption of such enterprises profitable. The project will also contribute to increasing the income and food security of the most vulnerable households as they adopt project-recommended agricultural practices. These are detailed in Annex 8.

### D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

243. The project will target regions and provinces and locally identified agriculture-based groups that are most vulnerable to climate change impacts. It is expected that the majority of these groups will be located in the poorest ones with the lowest Human Development Indices in the country, ranking as low as many least-developed countries (LDCs). These groups include pastoralists, indigenous peoples, smallholder farmers, and agro-pastoralists. Mountainous and mountainous nature of most regions makes access to local services and the capacity of many small landholders to adopt new technologies and practices very limited. The project will also target women-headed poor, often-small landholders with fewer assets. There are key needs and constraints that the project will address:

- **Financial needs** of the project beneficiaries, who will be poor and vulnerable families with very little savings or access to credit.
- **Fiscal constraints** among relevant government authorities, especially in investment capacity-building and in areas where there is a lack of infrastructure. These are particularly social services relative to the country's overall adaptation needs).

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 71

## D. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The bulk of support for the current project's focus – public goods, capacity-building, and technical assistance – are these type of support that is absolutely essential to transforming agriculture and reaching scale, but they generally do not have a direct return on investment in terms of financial returns. This is different from other development assistance (unlike strongly linked to activities with rapid and high returns). Other development assistance partners may be able to back this type of "soft" support, but investment levels tend to be modest and more geared toward pilots.

### D.5 Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project will build on the Philippines' existing national agriculture and fisheries modernization plans – the National Agriculture and Fisheries Modernization and Industrialization Plan (NAFMIP) for 2020-2025 and the One DA Strategic Plan for 2017-2030. The project will also complement the ongoing work of the Department of Agriculture (DA) and CRA services, as well as mainstreaming CRA enterprise development for sustainable CRA adoption, the project will adopt a "whole-of-government" approach to ensure that all relevant government agencies in the Philippines are empowered with prosperous farmers and fisherfolk." This approach includes, among others, inter-agency coordination on climate change adaptation and disaster risk management; national fisheries services network; nation-wide extension services for CRA practices and technologies; climate-resilient technology; business development services; value chain development; market access; agricultural insurance; marketing; and disaster risk management and early warning.

The project will be integrated with key national programs such as PAGASA Modernization, AMA, DAA, and other national programmes relevant to the project. Co-finance primarily comes from PAGASA Modernisation and DA's Banner Programmes, and the project is designed to help these and other national programmes succeed in their objectives.

### D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

The project’s total proposed budget is estimated at USD 39.2 million, of which 67% (USD 26.27 million) is proposed to be co-financed by the GCF. The project will directly benefit about 500 CRA farmers who are expected to deliver direct benefits to an estimated 1.25 million people in farming households and indirect benefits to 5 million people, or direct and indirect beneficiaries constitute roughly 1.7% and 5%, respectively of the Philippine total population.

The project’s proposed concessionality level is rationalised through (a) the project's provision of goods that are not available at market prices; (b) the project's focus on providing CRA services to farmers in selected weather-vulnerable agro-ecological zones, and (b) the delivery of goods to these farmers that might be priced above market rates but can contribute to the public good (e.g., improved CRA farming practices that will help farmers adapt to climate change).

Co-financing, leveraging, and mobilized long-term investments

With the contribution from the GCF of USD 27 million, the Project will have a gross co-financing ratio of 1.2 (USD 39.2 million / USD 32.8 million). The project will leverage additional co-finance contributions from the DA and 30% from PAGASA. The project would also leverage parallel finance in the form of credit from Land Bank (and other agricultural finance institutions) for CRA farmers who are not able to access credit through the GCF. It is estimated that if the project 45,000 target farmers/500 CRA Enterprises would borrow at 60% of the project investment package.


---
**Page 76**
---

This would be USD (20-32 million) of the borrowing rate is at 85% of the project investment package. This conservative estimate of average loan per farmer/CRA Enterprise is well within the Land Bank's ceiling and lower than current benchmark examples of the average P126,000 to rich farmers and cooperatives (see note on estimate of leverage finance).

### Project Financials and Sustainability

The results of the ex-ante CRA for this proposal suggest that in case all approved CRA enterprises are implemented under scenarios 2 and 3, the expected financial revenues from selection of the proposed CRA selected in the seven representative CRA enterprises in the five different agroecological zones will outweigh the costs. The financial results of the CRAs were calculated based on the average loan per farmer/CRA Enterprise as shown above. In case all approved CRAs are implemented under scenarios 2 and 3, the implementation of seven representative CRA enterprises in the five different agroecological zones was assumed. The total cost for scenario 1 (CRA1) is USD 80 million to USD 95 million (USD 400 per hectare), USD 670 million to USD 820 million (USD 1,400 to USD 15,700), depending on the proposed intervention scenario and agroecological zone. The total cost for scenarios 2 (CRA2) is USD 390 million to USD 460 million (USD 180 per hectare), USD 3,500 hectares in the case of interventions 2 (CRA2), 6 (CRA6), and 7 (CRA7); 32,500 hectares in the case of


---
**Page 77**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 73

## Intervention (ICRAS) and 4 (DRAs); and 50,000 hectares in the case of Intervention 5 (CRAS) and range from PHP 2.6 billion to PHP 25 billion (USD 50 million to USD 473 million). These results suggest the proposed interventions are economically viable and will generate significant net economic benefits for smallholder farmers, which will help offset the costs. Consequently, the smallscale farmers included in this project are likely to experience positive financial returns from their investments. They are expected to continue using proposed CRA practices after the project ends.

### Economic Viability

253. The economic analysis results were based on the same assumptions as in the financial analysis discussed above (Section 4.1). The analysis was conducted by applying a social discount rate of 6% and using shadow prices of carbon (the lower, and upper-bound prices of carbon, as per the World Bank’s calculations that adjust the financial revenues and costs to their economic values. An economic discount rate (social discount rate) of 6% is used in this analysis). The results show a range of net present value (NPV) from PHP 2 billion to PHP 19 billion (USD 76 million to USD 305 million) over the period of 20 years, depending on the carbon price. The internal rate of return (IRR) ranges between 8% and 40%, with a lower bound NPV range from PHP 2 billion to PHP 19 billion (USD 76 million to USD 305 million) over the period of 20 years, depending on the carbon price. The results show that the project would generate significant net economic incremental benefits, with accounting for costs of project implementation, calculated over 20 year horizon and using a social discount rate of 6% (with lower bound NPV range from PHP 2 billion to PHP 19 billion; with upper carbon pricing), with IRRs of 33% and 34%, respectively. The results obtained in the ex ante CBA’s economic cost benefit analysis show that the project would generate significant net economic incremental benefits compared to the "Without Project" scenario. For full results, please refer to Annexes 2 and 3.

### Application of best practices

254. The additional value of GCF involvement in this project will be realized through the Project’s more systemic approach to scaling up climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies. This is achieved by using lessons learned from other successful projects, such as the Green Climate Fund (GCF) supported programs, World Bank projects (e.g., Mindanao Inclusive Agriculture Development Project [MIADP], expected in 2022; and the Philippine Climate Change Resilience Program [PCCRP]), and by using a multi-stakeholder financing source, especially those closely associated with the national executing agencies/units involved in the Project.

## 1.2 Strengthening capacity to produce CIS

This Activity will support PAGASA and improve integrated agricultural climatic data systems.

### 1.2.1 Strengthening capacity to produce CIS

This Activity will establish National CIS Platform Regionally, with a focus on the Philippines, harnessing digital technologies and build capability to manage the Platform/Curries

#### 1.2.1.1 [PAGASA] Facilitate TWGs and CIS Platform to produce

- **Regional CIS Centres with staff and equipment at DA**
- **Provincial CIS Centres with staff and equipment**

This sub-activity provides PAGASA with human resources to contribute to the establishment and facilitation of TWGs and of PAGASA's role in the development of the National CIS Platform.

#### 1.2.1.2 [PAGASA] Provide training and technical support for TWGs and CIS Platform

FAO will assist in developing TWGs on a series of pilot TWGs and the design of CIS Platforms, providing training and technical assistance to PAGASA staff.

#### 1.2.1.3 [DA] CIS Development by National Supportive Specialists

- **Review of existing Regional TWGs and assist in TWGs**
- **Training on TWGs for DA CIS and CRA services development**

FAO will provide support to the design, implementation, and operation of the CIS Platform.

#### 1.2.1.4 [DA] CIS Conformances developed and monitored and contribute to

- **All up-graded agromet stations in regionally-based TWGs**
- **All DA and CGIAR staff trained on GADM**
- **All DA CGIAR and DA staff trained on GADM**

#### 1.2.1.5 [PAGASA] Upgrade agromet data, deliver training and contribute to

- **All up-graded agromet stations in regionally-based TWGs**
- **All DA CGIAR and DA staff trained on GADM**
- **All CGIAR and DA staff trained on GADM**

#### 1.2.1.6 [PAGASA] Provide technical support and quality assurance for CIS production

FAO will provide technical support for the upgrade of agromet stations, data collection, and quality assurance for CIS products.

#### 1.2.1.7 [PAGASA] Operation & Maintenance of agromet network

All up-graded agromet stations fully maintained by year Y1 by PAGASA

## 1.2.1.1 (DA) Deliver CTA enterprise development training

This Activity will expand around 1,500 farmers into more advanced CTA options by providing knowledge to them via CTA Facilitators. This includes training on new equipment and technologies in addition to which will be adopted by the CTA enterprises.

### 2.1.1.1 (DA) Deliver CTA enterprise development training

- **Farmer learning groups (1,500):** Established, drawing on existing farmer learning groups and/or established Farmer Master Trainers/CTA Enterprise Development Facilitators to deliver training.
- At least one post-training/post-conversion job per farmer group.
- Proportion of women and youth benefitting from this activity.

### 2.1.1.3 (FAO) Design and provide technical support for CTA enterprise development training

FAO technical support including mentoring with the Farmer Learning Groups, as well as providing a facilitation plan to address the specific needs of farmers and facilitating plans to ensure linkage with the private sector.

### 2.1.1.4 (DA) Facilitate CTA enterprise development training

- At least one post-training/post-conversion job per farmer group.
- Designing specific CTA enterprise development training for each farmer learning group.
- At least one private sector and financial institution engaging in the CTA enterprise development learning process.

### 2.1.1.5 (DA) Facilitate CTA enterprise investment planning

With technical support from FAO, DA will facilitate the creation of a business plan for each farmer learning group to be used as input into CTA Enterprise Development/RRM villages.
- At least one private sector and financial institution engaging in the CTA enterprise development learning process.

### 2.1.1.6 (DA) Facilitate CTA enterprise investment planning

With technical support from FAO, DA will facilitate the creation of a business plan for each farmer learning group to be used as input into CTA Enterprise Development/RRM villages.
- At least one private sector and financial institution engaging in the CTA enterprise development learning process.

## 2.1.2 (DA) Facilitate CTA enterprise investment planning

This Activity will help clusters of the 1,500 farmer learning groups into CTA options by providing them with technical assistance (e.g., business plans) and facilitating them to prepare CTA enterprise investment plans and implement CTA options.

### 2.1.2.1 (DA) Facilitate CTA enterprise investment planning

- At least 300 CTA enterprise investment plans and business plans developed.

## 3.1.2 Maintain CCA practices and support development of national GHG programmes

This Activity will mainstream DA and project approaches into national and LGU agriculture and forestry policies, strategies, plans and projects, with a focus on capacity building for DA and LGUs staff in the design, implementation and monitoring of CCA adoption beyond the project areas.

### 3.1.2.1 FAO Policy advisory and facilitation for CCA mainstreaming

FAO will provide policy advice to DA in providing continued policy support for CCA adoption at national level with a focus on different approaches of different programs to promote risk-informed decision making.

### 3.1.2.2 FAO Technical support for monitoring and evaluating existing CCA practices and policies

FAO will provide technical support for the design and evaluation of existing CCA practices and policies, with a focus on developing methodologies and systems to evaluate the effectiveness of CCA practices and policies in terms of their impact on climate change mitigation and adaptation.

### 3.1.2.3 FAO Technical support for monitoring and evaluating new CCA practices and policies

FAO will provide technical support for the design and evaluation of new CCA practices and policies, with a focus on developing methodologies and systems to evaluate the effectiveness of CCA practices and policies in terms of their impact on climate change mitigation and adaptation.

## 3.1.3 Develop a national CCA implementation monitoring system

This Activity will develop an on-line M&E network for capacity building by DA and LGUs for monitoring existing and new CCA practices and policies, with a focus on developing methodologies and systems to evaluate the effectiveness of CCA practices and policies in terms of their impact on climate change mitigation and adaptation.

### 3.1.3.1 Establish and operate the National CCA monitoring system

The National CCA monitoring system will include baseline data, mid- and end-line surveys, and a set-up system for continuous monitoring and evaluation of CCA practices and policies at national level with a focus on developing methodologies and systems to evaluate the effectiveness of CCA practices and policies in terms of their impact on climate change mitigation and adaptation.

### 3.1.3.2 FAO Technical support for monitoring and evaluating existing CCA practices and policies

FAO will provide technical support for the design and evaluation of existing CCA practices and policies, with a focus on developing methodologies and systems to evaluate the effectiveness of CCA practices and policies in terms of their impact on climate change mitigation and adaptation.

### 3.1.3.3 FAO Technical support for monitoring and evaluating new CCA practices and policies

FAO will provide technical support for the design and evaluation of new CCA practices and policies, with a focus on developing methodologies and systems to evaluate the effectiveness of CCA practices and policies in terms of their impact on climate change mitigation and adaptation.

## 3.1.4 Facilitate inclusive CCA value chain development

This Activity aims to facilitate public-private community dialogue and partnering for the design and implementation of new CCA practices and policies, with a focus on developing CCA value chains including CCA vulnerable farmers.

### 3.1.4.1 TWGs will convene and update a core set of national and regional CCA value chain actors

TWGs will convene and update a core set of national and regional CCA value chain actors to ensure that the design, implementation and monitoring of new CCA practices and policies are inclusive and representative.

### 3.1.4.2 FAO Technical support for developing and implementing CCA value chains

FAO will provide technical support for the development and implementation of CCA value chains with a focus on ensuring that the design, implementation and monitoring of new CCA practices and policies are inclusive and representative.

E.7 Monitoring, evaluation and reporting arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
246. In its role as Accredited Entity, FAO will oversee and supervise the implementation of this project in accordance with the agreement signed between FAO and the GCF and as detailed in the implementation arrangement document. The monitoring and evaluation activities for this project will comprise of a dedicated M&E specialist, supported by the FAO Office of Evaluation (OE) in headquarters.
257. Throughout the reporting period, project-level monitoring and evaluation will be performed by FAO Philippines in accordance with the GCF Performance Management Framework (PMF). The Project activities will be live with the relevant GEF standards and procedures and the GCF Performance Measurement System (PMS). The OE will provide technical support to the M&E specialist for overall coordination of the monitoring and evaluation activities of the various Project implementing entities and will report to the GCF.
258. The project will include a baseline study, a mid-term evaluation, and final evaluation. In line with the AMA, the Office of Evaluation (OE) will be on charge of the interim and final evaluation of the project. Evaluations will be conducted in accordance with the GCF PMF and GEF standards and procedures as well as other relevant criteria for sustainability, as applicable. Through operational and strategic recommendations, the interim evaluation will contribute to the continuous improvement of the Project implementation during its second half of the Project period. The final evaluations will assess the relevance of the intervention, its overall performance, as well as its impact on poverty reduction and sustainable development in the target communities. A methodology will be used on a detailed methodology including the use of different evaluation methods and tools. In addition to the primary data collection through interviews, focus group discussions, key informant interviews, etc., secondary data will be based on monitoring activities and reports prepared by project staff, including surveys to be implemented at baseline, mid-term and final completion.
Monitoring and Reporting
259. Responsibility for project-level monitoring will rest with the Project Management Office within the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR) who will set up a monitoring system in connection with the three core component projects. The monitoring system will serve to track and report on the performance of the overall project, as well as the outputs, outcomes, impacts and details in the results framework shown above in Section H1. Logic models will be developed for each of the three component projects. These logic models will be completed, and displayed in a dedicated module of the project MRS to be developed and deployed by the Project Management Office. The logic model will serve as an overview of the project and will include the project's data collection processes with mobile telephones and Collect Mobile, an innovative tool developed by FAO.
260. Monitoring officers will monitor AWPB implementation and the outputs that result from it, including those detailed in Section H1. They will also provide regular updates to the Project Management Office and management staff so that they may discuss implementation issues as they arise. This will take the form of bi-weekly meetings with the Project Manager and other key project staff. The monitoring data and information from this monitoring - reported to them by project staff, project partners, and beneficiaries – to prepare the annual progress report (APR) for submission to the GCF. The APR will be submitted in a timely manner and in accordance with the formats and standards agreed upon by the two organizations.
M&E methods
261. The project has some built-in design features for generating M&E data and information:
• Project design includes assisting the DA to set up new elements of a CRA monitoring system (Component 3) to track progress on the project.
• An MIS system will be set up for tracking project activities. This will build on: 1) the well-developed Monitoring Information System (MIS) that has been developed and implemented by the DA with the World Bank PDR project; 2) GAF's indicator system for validating project performance; and 3) the indicators used in the GCF projects that maps indicators at different levels (household, community, sub-national and national).

## Methodologies for monitoring and reporting of the key outcomes of the project/CRA Transformation

262. Before other project activities start, the project will launch a baseline survey, as already noted above in Section E.1: Impact Potential. In the implementation of the baseline and subsequent monitoring of the project, the project will use various surveys, including those for monitoring and tracking of the use of various applications and media, and also small feedback surveys through the Cis Platform; results, as well as its findings from the survey, will not be a substitute for the baseline survey. In addition, a baseline study of key institutions and regulatory systems will be done at project start-up with selected indicators to assess their capacity to support CRA adoption and household effects. The project will also use the following tools: (i) the GCF Enabling Environment Outcome Index to measure outcomes; (ii) and monitor and evaluate achievement of GCF Enabling Environment Outcomes. This tool will be used in conjunction with other monitoring and evaluation mechanisms, including final evaluations. The key outcomes and their related assumptions will be monitored and assessed with the following methods:

- Review of areas that are vulnerable to climate change for assessing continued relevance and targeting of the project – provincial CRA information will be updated using revised downloaded data, remote sensing, and findings from prior review surveys.
- Succession of baseline surveys and other services – surveys, including those for monitoring and tracking of the use of various applications and media, and also small feedback surveys through the Cis Platform; results, as well as its findings from the survey, will not be a substitute for the baseline survey. In addition, a baseline study of key institutions and regulatory systems will be done at project start-up with selected indicators to assess their capacity to support CRA adoption and household effects.

In addition, a baseline study of key institutions and regulatory systems will be done at project start-up with selected indicators to assess their capacity to support CRA adoption and household effects.

263. The project will set up mechanisms to ensure accountability to all stakeholders and beneficiaries, since the PADD's Guiding Principles for Adaptation and Resilience (GPAR) FAQ 140 is a project-based approach, sensitive to the dignity of all human beings, the varying needs of different segments within a community, and the importance of ensuring that no one is left behind. The following accountability mechanisms will be put in place: These mechanisms will deliver GCF commitments, including leadership, governance and staff competencies, transparent communication with stakeholders, and gender equality and inclusion, particularly of women and Indigenous Peoples; such mechanisms will include accountability performance in the project/ME.

# F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## 1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### 265. Several financial and operational risks have been identified that may inhibit the project from achieving the desired results.

#### 266. The project places a strong emphasis on fostering collaboration between the DA and PAGASA. Such collaboration is critical to the success of the project, as it will support the long-term institutional process it is supporting.
- **Description:** This risk will be mitigated by providing a number of channels and levels of collaboration (central and regional) as well as different activities for the PAGASA and DA staff involved in the project. A key element is also the relative intensity of interaction in both technical and management activities, meant to foster both direct relations and increased trust between the two institutions. The project will ensure that a clear framework for joint agreements of said roles and information flows is planned and envisioned by both parties as part of the GCF design process.

### 267. The project is geared toward equipping government and other agriculture sector stakeholders with the systematic application of climate-resilient agricultural practices (CRA). While this approach is innovative, it also presents a considerable emphasis on mainstreaming climate change considerations into major government services and programs. This could be challenging given the competing priorities and resource constraints, which could undermine the project’s ability to achieve its desired results.

#### 268. As with all projects that support farmers in adopting CRA practices, there is a risk that beneficiary farmers may move away from traditional farming practices. Traditional agricultural practices are more familiar to farmers; enterprises are less profitable and more labour-intensive compared with traditional farming practices. This risk could also occur if the project does not provide sufficient financial incentives for farmers to continue using traditional practices.

- **Description:** To mitigate this risk, the project will promote financially viable and attractive farming practices, as well as develop tools to help farmers make informed decisions about their farming practices. The project will also ensure that there is increased resilience to climate change impacts; this will incentivize farmers to continue farming using project-promoted CRA practices while also encouraging them (non-farmers) to adopt best practices as well.

### Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

#### Description
The DA and PAGASA encounter difficulties when trying to enhance collaboration, which is key to the success of this project and the long-term institutional process it is supporting.

### Mitigation Measures (3)

1. **Description:** This risk will be mitigated by providing a number of channels and levels of collaboration (central and regional) as well as different activities for the PAGASA and DA staff involved in the project.
2. **Description:** A key element is also the relative intensity of interaction in both technical and management activities, meant to foster both direct relations and increased trust between the two institutions.
3. **Description:** The project will ensure that a clear framework for joint agreements of said roles and information flows is planned and envisioned by both parties as part of the GCF design process.

## Selected Risk Factor 2
### Category: Technical and operational
#### Probability: Low
#### Impact: Low

**Description:** Some LGUs are unable to sustain the types of climate information and CCA services supported by the project. During the project design, the selection of the 9 LGUs that would bear most of the operational costs for the project (including hosting the Provincial CIS Centre with human resources, providing support to Municipalities and Farmers’ Associations, etc.) was based on their ability to sustain these costs.

However, at present, it is foreseen that additional resources will be allocated to LGUs under new fiscal rules. While COVID-19 has delayed the allocation of these funds, as a result of the project having reached the stage to be scaled up, there will be fewer constraints on government resources.

**Mitigation Measure(s):** This risk will be mitigated by careful planning to scale-up and make use of most of the resources available. The risks are further spread—in terms of capacity-building for services—by working with more local entities, including the DAP and farmers’ associations.


## Selected Risk Factor 3
### Category: Technical and operational
#### Probability: Low
#### Impact: Low

**Description:** Some beneficiary farmers revert to pre-project (climate-vulnerable) farming practices after project closure.

This risk, thoroughly assessed during project design, will be mitigated by ensuring that project-promoted CRA practices are financially viable and generate sufficient income, such that farmers will be incentivized to continue using them. The financial viability of the project-promoted CRA practices is expected to be promoted by the project has been thoroughly assessed through the project’s financial analysis (see Section 3.2).


## Selected Risk Factor 4
### Category: Other
#### Probability: Medium
#### Impact: Medium

**Description:** An extreme weather event (e.g., typhoon) affects regions/provinces targeted by the project, and as a result, project partners and/or beneficiaries refrain their efforts on post-disaster recovery rather than project support.

This risk will be mitigated by the design of the project, which will be active in multiple state regions and provinces in the country, thus minimizing the risk of all project activities being halted in the event of an extreme typhoon. There is also a planned phased and scaling-up process during implementation to shift emphasis onto new priority climate change areas.


## Selected Risk Factor 5
### Category: Technical and operational
#### Probability: Low
#### Impact: Low

**Description:** Traditional relationships between land users and landlords may undermine incentives for small-scale agricultural producers (who may not own the land they farm) to adopt CRA practices. While the majority of farmers (especially

## Mitigation Measures

This risk will be mitigated by promoting widespread awareness of the financial viability of project-promoted CRA practices, as well as the long-term benefits (due to increased resilience to climate change impacts), as well as other environmental and social benefits of CRA. The project will also seek to mitigate the risk that the intended project beneficiaries will not have sufficient incentives to adopt project-promoted practices.

### Selected Risk Factor 6

#### Category: Technical and operational
- **Probability:** Low
- **Impact:** Low
- **Description:** Failure to secure the programme approach with PILAR project (i.e. mismatch of timeframe, non-approval of one project, overlapping of activities and geographical focus).

#### Mitigation Measure(s)
- Section B.2 – Proposed project approach describes the complementarities of this (APA) project and PILAR, both in terms of thematic focus and geographical coverage, in discussion with Land Bank and PILAR partners (RRR) and DCP. The project will also seek to ensure that there is no overlap between the two projects. A formal up-gradation of programme governance at the start of the project to ensure synergies and avoid overlaps as PILAR’s funding proposal is being developed. In addition, a Memorandum of Understanding has been signed with Land Bank for the financial mechanisms; agreement has also been reached with Land Bank to work with selected existing financial institutions to provide CRA financing. The project will seek to ensure that there are no overlaps between the two projects and that the project will support in becoming more CRA-supportive. The learning will feed into the development of new financial instruments and products for CRA, which will be developed by PILAR. The progress on this work will be regularly monitored and discussed, with both APA – for the overall Philippine CRA transition – and the NSD.

### Description

The DAK and PAGASA encounter difficulties in fulfilling the Intra-fund co-financing in the form of staff and equipment, which may impact the timely implementation of the MOU and RPDs.

### Mitigation Measures

This risk will be mitigated by ensuring that during the inception phase of the project these operational concerns are flagged to the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR) for immediate attention. The DENR will ensure that these issues are resolved in time, so as not to impact the implementation of the project. In addition, succeeding cycles until the year 7 of the project, Department Special Orders will be issued to designate (co-financing) funds for the procurement of staff and equipment. These orders will also specify the amount of time allocated doing project duties. Similar orders will be issued for the official use or purchase of office and equipment-related assets for project purposes in line with their co-financing commitment and timing.

## G. GC POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

268. During project formulation, an environmental and social management framework was developed (see the Environment and Social Management Framework in Annex 3). The framework is aligned with the World Bank Group’s policies and against FAO’s Environmental and Social Safeguards. In addition, CIAT studies on CRA practices and FFS considerations were conducted to ensure that the project will not have any adverse social or environmental impacts.

270. The Project Environmental and Social Screening Checklist was prepared (ESMF Appendix B) and the project was identified as a Category B (Moderate Risk) project. While the project is expected to generate primary positive impacts, there are also some potential negative impacts that could arise from project implementation. Small-scale adverse social and environmental impacts could arise due to loss of land for agricultural activities or other uses, such as infrastructure development. The project will be divided into two major segments—during project formulation and later during project implementation.

271. Overall, the project is expected to bring about major positive impacts, as the overall objective is to increase the climate resilience of smallholder farmers in the Philippines. However, due to the potential negative impacts that could arise from project implementation, the project has been classified as Moderate Risk (Category B), largely due to instances of working poverty in the communities where the project will be implemented and the lack of a formal grievance mechanism for addressing grievances related to the involvement of IP communities. It is expected that the project activities will trigger the following Environmental and Social Safeguards.

272. The project will support the agriculture sector in its transition to a climate-resilient development pathway.
Investment in climate-smart agricultural practices is expected to improve climate information system advisories, enable farmers to proactively manage their farms in the face of climate change, and increase the resilience of smallholder farming systems. Improved collaboration between the DA and the PAGASA will be improved. It is expected that this work, combined with work under Component 2, will contribute to the implementation of the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy for the planning and planning of natural resources and agricultural extension services with a climate focus.
Under Component 2, food security and nutrition are addressed through the provision of technical assistance and support for climate change adaptation in the agriculture sector, particularly under the existing DCA crop programs. It is expected that this work will contribute to the implementation of the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy for the planning and planning of natural resources and agricultural extension services with a climate focus.
CRA Enterprise development under Component 2, thus creating a supportive enabling environment for farmers to pursue CRA activities in a more sustainable manner through the provision of technical assistance and institutionalized support. Component 2 is expected to improve the conditions of natural resources and agricultural land upon which farmer’s work, with the introduction of climate-resilient agricultural practices and natural resource

## Management (including water management). Environmentally, improved farming practices will support better-functioning ecosystems, which in turn can positively affect human health and well-being in the long run. Investments in machinery and equipment, as well as high-quality agricultural inputs used on farm-and off-farm, are expected to be made under Component 1. The project is also expected to make investments in training and extension services and activities under Component 2 that will ensure that farmers can “proactively do better” than they would have done without the project. This includes capacity building for women and men farmers. The project aims to promote sustainable pest management, reduce reliance on (overall use of) pesticides, and increase the adoption of organic farming practices.

## Long-term impacts

### Projected long-term impacts

The project is expected to improve food security in the region by increasing agricultural productivity and enhancing resilience to climate change. It will also contribute to improved nutrition for women and children through increased access to nutritious foods. The project aims to reduce poverty, particularly among women farmers who are often more vulnerable than men due to their limited access to resources such as land, credit, and technology.

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 277. Grievance and Redress Mechanism (GRM): The grievance redress mechanism (GRM) is an integral project component that will ensure the effective management of gender-related issues in all project activities and performance. The mechanism is based on FAO requirements and most importantly, it is based on existing mechanisms at the national level. At the community level, the SEAI committee will be responsible for PAGASA will inform communities about the GRM through culturally appropriate mechanisms, ensuring information on mechanisms is disseminated to all project stakeholders. At the provincial level, the Provincial Gender Focal Person (PGFP) and the Project Officer will be responsible for managing the grievance redress mechanism at this level. At the national level, the SEAI committee will be responsible for ensuring the right application of GRM principles. Project-related SEAH and GGR grievances will be managed through the existing mechanisms at the community, provincial, and national levels.

### 278. The gender assessment indicates that the rankings done does not accurately reflect the situation of rural women in the Philippines. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes by men who have extensive agricultural knowledge, administrative skills, and interests. Gender parity in the Philippines is considered a major challenge for development.

### 279. However, the gender assessment indicates that the rankings done does not accurately reflect the situation of rural women in the Philippines. Women are often excluded from decision-making processes by men who have extensive agricultural knowledge, administrative skills, and interests. Despite women's strong capacity, male heads of households have the last word and women often do not take the lead in decision making or resource allocation. The assessment also indicates that women have limited access to their own productive resources such as land, water, tools, seeds, and inputs. Women are also less likely than men to be involved in the cultivation of plants used for craft-making. Their comparative strength is communication, networking, financial management, and commercial negotiations will also contribute more effectively to the adoption of climate-resilient agriculture.

### 280. The proposed project promotes gender empowerment and social inclusion based on a household methodology that will be organized at the beginning of the project and will ensure that farmer activities will be sufficiently different.

## GCF Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 264. The Financial Management Unit for this project, GCF will ensure that financial management and procurement of goods and services using GCF resources will adhere to international standards and good practices. This includes financial management and procurement policies and practices in accordance with the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Paris Agreement (PAS), which include assessments of the entities' financial management and procurement policies and practices standard operating procedures, internal control systems, and other relevant requirements. In addition to ensuring that the necessary financial management and procurement functions are performed by the FAO team in accordance with the GCF Financial Management and Procurement Policies and Procedures, the project will also hire relevant experts in the PMO, including a Chief Financial Officer (CFO) country office.

### 265. The FAO Technical Assistance (TA) team will perform a range of support functions for the project (as outlined in Section B.4), including functions related to financial management and procurement performed by the DA and FAD teams. In addition, the TA team will provide technical assistance on financial management policies and practices and minimizing procurement-related risk. In addition, the FAO TA team will directly manage a portion of the project's budget (approximately 10% of total project budget). The financial management and procurement performed by the team will be guided by relevant FAO rules and regulations, as well as relevant provisions in the GCF Financial Management and Procurement Policies and Procedures. All financial transactions were reviewed and deemed satisfactory by the GCF Secretariat and Accreditation Panel as part of FAO's accreditation process.

### 266. FAO has deployed an Oracle-based Enterprise Resource Planning (ERP) system entitled the "Global Resources Management System" (GRMS). This system provides all FAO employees around the world with travel, human resources, procurement, and financial management functions. The GRMS is a web-based application that supports financial monitoring and reporting, increases transparency and visibility, and strengthens internal control frameworks. The GRMS also includes a coding structure for project costs, which enables the tracking of income and expenditure by donor and project. It also provides a standardized coding structure that enables data to be recorded in a consistent manner across all projects.

### 267. In terms of procurement, FAO operates in accordance with its Manual Section on "Procurement of Goods, Works, and Services," and in terms of sub-contracting the delivery of specific activities using Letters of Agreement (LoA) or Memorandum of Understanding (MoU). The GCF will provide a Chief Financial Officer (CFO) country office which provides policy and operational support to FAO offices and staff undertaking these activities to ensure the

Organizations procure goods, works, and services based on 'Best Value for Money' principles in sub-contracting delivery for agreed results. FAO operates in accordance with its policy governing the indirect implementation of FAD-GCF projects.

286\. Financial management and procurement by the DA and will also be overseen and supervised by the FAD-GCF project supervision team. The FAD-GCF project technical assistance team will undertake regular supervision missions to ensure that all countries are ensuring financial management and procurement are being performed in line with the agreed standards and practices.

289\. FAO Oversight of EIT as fund receiver / Recipient Entity is primarily focused on assuring the timely and unhampered transfer of the full funds to EE. The Financial management of GCF resources under BTI as fund receiver / Recipient Entity will be carried out in accordance with the requirements set by the DA, including PAGASA within a set number of days, bearing fiduciary risk and maintaining sound financial management systems and arrangements.

# Appendix 2 - Climate risks in different regions and farming systems, and adaptation interventions that will address the risks

## Major biomes: Amazon rainforest, tropical savannas, temperate grasslands, Mediterranean basin, Central Asia, East Africa, South America, Southeast Asia, Sub-Saharan Africa, North America, Europe, Southern Africa, Australia, New Zealand, Pacific Islands, and Latin America

### 2) Corn landland farming

#### Current climate
- Increase in temperature by about 1.5°C since 1980s (1976–2010)
- Increase in intensity of rainfall during dry season
- Increase in TC frequency

#### Future climate
- Increase in precipitation in DAF and total annual rainfall decrease in JJA and SOI index increase by 1941–2010

#### Secondary climate impact
- Increase in precipitation in DAF and total annual rainfall >50% by 2050 under RCP.3
- Increase in temperature, especially during dry season

#### Adaptation interventions (and mitigation, if any)
- Heat and drought-tolerant varieties, timing of farm practices (e.g., sowing date), water management and conservation measures, improved irrigation method, irrigation, seasonal and short-term forecasts.

#### Adaptation benefits (and mitigation if any)
- More climate-resilient production (i.e. heat and drought), and reduced risk of food insecurity for farmers. Efficient water use. Reduced methane emissions.

### 3) Wheat landland farming

#### Current climate
- Increase in temperature by about 1.5°C since 1980s (1976–2010)
- Increase in intensity of rainfall during dry season
- Increase in TC frequency

#### Future climate
- Increase in precipitation in DAF and total annual rainfall decrease in JJA and SOI index increase by 1941–2010

#### Secondary climate impact
- Increase in precipitation in DAF and total annual rainfall >50% by 2050 under RCP.3
- Increase in temperature, especially during dry season

#### Adaptation interventions (and mitigation, if any)
- Early warning systems, better crop management, disaster insurance, integrated pest management.

#### Adaptation benefits (and mitigation if any)
- Production damage and loss are reduced through early warning.
- More climate-resilient production (i.e. salt-tolerant), and higher and stable income for farmers.

### 4) Temperate crops

#### Current climate
- Increase in temperature by about 1.5°C since 1980s (1976–2010)
- Increase in intensity of rainfall during dry season
- Increase in TC frequency

#### Future climate
- Increase in precipitation in DAF and total annual rainfall >50% by 2050 under RCP.3
- Increase in temperature, especially during dry season

#### Secondary climate impact
- Increase in precipitation in DAF and total annual rainfall increase up to 2C in MAM and JJA under RCP.4.5
- Increase in temperature, especially during dry season

#### Adaptation interventions (and mitigation, if any)
- Heat and drought-tolerant varieties, timing of farm practices (e.g., sowing date), water management and conservation measures, improved irrigation method, irrigation, seasonal and short-term forecasts.

#### Adaptation benefits (and mitigation if any)
- More climate-resilient production (i.e. heat and drought), and reduced risk of food insecurity for farmers. Efficient water use.

## Criteria/Process Stage / Timing Environmental and Social Risks

### Importance in terms of climate change exposure, crop and livestock production, and poverty levels of the municipality.
The first lots of municipalities will be selected by the Project Steering Committee (PSC) and finally approved under the overall and final confirmation of the AE.

### Working with LGU to support CRA enterprise development planning activities, FFS, MAE, and implementing CRA at farm level.
Each lot of municipalities will establish a local DRR plan in local policies preferably with some counterpart budget. The selection of sites will be based on consultations/agreement with beneficiaries.

### Access to existing climate risk management DRR plans in local policies preferably with some counterpart budget.
The final lots of municipalities will be selected by the Project Steering Committee (PSC) and finally approved under the overall and final confirmation of the AE.

### Past and ongoing relevant initiatives in place to build on, and a clear plan for future actions.
The selection of sites will be based on consultations/agreement with beneficiaries.

### DA's confirmation immediately after the meeting discussion on the draft FFS/CRFs.
FFSC and MSP processes will be carried out in areas where there are IP populations.

## Selection of sites (based on CRFA)
Upon selection of Farmer Beneficiaries, Final selected sites will be reviewed by the Project Steering Committee (PSC) to ensure synergy with PHLAR and other ongoing projects in the Philippines CRA Transformation

### Selection of sites based on consultations/agreement with beneficiaries
The selection will be approved by the relevant LGU and finally approved under the overall and final confirmation of the AE.

The sites for community development such as small-scale irrigation, etc. will be identified through the CRA enterprise investment planning ad

### Priority vulnerable crop/farming systems identified in CRVA, and/or other climate risk management initiatives (e.g., DRR)
These sites will be established after FFS/CRFs, and Local plans have been finalized.

### Priority vulnerable target groups identified in CRVA
The selection of sites will be based on consultations/agreement with beneficiaries

### Prioritizing of each site to the anticipated Farmer Beneficiary Number (FBN) and CRA investment amount (CRAIA)
The selection of sites will be based on consultations/agreement with beneficiaries

## Level Type: Related Information

### Criteria Process

**For Activities 2.1.3**

- **Factor beneficiaries are selected based on being vulnerable, with a target group identified in climate resilience and vulnerability assessments (Section 2.4 of this document) or Section 2.5a), while specific community vulnerable groups (see Section 2.5b of this document) will be prioritized for final confirmation of the AE).**

- **The following eligibility criteria were considered, also linked to FFS sites and participatory planning under Component 2, see above:**
    - Size of land small-scale farmers (<1 ha) or if corn + (2.5 ha)
    - Vulnerability level (crop farm system, farmer group type, local adaptation capacity)
    - Agrarian reform beneficiaries (ARSB), informal farmer groups, cooperatives established small shops
    - Women farmers (50%)
    - Micro-entrepreneurs
    - SAAD beneficiaries, or fitting same criteria on poverty, food security
    - Specific vulnerable groups (similar to SB beneficiaries) – while these groups are not necessarily the target group identified for support through linkages with social protection programs, they will be identified for support through linkages with social protection programs.
    - Willingness to commit to CRA enterprise development needs assessment, including willingness to participate in testing and rolling out new practices and implementation of CBA.

**For Activities 1.2.1, 2.1.2, 2.1.3**

- **Experience in farmer group learning, production and marketing**
- **Have at least 50% members in line with the project criteria for CRA**
- **Willingness to experiment CRA and develop CRA enterprises**

### Stage / Timing

**For Activities 2.1.3**

- **The selection of beneficiaries will take into due consideration specific environmental and social factors, including those gathered during the target municipalities during their selection.**

**FFC and KSP processes will not be carried out in areas where there are IP populations.**

**For Activities 1.2.1, 2.1.2, 2.1.3**

## Level Type: Related General Activities

### Criteria/Process

- The training and the respective individuals' responsibilities and roles will be defined.
- PAGASA and the DA will review the lists of nominated participants for each activity, taking into consideration the activity and the participant's responsibilities and expertise.

The following criteria will be used and tailored by the Executive Technical Committee (ETC) in consultation with the relevant Advisers:

- Job description and role/responsibilities are relevant to the project and topics of training and workshops.
- Relevant experience in the field of climate change, particularly for technical training and capacity building activities i.e. professionals from the public sector especially for career development facilitators.
- Women (50%).
- Young people.
- Willingness to commit time for training, knowledge sharing, and follow-up.
- Willingness to conduct training, facilitate for farmers/FO if necessary, and provide feedback on capacity building and career development facilitators.
- Willingness to contribute in the project learnings i.e. pre and post trainings/workshop assessments, baseline, mid and end evaluations.

| Rice - irrigated and rainfed | Drought resistant variety |  |  |  |  |

| Rice - irrigated and rainfed | Water harvesting technique | Dry periods | Yes | Luzon – RG and RZ and elsewhere | + environmental<br>+ social |
| Rice - upland | Alternate wetting and drying (AWD) Management System for Responsible Farming | Drought, heavy precipitation | Some |  | + environmental<br>+ social |
| Rice - upland | Drip irrigation | Heavy precipitation, flooding | Yes | All upland areas | + environmental<br>+ social |

| Corn - sloping to hilly lands | Agroforestry Nitrogen Stabilization System (AGNIS) | Drought and heavy precipitation | Yes | All upland areas | + environmental<br>+ social |

# Appendix 6 - Table of differences and complementarities between GCF projects in the Philippines

| FAD | SAF010 (Land Bank) | UNDP |
| --- | --- | --- |
| **Title** | Adapting Philippine Agriculture to Climate Change (tbd) | Multi-hazard impact-based forecasting and early warning system (MHI-FES) for the Philippines |
| **Budget** | Total 39 m, 26.3 m GCF; 8.3 m DA co-financing; 4 thm PADASA co-finance (indicative) | 10 m GCF; 10 cm co-financing; total 20 m (SAP) |
| **Project Duration** | 7 years (tbd: 2023 - 2030?); lifespan 20-30 years | 5 years (2020 to 2025); lifespan 10 years |
| **Focus (system)** | Agriculture systems, lowland and upland in major climate impact and agroecological systems in the country | Human life, and properties, notably buildings |
| **Focus (work area)** | Climate-resilient agriculture for different agriculture systems in Climate Change Adaptation | DRR; no Ag focus or other sectors. Focus on city administrations within Provinces |

  - Budget: 26 m GCF, 12 m co-financing, total 38 m

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 119

## FAO
- **Timescale of climate monitoring:** continuous monitoring and monthly operational agromet advisories
- **Project sites:** Provinces of Cotabato CAR (3), Lanao del Sur, Sulu, Basilan, Camarines Norte and Sur, Mindanao Bukidnon and N Solobat
- **Procurement 1 (met equipment only):** Agrimet sensors to existing weather stations; also using BSWIM stations (only for agrimet sensors). Standard sensors are not sufficient for agrimet sensors.
- **PAGASA Co-finance:** Weather stations and operation personnel of weather stations

## SAP010 (Land Bank)
- **Timescale of climate monitoring:** Early actions starting from a few days before tropical cyclone
- **Project sites:** Tuguegarao in Cagayan (wind and storm surge), Bontoc in Mountain Province (landslide, flood), Palo in Leyte (wind and storm surge), New Bataan in Davao del Sur (wind and landslide)
- **Procurement 1 (met equipment only):** AWS (particularly for heavy rainfall); tide stations; landslide monitoring device (no agrimet sensors).
- **PAGASA Co-finance:** No funding for PAGASA

## UNDP
- **Timescale of climate monitoring:** No temporally regular interventions, nor early actions for tropical cyclone
- **Project sites:** Eastern Seaboard: 3 regions: Bicol, Eastern Visayas, Caraga
- **Procurement 1 (met equipment only):** Nothing major for PAGASA
- **PAGASA Co-finance:** No overlaps on logistics of AWS as SAP010 invests only in Tuguegarao and Bontoc; no tide station; Palo in Leyte, New Bataan, and Davao del Sur.

## Possible overlapping/compatibility
- **FAO** and **SAP010 (Land Bank):** No overlapping foresight
- **UNDP:** Land Bank focuses on more vulnerable areas than SAP010. For example, Cagayan Region (Land Bank and UNDP) vs agriculture; FAO project chose the region based on CC vulnerability to agriculture and priority crops, while UNDP chose coastal communities only Bicol (UNDP) and FAO coastal vs farming communities in the coast may benefit from both projects.
- **FAO** and **PAGASA Co-finance:** No overlaps on logistics of AWS as SAP010 invests only in Tuguegarao and Bontoc; no tide station; Palo in Leyte, New Bataan, and Davao del Sur.

**Page 124**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 (PAGE 109)

## FAO

### Climatic hazards
- Drought, ENSO, heavy precipitation, floods, typhoons/high wind

### Knowledge management
- Use of short-term and long-term agrimet information for generating early warning systems for mainstreaming agriculture in planning at national level. Communication down to farmers.
- Harmonization with the sector, API, local climate warriors, meteorological services, enhanced ENSO alert warning system, only system with seasonal timescale

## SAP610 (Land Bank)

### Climatic hazards
- Flood, landslide, storm surge, and severe wind (most of them relatively are tropical cyclone-driven)

### Knowledge and decision support systems framework or Multi-hazard Early Warning System for Early Warning System (MWEWS) for coastal hazards, sub-seasonal product from CLIMPS may also be used in this project

## UNDP

### Climatic hazards
- Tropical cyclones, sea level rise, storm surges, flood

### Knowledge management system, decision support tool (DST), system for early warning and forecasting of coastal hazards. MWE of coastal hazards, NCFRM climate responsive vulnerability information system on climate scenarios and climate risks, and integrated risk information for decision makers

## Possible overlapping/compatibility

- Early warning and forecasting from Land Bank project may benefit FAO
- Better coastal CC resilience from UNDP project benefit FAO
- Potentially, FAO project is a connecting hub with Land Bank project and compatible with UNDP (CC vulnerability).

**Page 125**
---

| FAO | SAP10 (Land Bank) | UNDP |
|---|---|---|
| Ag, FS related climate activities and services | Agrometeorological advisories, more seamless agromet advisories across time scales including plant pest and disease alerts, crop yield levels | Climate-based agriculture services |

## National programs/projects

### Building upon, Complementing and Mainstreaming

1. **Philippine Agriculture and Fisheries Modernization Plan (PAFMP) 2020-2035**
   * Build capacity for use of climate information and agronomic services in AFNP planning
   * Enhance the resilience of fishing and farming communities and resilience of ecosystems
   * Enhanced access to climate information, advisory services, and financial services
   * Broader farmers and fishers access and participation in value chain

2. **Adaptation and Mitigation Initiatives in Agriculture (AMIA)**
   * Use CRIA projects as development future selection of target municipalities.
   * Complement with AMIA in selecting project municipalities and villages to engage
   * AMIA will provide training, capacity building, and technical assistance on CRIA innovations and services. The project will promote AMIA villages as hub for learning
   * Utilize AMIA developed climate-resilient fisheries investment prioritization framework
   * Enhance access to climate information, advisory services, and financial services
   * Farmers' adoption of CNA
   * Complement with AMIA in selecting project municipalities and villages to engage

## Sustainability rationale

* Training materials and capacities built through the project will be mainstreamed into national programmes and policies of the latter toward climate-resilient advisories and services to farmers.
* CSIs and CRIA mainstreamed into the AFNP/AMIA framework
* AMIF will support the establishment of financial institutions in target municipalities and villages, which will increase farmers' access and participation in value chain.

* National agricultural and CGI platforms developed by the GCF project will be mainstreamed into national sites and systems, including at AMIA / AMIA CREATE.
* The project will ensure that all CRIA products and services will become accessible to all areas of the country
* LGUs will either establish or improve their own CSIs and CRIA units
* AMIF will support the establishment of financial institutions in target municipalities and villages, which will increase farmers' access and participation in value chain.
* AMIA villages that provide knowledge and learning will be mainstreamed into national programmes and policies of the latter toward climate-resilient advisories and services to farmers.
* AMIA villages will continue operating on their own using their respective local resources.

### 3. Special Areas for Agricultural Development (SAAD)

- The SAAD is implemented in the poorest provinces of the Philippines with the objective to alleviate poverty through increased food production and productivity.
- SAAD areas provide poor and needy farmers and fishermen with the appropriate technology, financing, infrastructure, and services that will enable them to increase their income and improve their livelihoods and welfare. With this, SAAD areas were considered as one of the criteria in the project.

### 4. Philippine Rural Development Project (PRDP)

- The PRDP project will utilize and build on the PRDP’s tools such as geotagging, value chain analysis, and climate risk assessment to develop a comprehensive understanding of the CRRA profiles under AMA.
- To ascertain the climate resilience building needs of farmers and fishermen in the project areas, the PRDP project will conduct an assessment of the value-chain cluster, and climate-resilient agriculture and fisheries sector, including livestock.

### 5. Philippines Climate Change Adaptation Project (PHCCAP) funded by GCF

- The project, which ended in 2016, supported streamlining the climate change adaptation planning process at the local government unit level.
- PHCCAP has developed a set of tools and methodologies to support the implementation of climate change practices and technologies.

### 6. Agriculture lending and insurance

- The proposed GCF project will support the linkage of the beneficiaries to existing financial institutions, such as the Rural Bank of the Philippines, the Philippine Crop Insurance Corporation and the Agricultural Credit Policy Council, as well as local banks.
- National agricultural CGI and platforms and capabilities will be improved through the development of a national CGI platform and capacity building for farmers by farmers, particularly the poor and most vulnerable.

### GCF Project

- The GCF project will support PRDP’s climate investment plans and other relevant projects in the country to scale up PRDP activities to increase the benefits of climate change adaptation.
- The project will also support the implementation of the Climate Change Adaptation Financing (CCAF) USD 250M to scale up PRDP activities to increase the benefits of climate change adaptation, including the development and implementation of climate-smart economic recovery efforts by investing in rural infrastructure, enterprise development, and other livelihood opportunities.
- The project will also support the mainstreaming of PRDP planning approaches and tools across Department of Agriculture (DA) programs and projects.

### GCF Project

- GCF project will systematize the most fundamental climate change adaptation needs such as national agro-METIS platforms and sustain the improvement of the accuracy of weather forecasts.
- The project will also support the development of a national CGI platform to provide access to climate information and CRA options for practical use in local plans and projects, including the mainstreaming of CGI and CRA technologies and practices.

### GCF Project

- The project will also support the implementation of more possible applications and in the GCF project, model climate-resilient agriculture practices and technologies developed by PHCCAP.
- The project will also support the development of a national CGI platform to provide access to climate information and CRA options for practical use in local plans and projects, including the mainstreaming of CGI and CRA technologies and practices.

### GCF Project

- The project will also support the implementation of more possible applications and in the GCF project, model climate-resilient agriculture practices and technologies developed by PHCCAP.
