# Secretariat’s assessment of FP201

## Proposal name: Adapting Philippine Agriculture to Climate Change (APA)

### Accredited entity:
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country(ies):
(Oct) Philippines

### Project/programme size:
Small

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

### 1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

#### Strengths
- An innovative approach that complements the country’s efforts to develop climate-resilient agricultural systems.
- Aimed at vulnerable and rural populations, particularly smallholder farmers and women's groups.

#### Points of caution
- None

### 2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum X1 titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations."

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

#### 3. The Philippines is a country with high vulnerability to the impacts of climate change.
- Ranked fourth on the Long-Term Climate Risk Index (2008–2019), the country is continually exposed to extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones, droughts, floods, and irregular precipitation.
- Climate change has a negative impact on agricultural productivity, with smallholder farmers and rural populations in agricultural systems being increasingly exposed to climate risks and the losses and damages associated with extreme weather events.

#### 4. The objective of the project "Adapting Philippine Agriculture to Climate Change" (APA) is to increase the resilience of rural men and women in areas vulnerable to climate change who depend on agriculture for their livelihoods.
- This will be achieved through improving farmers' capacity to develop Climate Resilient Agriculture (CRA) enterprises and adopting financially sound practices, as well as increasing access to credit and other financial services. The project aims to build supporting systems for scaling up.

#### 5. The project aims to achieve the following three outcomes:
- None


---
**Page 138**
---

### Outcome (i): Increased institutional capacities for the development and provision of climate information and Climate Resilient Agriculture services

#### Component 1: Institutional Capacities for Climate Resilient Agriculture services development

- **Application**: This component aims to address the capacity gaps identified in implementing the Adaptation and Mitigation Initiative in Agriculture and other DA programmes. These gaps are in localized climate information services (LIS), tailored CRA technologies, agribusiness models, and institutional capacities.
- **Activities**: The activities will ensure PAGASA and DA collaboratively collect and analyze relevant climate and agricultural data to develop CRA products that can be used by farmers and others at appropriate times. The project will provide tools and training for farmers and others.

#### Component 2: Adoption of Climate Resilient Agriculture through enterprises by farmers (female and male) and other stakeholders

- **Activities**: These activities will ensure PAGASA and DA collaboratively collect and analyze relevant climate and agricultural data to develop CRA products that can be used by farmers and others at appropriate times. The project will provide tools and training for farmers and others.

#### Component 3: Strengthened regulatory framework, market and knowledge management for mainstreaming and scaling-up Climate Resilient Agriculture

- **Activities**: These activities will ensure PAGASA and DA collaboratively collect and analyze relevant climate and agricultural data to develop CRA products that can be used by farmers and others at appropriate times. The project will provide tools and training for farmers and others.

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

#### Project objective
The project objective will be achieved through three complementary outcomes that comprise its three components.


---
**Page 139**
---

### Activities under this Component will support communities vulnerable to climate change in improving their livelihoods and resilience through CRI adoption, while enhancing both their resilience and income-generation capacities.

Activities under this Component will support communities vulnerable to climate change in improving their livelihoods and resilience through CRI adoption, while enhancing both their resilience and income-generation capacities. As a result, farmers will be able to adapt to the effects of climate change by better managing their farms during extreme climatic situations and become economically sustainable by linking themselves to CRI investment opportunities and value chains. This Component will improve farmers’ access to climate information services (CIS) through the use of mobile phones, radio, television, and farm equipment, in order to reduce risks and losses, produce enough volume, attain economies of scale, ensure market access, and increase their income. The Component will also support sustainable and resilient clustered community-based entrepreneurs. With a special focus on women, Indigenous Peoples (IPs), and smallholder farmers, the Component will address structural and market barriers to CRI adoption with complementary building of CRI practices with value chain linkages. This will be achieved through the use of information and communications and mobile technologies in providing information and linking farmers vulnerable to climate change to CIS and CRI advisers and services. Another particular focus will be on improving access to credit for women, IPs, and smallholder farmers by clustering enterprises with local financial institutions such as Land Bank (the direct access entry), microfinance institutions (MFIs), and other formal and informal financial service providers. On the other hand, the project will provide these financial institutions with technical assistance in developing and implementing innovative agricultural insurance products that are supportive of CRI and accessible to smallholders, especially women and Indigenous peoples. The project will work through agencies such as the Microfinance Council of the Philippines, Cooperative Development Authority, Philippine Crop


---
**Page 140**
---

## Component 2: Mainstreaming Climate Resilient Agriculture

### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: N/A

- Direct beneficiaries of the project are a 250,000 vulnerable and resource-poor persons (254,896) living in 100 municipalities in 9 provinces of 5 administrative regions of the Philippines. These are mainly poor producer households whose livelihoods are derived primarily from crops and farming systems and that will be applying climate-resilient technologies and practices.
- This is equivalent to nearly half of the poor and over 18 per cent of the rural population of the 9 targeted provinces, which are among the most vulnerable in the country. The project financial analysis indicates that the direct beneficiaries represent about one-third of all households living below poverty line in the most vulnerable to climate change in the country.
- Furthermore, the farming system diversification efforts as part of CRA practices are expected to increase the availability of safe and nutritious food for both household consumption and the market.

### 18. The project will make important contributions to:

(a) Reduction emissions and increased resilience:
(b) Adaptation results area 2 - Vulnerable people and communities;
(c) Adaptation results area 3 - Health, well-being, food and water security; and
(d) Mitigation results area 4 - Forestry and land use;
(e) Enabling environment:


---
**Page 141**
---

## Core Indicator 5: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to strengthening institutional and regulatory frameworks for low emission resilient development pathways in a country-driven manner

### Core Indicator 6: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to technology generation, transfer and innovation

### Core Indicator 8: Degree to which GCF investments contribute to effective knowledge generation and learning processes, and use of good practices, methods and tools

In over 3 million people (50 per cent of whom are female) are expected to benefit indirectly from the project through improved access to climate-resilient agricultural activities in Component 3. These beneficiaries represent 1 million farming households that are living in the prioritized provinces and are highly sensitive to climate risks.

The number of indirect beneficiaries is estimated at over 20 million people, including farmers in the Philippines. Component 1 will develop and strengthen CIS and CRA services that will benefit not only the 9 priority provinces but also other provinces in the country. This component will also involve building the capacity of institutions to plan and provide support services for climate-resilient agricultural practices. The project will create an enabling environment that is more climate-informed and essential to widespread CRA adoption and the scaling up of low-emission agriculture.

The project will increase awareness among farmers, particularly women, about the importance of climate change adaptation and mitigation in their livelihoods. It will raise awareness of climate risks and the benefits of CIS and CRA practices, reaches farmers beyond the project's targeted provinces; at the same time, mainstreaming CRA measures into LGU priority programs and policies.

There are 10 million people living in high climate change-impact regions and 20 million more rural people in regions with high climate variability.

The project is also expected to achieve a reduction in greenhouse gas emissions of 4.38 Mt CO₂ over 20 years through the application of climate-resilient and low-emission agriculture practices on 25,000 hectares of land by 25,000 farmer households.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

The project aims to be a catalyst for a broader shift in agriculture across the Philippines, from its historical reliance on extreme vulnerability – in terms of high damages and losses from extreme climate events and low adaptive capacity of highly exposed farmers – to an alternative paradigm where farmers (and other stakeholders including local government units and communities) are able to understand and monitor short-term and longer-term climate change risks, as well as adopt appropriate adaptation measures that will reduce their exposure.

The proposed project will make crucial contributions to the process of transforming Philippine agriculture towards climate resilience - a clear indication of its potential to produce impact beyond the scope of the project. In addition to the above, the project will develop synergies and complementarities with other GCF projects addressing urban and coastal adaptation in the Philippines as well as other national and more holistic adaptation approach in the country.

There are seven key ways in which the project will achieve the proposed impact:

- Institutionalizing provision of CIS and CRA services;
- Strengthening extension and service delivery capacity;
- Developing new models for scaling up successful adaptation at scale;
- Mainstreaming CIS into core government services, programmes, planning and budget;
- Developing inclusive CRA supply chains and support;


---
**Page 142**
---

### Sustainable development potential

The project will contribute to climate change mitigation by reducing greenhouse gas emissions, primarily through alternate wetting and drying (AWD) technology for rice culture and also through improved water use efficiency in other crops.

The project will also deliver environmental co-benefits such as improved biodiversity for increased ecosystem resilience, enhanced soil fertility, the conservation of indigenous, stress-resistant varieties, and enhanced micro-watershed management. Soil quality, water quality and quantity, and overall land productivity will be improved by 20% (see annex 3). These practices will directly improve soil quality and contribute to water conservation and ecosystem benefits, including increased biodiversity and reduced erosion (Sustainable Development Goal 15). Where appropriate, these practices will be planned and executed in a wider watershed management context, with the relevant environment and water agencies and on a province-by-province basis.

Social co-benefits. Although the GCF funds will be used for climate change concerns, they will improve the livelihoods of indigenous peoples and women farmers, particularly the lack of support for very small-scale farmers, including indigenous farmers who are largely dependent on agriculture and associated natural resources. The project will contribute to reducing poverty by increasing access to markets and improving food security and nutrition. The main areas of socioeconomic activity for indigenous peoples are at the uplands, where they experience a high level of vulnerability due to their lack of access to education, health care, and nutritious food, as well as the availability of staples essential for their traditional diets; the project will contribute to reducing poverty by increasing access to markets.

The project will pay special attention to women and indigenous peoples, providing them with appropriate adaptation technologies and capacity-building activities. Recognizing that existing gender inequalities in rural areas are a major barrier to the empowerment of women producers in the Philippines, special training and approaches will be used to ensure greater equality between men and women in all aspects of the project. Women's groups traditionally play a significant role in agriculture.

The project will generate social benefits from the explicit inclusion of women in technical assistance activities and gender mainstreaming in CDA enterprise development. Special attention will be paid to ensuring trainers and project staff are trained on gender equality and social inclusion, including through the use of participatory approaches. The project will ensure that participatory processes consider the differentiated needs of women, including women from diverse ethnic groups (e.g., indigenous peoples) and those with specific vulnerabilities (e.g., access to trainings and information, time constraints). Gender-sensitivity will also be mainstreamed throughout training to encourage a more equitable distribution of domestic and care work. The project will ensure that the empowerment of women through increased knowledge and income will raise the development impact in the area of women's empowerment and gender equality. This is because their strengths are well integrated in rural agriculture in order to support its future.

The project is also expected to generate positive impacts for indigenous men and women, including improved access to land and other resources that they need to ensure their rights are safeguarded through the implementation of an Indigenous Peoples Plan (see


---
**Page 143**

### Economic

The overall objective of the project is to increase the climate change resilience of the most vulnerable households through developing CRA enterprises and to make adaptive investments in infrastructure and services that will be made based on the incremental income increases that vulnerable households will accrue upon adopting proven recommended agricultural practices.

#### 3.4 Needs of the recipient
Scale: N/A

- The project will target farmers and producers locally identified as groups most vulnerable due to climate-related disasters.
- Climate change-vulnerable provinces, largely in Mindanao and Visayas, often overlap with those that have the country's lowest human development index (HDI) scores or are located in low-income categories (LICs).
- General access to services and economic opportunities in these areas is often quite low. The archipelagic and mountainous nature of most regions reduces access to local services and the capacity of many small local government units to support local populations.

#### 3.5 Country ownership
Scale: N/A

- The project is fully aligned with and directly contributes to the DA's basic planning documents, including the National Agriculture Development Plan (NADP) and Industrialization Plan for 2020–2025 and the One DA Reform Agenda and its key strategies.

#### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness
Scale: N/A

- The project’s total proposed budget is estimated at USD 39.25 million, of which 66.9 per cent (USD 26.27 million) is proposed to be a GCF grant, and 33.1 per cent (USD 12.98 million) is the Philippines’ contribution.
- The project will directly benefit approximately 405,000 farmers and indirectly benefit about 1.25 million people in farming households and indirect benefits to 3.5 million people.
- The direct and indirect beneficiaries constitute roughly 1.2 per cent and 7 per cent, respectively, of the Philippine population.

#### Project’s proposed concessionality level
The project’s proposed concessionality level is rationalised through:
(a) the project's provision of concessional financing (i.e., a loan with interest rates lower than market data) to Philippine small-scale farmers in the selected weather-vulnerable agroecological zones; and
(b) the delivery to these farmers of goods that might be private by nature but in the long run contribute to their resilience, such as improved seeds, fertilisers, and other inputs that will help them adapt to climate change.

#### World Bank financing from GCF
Of total co-financing amount of USD 12.98 million, the co-financing ratio is 54:46.
- 63.7 per cent from the DA.
- 36.3 per cent from PAGASA.
- The Government of the Philippines, acting through the DA, will provide concessional financing to farmers in the form of credit from Land Bank (and other agricultural finance programmes).
- The potential leverage ratio is 1:20, meaning that for every USD 1 invested by the World Bank, it can mobilise up to USD 20.
- For the project’s 6,500 target farmer/SFA CRA enterprises would borrow at 40 per cent of the project investment amount. This represents a conservative estimate of the average loan per farmer/CRA enterprise and is well within the Land Bank's ceiling and lower than current borrowing rates, for example of the average 120,000 Philippine pesos by rice farmers and cooperatives.

### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

**38. Project background:** The project aims to increase the climate resilience of the most vulnerable farming households in the Philippines by building up the institutional capacities for delivering climate-resilient agriculture (CRA) enterprises, developing more resilient agricultural CRA enterprises, and strengthening the capacities of the government and the private sectors to build supporting systems for scaling up the CRA and CRA services. Among the key components are: (i) development of a national framework for CRA; (ii) establishment of farming and agroforestry systems, more effective use of indigenous, stress-tolerant varieties, and enhanced access to inputs and markets; (iii) improved livelihoods and food security while reducing poverty in vulnerable communities; and (iv) increased resilience among the most disadvantaged groups. The project will be delivered in at least nine provinces in five regions in the Philippines. It is expected that 100,000 farming households, including approximately 35,000 most vulnerable and resource-poor households to implement their CRA investment plans.

**39. Environmental and social risk category:** The accredited entity (AE) assigned the project to Category B under the GCF environmental and social risk categorization. Potential adverse impacts are considered in the manner described for Category A projects, but with a focus on areas of concern such as proximity to protected areas, and the involvement of indigenous peoples' communities, which are generally site-specific, largely reversible, and readily addressed through mitigation measures. The AE has identified no potential adverse environmental or social risks that would fall under Category B it will be supported under the project. The Secretariat confirms the environmental and social risk categorization and the corresponding category B environmental and social risk accreditation level.

**40. Safeguards instruments:** All provided an environmental and social management framework (ESMF) which identifies general environmental and social impacts and mitigation measures and opportunities to enhance positive impacts per the project components. As further details are developed, the AE will develop a more detailed ESMF that includes specific principles and procedures to mitigate impacts for implementation at subproject level. The ESMF includes an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) which is prepared by and requires that environmental and social risk management plans (ESMRPs) be prepared on the environment and social risks associated with each project component, in consultation with the AE's environmental and social safeguards that are aligned with GCF policy. The ESMF also establishes institutional arrangements, grievance redress mechanisms (GRM), and monitoring and reporting requirements to ensure effective implementation of environmental and social safeguards compliance. Among various safeguards instruments, the ESMF includes an ESMP template, biodiversity assessment, risk management plan, stakeholder engagement plans, community consultations and plan for continued stakeholder engagement, and an Indigenous Peoples' Planning and Implementation Plan.

**41. Compliance with the GCF environmental and social safeguards (ES) standards:** The paragraphs below describe the program's compliance with the GCF ES standards.

**42. Environmental management and assessment of environmental and social risks and impacts:** The project is consistent with the GCF against environmental, social and social policy and standards. The ESMF discussed the environment and social baseline at the previous stage of the project development process, identified potential adverse environmental conflicts and community health and safety, and identified the overall environmental and social impacts and mitigation measures per project components. ### ES2: Labour and working conditions

The project identified potential risks associated with rural employment, occupational safety and health (OSH) related to agricultural activities, child labour, and gender-based violence. The project proposes a risk matrix for each of these areas when supporting small-scale infrastructures; where conflicts in natural resources allocation are present, the project will work closely with local authorities and communities to address such issues during subproject screening. However, given that nearly all the regions targeted by the project have already been identified as high-risk areas for child labour, gender-based violence, and OSH, the project will conduct conflict-sensitivity assessments at subproject level to avoid inducing or exacerbating existing risks.

#### Specific measures

- **Rural employment**: The project proposes specific measures to address the risk of child labour in rural employment. These include ensuring that children are not involved in work harmful to their health and safety; identifying and addressing significant OSH issues, which are included in the non-eligibility list of the project's ESMP.
- **Occupational safety and health (OSH)**: The project environmental and social screening checklist provides further details on activities risking exposure to hazardous substances or conditions. These include the use of pesticides by smallholder producers and other vulnerable informal agricultural workers; and high levels of "working poverty" in rural areas, which can lead to unsafe working practices and low productivity.
- **Gender-based violence**: The project proposes specific measures to address gender-based violence, including ensuring that women are not involved in work harmful to their health or safety. These include identifying and addressing significant OSH issues; and introducing complementary measures such as training for agricultural extension workers on gender-responsive OSH measures, complementary measures will be introduced at the subproject level.
- **Child labour**: The project proposes specific measures to address child labour, including ensuring that children are not involved in work harmful to their health or safety. These include identifying and addressing significant OSH issues; and introducing complementary measures such as training for agricultural extension workers on gender-responsive OSH measures.

### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention

The project is expected to improve the natural resources and agricultural land following the CBA practices and the adoption of climate-resilient agricultural practices that promote sustainable management practices. However, increased agriculture productivity may lead to increased use of pesticides and fertilizers. The project will provide guidance on the use of certified or unregistered seeds where farmers lack the information and knowledge and generate indicators for tracking the use of such seeds.

### ESS 4: Community health, safety, and security

Community practices to be promoted by the project will improve farming practices and support better functioning ecosystems, the human well-being, and food security. The project will promote economically profitable and financially viable CBA measures and increased institutional support on scaling up and implementing them. It is important to note that the use of pesticides and fertilizers in agricultural inputs on off-farms and agricultural production can generate adverse impacts on the health and safety of farmers and their families, as well as the environment.

### ESS 5: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The land tenure analysis conducted by the AIF as part of the ESF concluded that the land tenure system is highly fragmented. This fragmentation creates a number of barriers to sustainable land management which hinders farmers from taking a long-term view on sustainable land management. The project will prohibit activities that may temporarily remove people from their homes, means of production and livelihood or restrict access to their means of livelihood cannot be initiated as they may produce loss or damage of assets or income for local communities are considered high-risk.


---
**Page 147**
---

### ES 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The ESMIF triggers the AE’s relevant safeguards as the project may work with communities in the provinces of Hainan, Guangdong, Fujian, and Zhejiang to include activities affecting plant genetic resources. One of the project provisions, Hainan, is home to the rice terraces of the Hani people, which are a UNESCO World Heritage Site. Given its unique value as an internationally important agricultural heritage system and for its biodiversity conservation potential, the project will not allow any genetically modified organisms (GMOs) or other activities of any kind within natural habitats and existing or proposed protected areas or their buffer zones. Activities that may have adverse impacts on plant genetic resources will operate in or source from farms in protected areas or their buffer zones. Activities near sensitive and valuable ecosystems, protected areas and habitat of endangered species, near watercourses, aquifers, and other critical ecological features are prohibited. Areas containing GMOs or areas containing valuable resources will be considered high-risk for exclusion. The project will only use certified organic seeds and varieties that are not genetically modified organisms (GMOs) and are registered. The project will identify the specific varieties and crops in collaboration with competent authorities, local communities, and other stakeholders.

### ES 7: Indigenous Peoples Policy and Sub-ES 7: Indigenous peoples

With indigenous peoples (IPs) present in the country, an IPPP has been provided in Chapter 4 of the ESMIF, as well as guidance on how to implement it. The IPPP will exclude activities that would “likely create adverse impacts on indigenous peoples (IPs) and/or their potential for self-determination.” IPs are defined as individuals or groups living in a group’s IP homogenous village or a village of mixed ethnic/IP composition. The IPPP contains information on screening and the steps and process for FPC. Indigenous women are considered to be particularly vulnerable, and the project will ensure that they participate fully with communities. The AE has incorporated recommendations that include continuation of traditional practices and customary laws and practices, as well as recognition and respect for tenure systems where relevant and requested by IPs. Two-way learning processes between extension officers and IPs will be established in collaboration with local IP leaders and facilitators.

In terms of implementation, recommendations include IP representation within the project’s decision-making bodies including the national decision-making and oversight body, and


---
**Page 148**
---

### Ensuring IP Expertise in Project Implementation

The AE will trigger its relevant subgrantor given that agricultural landscapes are considered as cultural heritage sites used by indigenous beneficiaries. The ESFM prohibits the project involving sacred grounds, burial sites, cultural landscapes, and other areas of cultural significance for indigenous peoples.

Activities risking any damage or loss to cultural property, including sites having archaeological potential, are prohibited unless a change in procedures is followed. Such activities will not be eligible. The ESFM specifies that the change find procedures are to be followed by all third-party contractors if any cultural resources, such as sacred grounds, burial sites, graveyards and/or ancestral graves, are discovered during the project implementation.

Subprojects will be screened for the presence of cultural resources and potential impacts on physical or spiritual aspects of indigenous peoples. The ESFM requires that a cultural resource impact assessment is conducted in all cases where cultural resources are present within the project area. In case of the presence of cultural resources, the ESFM requires that due diligence must be undertaken to ensure that the project complies with relevant national laws and regulations, including those of the indigenous peoples' own governing body. The ESFM also requires that a cultural resource impact assessment is conducted in all cases where archaeological heritage that are significant to indigenous peoples.

In cases where there is a high chance of encountering cultural resources, the ESFM requires that a cultural resource impact assessment is conducted at the project level and contracts for any civil works must refer to the need to include recovery of "chance findings" in line with national procedures and rules. In addition, the project Indigenous Peoples Plan also requires that a cultural resource impact assessment be conducted at the project level or at the subproject level.

### Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) Safeguarding

The revised GCF Environmental and Social Policy adopted by decision B.8M-2021/18 requires safeguarding against sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment (SEAH) in GCF-financed activities. Due to the high prevalence of gender-based violence (GBV), including sexual violence, in the project has incorporated screening and management of SEAH-related potential risks and impacts into its ESFM. The project will also ensure that all project staff are trained on SEAH. This will also involve assessing the level of risks related with SEAH. Violence against women, including GBV, is a priority area for the project. The project will screen and manage GBV risks in the gender action plan. Alignment of gender and safeguard policies within the project has been also analysed and leveraging opportunities have been identified under the project.

In particular, the project's grievance redress mechanism (GRM) will be accessible for all project stakeholders to report incidents of sexual harassment or abuse. The project is committed to having a survivor-centred and gender-responsive GRM and will have specific procedures for SEAH complaints. The project will ensure that the GRM is implemented by the Project Management unit and Regional Project Offices will be responsible for managing the GRM which has a strong link with FAO Philippines personnel to ensure the right application of GRM principles and practices. The project will also ensure that the GRM is integrated into the existing FAO-GRM system, which will also be strengthened to include a procedure for SEAH so that it is in line with the GCF policy on safeguarding against sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment.

The pathways will be established and operationalized under the project in collaboration with the Union of Indigenous Peoples (UIP) and other relevant stakeholders. The project will provide support, legal and social/reintegration support. Regarding land tenure, conflict and other health and safety risks, the project will ensure that all information on these issues is made available and accessible to all project affected persons, and special attention will be paid to SEAH.

## Implementation arrangements

The AEF will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including all aspects of project appraisal, administrative, financial and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation, project closure and evaluation. While a dedicated Project Management Office (PMO) will be established along with the establishment of the EEF to manage the day-to-day operations of the project itself, the PMO will be supported by a National Safeguard Specialist, National Gender, Indigenous Peoples, and Environment focal points.

A technical assistance team established by the AEF will also collectively ensure the operationalization of the safeguard measures. A detailed environmental and social risk assessment (ESRA) was conducted prior to their implementation, and ESMPs and IP plans will be developed to mitigate any medium-level risks that may arise during project implementation. The EEF will provide regular monitoring and reporting on the safeguards implementation on all project activities and adherence at the field level. Where needed, additional environmental and social specialists will be mobilized and resources allocated for this purpose.

The AEF will also ensure that safeguard specialists will provide trainings on screening, training, reporting and GRI at subproject levels.
### Project appraisal

The PMO established by the AEF will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including: (i) all aspects of project appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation to ensure that the project is implemented in a timely manner and achieve objectives; (iii) ensuring the quality of project monitoring, as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting on project progress. The PMO will also be responsible for preparing annual work plans in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the accreditation master agreement (AMA).

### The EEF

The EEF is a FAO and the Government of the Philippines acting through the Philippine Department of Agriculture (DA), the Philippine Atmospheric, Geophysical and Astronomical Services Administration (PAGASA) and the Bureau of Agricultural Research (BAR). The DA is a government agency responsible for the promotion of agricultural development by providing the policy framework and enabling environment to support the growth of farmer-oriented business enterprises. PAGASA was established on 8 December 1972, with a mandate to provide meteorological services in weather forecasting, climate monitoring, disaster risk reduction and economic security of all, as well as to promote national progress by undertaking scientific and technological services in meteorology, hydrology, climatology, astronomy, and other geophysical sciences. BAR is the research arm of DA and is responsible for the Philippine Science Institutes of the Department of Science and Technology (DST). It is a national government agency funded through the annual budget appropriation from the DA and other sources of government funds. BIT manages the cash resources of the Government, performs banking functions in relation to the management of public financial transactions, and acts as custodian of the financial transactions of all national secretaries, bureaus, agencies and instrumentalities.

As an EEFAO, through its representation in Philippines, will deliver technical assistance and capacity building services to the project team. The EEF will also provide access to comparative advantage and in-house experts to ensure that the project achieves its desired


---
**Page 150**
---

results are effectively equip the Government and stakeholders to sustain the process of CBA transformation introduced by the project.

### Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

The project has been developed based on a wide range of consultations with stakeholders including national government agencies, international organizations (IOs), civil society organizations (CSOs), UN agencies and international organizations at national and local levels. To better understand the local needs and priorities, several rounds of stakeholder engagement were conducted in 2015-2016. These were conducted with farmers, women (including women farmers), CSOs, groups, and religious and ethnic minorities. The project has also been developed based on a series of public consultations held in 2017. The GCM summarizes current results of public consultation and provides guidance and timelines for stakeholder engagement activities required under each sub-component. The stakeholder engagement plan and plan for continued engagement will as well as regularly monitoring and reporting, required for all site-specific ECMs. The project will disclose all safeguard instruments (SIs) and related documents in a clear and accessible manner on the website, both in English and local languages, and for moderate-risk subprojects 30 days prior to endorsement.

### Grievance redress mechanism (GRM)

The AE will establish a project-level GRM that will ensure access to justice through traditional mechanisms, provide timely grievance redress and avoid potential religious discrimination. In addition to the project-level GRM, the AE will also establish a site-specific GRM for each sub-component. The project-level GRM will be established at the Office of the Inspector-General and the GCM Independent Redress Mechanism. The AE will ensure that all grievances received under this mechanism are documented and reported as part of the safeguards performance monitoring conducted by the IG. In addition, a dedicated grievance redress officer will be available to provide assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made.

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF gender policy. The gender assessment included the sector-specific analysis of gender equality issues in the project area, as well as legal and policy frameworks around gender-related laws (legal, administrative and supervisory framework, etc.). It also identified key gender equality issues that could be addressed in particular: division of agricultural labor; risks opportunities where the project could find leverage, among others. It provided the key recommendations for empowering women and overall improving gender equality in the project area. The gender action plan addresses the gender issues around indigenous peoples and youth as these groups also represent important elements of the Philippine context. The AE has identified the specific challenges and barriers to gender equality that still exist among these subgroups and the complexities related to the egalitarian nature of indigenous communities.

The GCM submitted with the proposal contains activities aligned with those of the project, performance indicators, baseline, sex-disaggregated targets, timelines and responsibilities for each sub-component. The AE will ensure that all site-specific ECMs are reached upon indigenous peoples and youth, while maintaining the primary focus on women.

### Mainstreaming gender equality and the empowerment of women and indigenous peoples

The AE has identified a number of activities to mainstream gender equality in the project, and activities that include events, as well as development of products. The participation angle aims


---
**Page 151**
---

To ensure that the proportion of participants from each of the three groups is roughly equal to the relative population sizes at national level: 10 per cent for women, 12.5 per cent and 25 per cent for indigenous persons, and youths, respectively. In some project areas, such as the Cordillera Administrative Region (CAR), the proportion will be adjusted accordingly. The remaining ten per cent of the time involved will be devoted to the importance of mainstreaming. As the foundation of GCR, mainstreaming is a process that ensures that all aspects of development are inclusive and equitable for all groups. This includes events and products, as well as indigenous knowledge and systems. This is intended to encourage citizen monitoring in accordance with the Guidelines on Research and Documentation of Indigenous Knowledge Systems (2013). The guidelines were developed based on the Indigenous Peoples’ Rights Act laws produced in 2012 by the National Commission on Indigenous Peoples. Water management, a prominent issue for indigenous peoples, will be addressed through the establishment of Farmer Field Schools for indigenous peoples, if aligned with the Ancestral Domain Sustainable Development and Protection Program (ADSDPP).

The GCR has been developed as part project components. For Component 1, on institutional capacities for CRA services development, gender activities revolve around targeting women and girls in CRA enterprises and investments. This is done by ensuring that they are empowered and capacitated to be part of the enhanced institutional capacities for GIS and CRA services at national level, contributing to the GCR platform/Regional and Provincial Centres, and to become a key player in the development of gender-sensitive and gender-responsive CRA enterprises and investments. The expected gender outcome is women’s increased access to and control over land and other productive resources, as well as their capacities on climate change and climate-resilient agriculture, facilitating female entrepreneurship and increasing their participation in the design, implementation, and services, and leveraging gender equity advancements in the Philippines while addressing specific gender issues.

Component 2, CRA enterprise development, aims to strengthen capacities of farmers to develop CRA enterprises and adopt economically viable and financially sustainable CRA practices. The expected gender outcome is increased economic resilience of women, especially indigenous women, through their participation in CRA enterprises and investments; gender-sensitive and gender-responsive CRA enterprise plans and investments; and 2 women and girl-led CRA enterprises. This will be achieved by targeting women-headed households in CRA investment planning for special groups, leveraging social protection.

Component 3 aims to provide an enabling environment for mainstreaming GCR and to raise awareness of the importance of GCR at national level, including municipalities/provinces/regions. This goal is supported by strengthened capacities of government officials, financial institutions, and other stakeholders to implement policies and programmes and to monitor CRA implementation. This will apply to the private sector’s use of GIS/CRA services to support CRA value chains; and to the capacity of banks and financial institutions to provide credit for CRA enterprises and investments, as well as CRA-related financial products. The expected gender outcome is that women from deprived backgrounds are capacitated in the design, implementation, and services of GCR information, education and communication materials and campaigns, national and local policies and programmes, and the development of CRA enterprises and investments; and access to CRA-related financial products that improve women’s access to finance and enable sustainable CRA adoption at scale.

The day-to-day implementation of the GCR for the project will be led by the National Gender and Social Inclusion Specialist, who will be recruited by the project. The specialist will work as part of the PMO and in close collaboration with the Regional Project Officer. The

### Risks

#### Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

- The total financing is USD 39,254,600 of which USD 10,267,273,510 is GCF grant financed.
- Co-financing is a combination of grant and in-kind contribution totaling USD 12,869,940 of which USD 10,266,400 provided by the DA, of which USD 4,714,540 go to PAGASA. In addition, the DA will provide USD 3,000,000 in-kind contribution.
- The co-financing ratio is 1:49 and 16 per cent of total financing (including parallel finance).

#### Accredited entity/executing entity's capability to execute the current project (low risk)

- FAO will act in its capacity as the DA, and also EE. FAD is a agency in the United Nations system that is specifically involved in agriculture issues.
- FAD has an extensive track record in the implementation of projects in developing countries and GCF projects.

#### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

- Co-financing (including parallel finance) will be provided ultimately by the government of Philippines through DA and PAGASA. Given that the co-financing amount is substantial, implementation may be impeded in the event co-financing is not released.
- Most of the co-financing will be disbursed in 2018/2019, which could delay project execution.
- The Secretariat derives comfort from the fact that the Government of the Philippines (rated Baa2 stable, unchanged since 2014) was characterized by moderate government debt levels and debt affordability. However, this is subject to the country’s economic performance, a strengthening fiscal position and limited vulnerability to external shocks.
- Co-financing will be disbursed in two tranches, with the second tranche being released only after the first tranche has been fully utilized.

- Capacity-building and technology transfer activities represent half of the total GCF funding requested and may not result in reaching expected results. Execution delays could occur due to capacity constraints or lack of availability of resources for these activities.
- Another risk factor concerns the programme approach for Philippine C4A transformation project. The Secretariat is concerned about the potential environmental and climate impact beyond this project. There are close links to another proposed GCF project in the pipeline, “Accelerating adoption of climate-resilient agricultural production through improved access to credit and financial services.” A similar project has been developed with the Land Bank of the Philippines (Land Bank) as the GCF direct access entity.
- However, these risks are mitigated, as the climate impact potential of this project does not

The enterprise risk management unit at FAO headquarters monitors compliance with fraud prevention and risk management policies. FAO confirmed that it manages risks on an extensive and ongoing basis through its internal control framework, which includes the FPP.

#### 4.4 Fiduciary

- The Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) will serve as the accredited entity (AE) for the project. FAO will co-work with the Government of the Philippines acting through the Philippine Department of Agriculture (PDOA), the Bureau of Animal Industry and Aquatic Services Administration (PAGASA) and the Philippine Bureau of the Treasury (BTF) as the executing entities.
- FAO will be the AE responsible for the overall management of the project, including: (i) all aspects of project appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial, and technical oversight and supervision over the implementation of the project activities funded by GCF proceeds. FAO will accomplish these tasks in accordance with the detailed provisions outlined in the accreditation master agreement (MMA) between FAO and GCF.
- As the AE, FAO, PDOA, PAGASA, and BTF, together with their respective entities, will deliver technical assistance and capacity-building to complement the activities of DA and PAGASA. On the other hand, the PDOA, PAGASA, and BTF will be responsible for the overall management and execution of the project activities funded by GCF proceeds.
- FAO and PDOA will also be responsible for executing their co-financing activities. FAO, DA and PAGASA are separate legal entities belonging to different government departments and therefore have a degree of operational autonomy.

- GCF proceeds received by FAO in its capacity as AE will flow to FAO or EE as well as to the TTF, after being passed through BYT and approval of the identified project activities.
- IFIs as a counterpart (within the framework of the Bipartite Agreement) are the principal custodian of all national government funds, including the resources of this project, as received from DA. FAO informed GCF that the IFI has approval authority on disbursements from FAO to the TTF.

The AMA was signed with the AE on 8 June 2018, and it became effective on 4 October 2018.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of the Philippines, a country where GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, GCF’s ability to sue for damages or seek compensation for any losses it may suffer need to be further assessed. The Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of the Philippines on 19 March 2022. The final negotiations in this regard were the Delegation Meeting held on 26 April 2022 and the Secretariat submitting the package of privileges and immunities documents to the delegation.

The GCF Secretariat’s Legal and Compliance Independent Unit has both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their riders activities and/or investigations without appropriate privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursement by GCF are made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP201

## Proposal name: Adapting Philippine Agriculture to Climate Change (APA)

### Accredited entity:
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country/[ies]:
(The) Philippines

### Project/programme size:
Small

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential

This funding proposal for a small project in environmental and social safeguards category was submitted to the Government of the Philippines as an executing project with both adaptation (80 per cent) and mitigation (20 per cent) impact. The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) is the accredited entity (AE) and one of the four examining entities (EEs). The other EE is the Department of Environment and Natural Resources (DENR), the Philippine Atmospheric, Geophysical and Astronomical Services Administration (PAGASA), and the Philippine Department of Agriculture (DA).

The project was subject to a number of adjustments to the funding proposal package which meant that it was not submitted for B.14 but for B.20. The total amount requested is USD 37.5 million, of which USD 27.1 million is GCF finance, and USD 10.4 million is co-finance from the Government of the Philippines. The project also includes parallel financing in the form of a loan from the Asian Development Bank (ADB) for USD 10.63 million.

The project builds on GEF SAP910, which targets early warning systems for urban cyclones, expanding the agrometeorological capacity of PAGASA, with the two initiatives complementing each other and providing a more comprehensive approach to early warnings.

The project addresses the high vulnerability of the Philippines (ranked fourth globally in terms of long-term climate risk) is increasingly frequent and severe extreme weather events. Disaster risk levels rise with the occurrence of more intense tropical storms, including heavy rainfall, flooding, landslides, and storm surges. The country's complex agroecological zones. Climate model projections indicate that large parts of the country are expected to experience a decrease in mean annual precipitation by 2050. Some regions are expected to experience drier long dry seasons and wetter rainy seasons, whereas droughts will become more frequent and intense in some areas.

Projections for cyclones are less certain but suggest an increase in intensity (rather than frequency) of tropical storms. The Philippines is also vulnerable to other climate-related hazards and climate-related disasters, which have a direct impact on the food security of rural households and the wider local economy.

The objective of this project is to adapt Philippine Agriculture to Climate Change (APA) is to increase the resilience of people who live in rural areas vulnerable to climate change and extreme weather events. The project will contribute to reducing vulnerability at the country's agricultural system towards climate resilience. This will be achieved through improving farmers' capacities to utilize climate information systems (CIS), to develop climate-resilient agriculture (CRA) enterprises, and to adopt financially and economically viable CRA.


---
**Page 160**
---

### Project Overview

The project aims to achieve the following three outcomes:

- **Outcome (a): Increased institutional capacities for the development and provision of climate-resilient agricultural practices**
- **Outcome (b): Adoption of CRA through enterprises by farmers (female & male)**
- **Outcome (c): Strengthened regulatory framework, markets, and knowledge management for mainstreaming and scaling up CRA**

### Project Context

The project will support institutional capacity building, regulatory frameworks, and financing mechanisms for CRA at the national level. The project support will be delivered on the ground by a consortium of local partners in 10 provinces across four representative regions (out of 18 in the country), across the four out of six agroclimatic zones expected to experience the most severe climate change impacts, as follows:

- **Northern Philippines**: Increase in heavy precipitation and tropical cyclones; increase in temperature damage and droughts from the combined effects of extreme weather events.
- **Eastern Seaboard**: Increase in heavy precipitation and tropical cyclones; increase in wind damage and likelihood of cyclone-related floods; likelihood of storm surges.
- **Western and Central Mindanao**: Increase in temperature and reduced precipitation, increased frequency of heatwaves; increase in heavy precipitation and lower soil moisture, with cyclones potentially hitting new areas.

### Requirements for Scaling Up

The project will support the scaling up of climate-resilient agricultural (CRA) systems, supported by financial and technical partners. The Philippines experiences a number of barriers to wide-scale adoption of CRA, in the form of (1) weak and incomplete localized climate information services; (2) limited access to financing for CRA investments; (3) fragmented programmes, budgets, extension; (4) limited systems for extension and outreach on agriculture and climate change; (5) lack of incentives for farmers to adopt CRA practices; (6) limited access to finance and risk-mitigation tools; and (7) compounded shocks of climate disruption and greater disadvantages among marginalized groups.

### Adaptation Impact

The project is expected to have significant adaptation impact in contributing to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals, including increased resilience of vulnerable people and communities; and adaptation results area 2 - increased resilience of health, web, and energy sectors.


---
**Page 161**
---

### GCF/B.35/02/Add.8/Rev.01 Page 101

facilitating on-farm learning to adopt CRA practices from the newly established CRA enterprises (covering 205,000 farmers). Increasing farmers'/farmer groups' access to finance and leveraging support from other programmes will be a focus for both target groups. These farmers/farmer groups will also benefit from the establishment of climate-resilient facilities in the 100 municipalities. Applying climate-resilient technologies and developing CRA enterprises will enable these farmers/farmer groups to sustainably increase their crop yields, crop diversity, incomes and household nutritional status.

Over 3 million people (50 per cent of whom are women) are expected to benefit. Indirect benefits include the creation of employment opportunities in the agricultural sector as well as services in component 3. These beneficiaries represent 1 million farming households who are living in the priority provinces identified by the government. The project is estimated that this intervention will be expected to represent about 5 per cent of the total population of the Philippines.

Component I will strengthen and integrate CLA and CRA services that will benefit not only the nine prioritized provinces, but also other provinces where climate change adaptation and mitigation are needed. This component will also involve building the capacity of institutions to plan and provide support services for farmers/farmer groups in the priority provinces. The project will also contribute towards the sustained use of GCF Component 3 particularly the activity to heighten awareness of climate risks and vulnerabilities, including through the establishment of a Climate Change Adaptation Network in targeted provinces; and mainstreaming CRA into national and local government unit (LGU) programmes could have long-term benefits.

Based on research by FAO, and the International Center for Tropical Agriculture, and further cost-benefit analysis, a range of CRA options were identified, which those piloted in the project area. These include intercropping with legumes, sorghum, or pigeonpea; using CRA options involving optimal combinations of intercropping and rotation, drought resilient and short-cycle crops; and intercropping with legumes, sorghum, or pigeonpea. All these options will be used for the MFA project, offering both climate change adaptation and mitigation benefits.

Mitigation impact: The project is expected to achieve a reduction in greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions of 250,000 tonnes CO₂e per year through the application of CRA practices over 29 years through the application of these climate-resilient and low-emission agriculture practices on 250,000 hectares of land by 2041.

GHG emission reductions/gains and the resulting carbon balance from proposed CRA intervention packages were assessed using FAO's Ex-Ante Carbon-balance Tool (Ex-ACT) for a 29-year period. The estimated GHG emissions reductions/gains and the resulting carbon balances were estimated by applying the tool for each of the project's seven CRA intervention packages, which include intercropping with legumes, sorghum or pigeonpea; rotation; and drying). The emission factors used were evaluated against default values in the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories (2006 IPCC Guidelines) and published scientific literature. The results are presented as a range of emissions reductions/gains from the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP).

The project also considers other important non-CO₂ emission factors in the GCF calculations follow the 2006 IPCC Guidelines, and that some of these have been updated in the 2019 Refinement to the 2006 IPCC Guidelines for National Greenhouse Gas Inventories. However, using the default values from the 2006 IPCC Guidelines, the project is expected to achieve emission reductions compared with the 2019 Refinement details. The expected emission reductions presented in this document are based on the default values and do not include any potential nitrous oxide reductions from the intercropping of coffee and peanuts). In response to questions from the independent TAP, it was confirmed that additional emission reductions may be achieved when updating the GHG baseline.

The independent TAP notes that neither the funding proposal nor its annex 16 (explaining the GHG emission reduction calculations) indicate how monitoring, reporting and

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

The project seeks to catalyze a paradigm shift in agricultural production from its baseline state characterized by vulnerability to losses and damage from extreme weather events, and by the low adaptive capacity of highly exposed farmers. In an alternative paradigm, smallholder farmers (and other stakeholders) are able to understand and monitor short-term and long-term climate risks and engage in a continuous process of managing these evolving risks. This requires target farming communities to be organized and to develop enterprises to sustain OAAs practices and achieve economies of scale.

Given this bringing about shift is the project’s goal to make a business case for reorienting existing investments into investment in APA and other climate-smart farming agriculture in the Philippines. The project’s exit strategy supposes that the financial model for


---
**Page 163**
---

### CRA enterprises can be replicated by the DA

In answer to a query from the independent TAP about how realistic this is, given the cost of the intervention being piloted through the project, involving a subsidy of USD 375 per farmer, which would cost USD 1.9 billion to roll out to five million farmers, the TAP noted that the DA has sufficient budgetary capacity to replicate the project model in the ability of the DA to replicate the project model: "The Government would have sufficient budget to replicate this model." The TAP also noted that the Department of Agriculture's [i.e. flagship programmes] budget in 2022 was USD 436 million for the whole country, targeting a large number of farmers and other stakeholders. This includes the following key budget allocations (in 2022) such as those for agricultural machineries, equipment, facilities at USD 110 million, small-scale irrigation projects USD 159 million, and for its Agriculture Modernization and Industrialization Fund USD 378 million to support the modernization of production systems and practices.

Currently, the project also aims to crowd in diverse commercial and development finance, particularly through increased access to credit by farmer groups, made possible by the enterprise model with enhanced business capacity and group collateral. The enterprise development model is seen as a key component of the DA's overall strategy for a sustainable solution to financing CRA, since enterprises should become profitable within a few years, requiring gradually less financial support from the DA.

Central to this model, however, are two assumptions which the independent TAP considers may well be flawed, limiting the project’s potential to bring about a fundamental paradigm shift in the delivery of agricultural extension services. The first assumption concerns agrometeorological information and crop modelling that is well packaged to advise farmers on appropriate planting dates and other agronomic practices. This is seen as an important solution to financing CRA, since enterprises should become profitable within a few years, requiring gradually less financial support from the DA.

The second assumption which may not hold is that produce from new climate-realistic agricultural systems will be able to command premium prices in the market, thereby encouraging investment in CRA practices. The independent TAP queried with the AE the strong emphasis in the funding proposal on strengthening upstream supply chain linkages, while appearing to

1.3 Sustainable development potential

The project contributes a wide range of co-benefits that enhance its sustainable development potential.

20. **Benefit**: The project will generate social benefits from the explicit inclusion of women in technical training, CRA demonstration plots and CRA enterprise development, with additional attention to gender equality and social inclusion. This will lead to a significant role in agriculture. Physical safety and improvement of nutritional status will also be fostered through the provision of food security for vulnerable groups such as women farmers, who are among the most vulnerable. In times of food shortages, women are the last to eat, as they tend to have less access to food.

21. **Special attention**: Will be paid to ensuring trainers and project staff are trained on gender equality and social inclusion, as well as preventing/dealing with sexual exploitation, abuse and harassment in the field. The project will also ensure that women from diverse indigenous and ethnic groups, and potential constraints they may face, are included in all aspects of the project. Gender sensitivity and awareness will be mainstreamed throughout training to encourage a more equitable management of resources and care work, further strengthening gender equality within the agriculture sector. Incentives for women’s participation in agricultural activities will also be increased in the area of women's empowerment.

22. **Environmental co-benefits**: The funding proposal states that the project will deliver environmental co-benefits such as improved biodiversity (by increasing integrated farming and agroforestry systems), more effective use of indigenous, stress-resistant varieties, and enhanced maize-wheat intercropping practices. These practices will also contribute to soil conservation and water management.

23. **Adaptive technologies**: And agricultural practices will directly improve soil quality and contribute to the reduction in greenhouse gas emissions by increasing the use of organic inputs and more integrated agricultural systems. Where appropriate, these practices will be planned and executed with the support of relevant government agencies, such as the forestry environment and water agencies and on a province-by-province basis.

24. **Socioeconomic benefits**: The project will contribute to improving livelihoods and food security for small-scale producers in rural communities. Farming returns diversification efforts are expected to increase the availability of safe and nutritious food for both households and markets, while also increasing income from agricultural activities for farmer organizations for climate-resilient and low-emission production, improved basic processing technologies, and increased access to markets through value chain development and enterprise development.

25. **Project impact**: The project adopts the key elements of sustainable agri-food system transformation that are highly likely to significantly reduce poverty, hunger, and malnutrition in the target communities. The project’s economic impact has been made on the basis of the incremental income increases that vulnerable households will receive from increased agricultural productivity.

26. **Equity and land**: The independent TAP noted that the funding proposal mentions that adverse social impacts could arise from “social inequality and elite capture” and project Risk Factor 5. This risk factor is considered high, as it may undermine incentives for small-scale agricultural producers (who may rent the land they farm).


---

---

or have limited tenure) to adopt CRA practices. In addition, it was observed that the indigenous people’s planning framework (in annex 6 of the funding proposal) reports a perception that DA programmes “reached” the “rich farmers” or the farmers connected with the local government and other powerful groups, while not reaching the poorest and most vulnerable farmers, IP [i.e. indigenous] farmers, and/or IP women farmers.”


The independent TAP asked the AE whether these issues have been fully taken into account in the project design. The AE responded that all stakeholders from the target sector in the Philippines have been consulted on the project design. In response, the AE acknowledged that the lack of access to land is a major constraint for small-scale farmers and IP women farmers, their choice and diminishing their capacity to invest in improving their living environment and livelihoods, and that this issue has been addressed through the project.


The AE stated that: “the Project recognises the relevance of small-scale farmers to apply CRA because of the fear to lose access to land when it is rented or when they have internal conflicts with other stakeholders. The Project also recognises that there are cases where even if those who are most poor and vulnerable, are also bonded labourers working at risk or at the risk of becoming bonded labourers, such as in the case of women-headed households, indigenous farmers, landlords, including small-binder landlords. Social inequality and elite capture are a risk, and corresponding risk-mitigation measures have been mainstreamed throughout the Project. The project recognises that there is a need to ensure that all stakeholders are involved (in the current framework) both highlight measures to further monitor and ensure the empowerment of participants in the project.”


Examples of measures to deal with these issues include ensuring participation, especially of poor and vulnerable people in the selection of target beneficiaries, based on a climate vulnerability assessment. The AE also stated that “the Project has engaged project personnel on gender equality and social inclusion, including barriers/challenges and best practices for women’s empowerment, particularly in relation to land tenure in different contexts and needs of beneficiaries; CRA ‘enterprise investment’ plans” that include measures to address the above issues. These plans are designed with input from various vulnerable categories (i.e., men/women, low-income households, indigenous peoples, religious minorities, among others); “the Project has also engaged women’s groups, including women’s cooperatives and development for women’s organizations, including low-income women, female-headed households and indigenous women; men and women in leadership roles to serve as champions; elaborated plans for the empowerment of women and vulnerable groups; and that a project grievance redress mechanism be to communicated in local languages and made available.”


The AE also confirmed that consultations have been conducted with CSOs, including some who are involved in broader land reform discussions. “In 2018 and 2019, diverse consultations were held with various stakeholders, including women’s groups, indigenous beneficiaries and farmers from Mansin, B’laan, and Tagabon ethnic groups, the Association of Rural Indigenous Farmers (ARIF), the Federation of Small Farmers and Fisherfolk Organizations, the National Commission on Indigenous Peoples, Uyong ng mga Manggagawa sa Agrikultura (UMA Philippines), the Philippine Confederation of Agricultural Workers (PCAW), the Philippine Confederation of Small Farmers’ and Fisherfolk’s Organisations, among others, who provided feedback on project design and agreed and highlighted the relevance and need for the project (See Gender Action Plan).” The AE also stated that “in 2019, 30 community consultations were conducted with 120 persons [of which] 57 were female and 108 IP farmers.”

# Needs of the recipient

The project will target regions and provinces and locally identified agriculture-based groups that are most vulnerable to climate-related disasters. Provinces that are vulnerable to climate-related disasters include those in the north-eastern part of the country, ranking as low as many least developed countries. General access to services and economic development is limited by the mountainous terrain and the ethnographic nature of some regions reduces access to local services and the presence of many small LGUs to support local populations. Even quite productive lowland areas have high densities of poor and often small-scale farmers, who are generally not able to provide credit for themselves or other small-scale farmers with very little surplus income to invest in CRA, and little access to microfinance.

The main source of CRA finance is driven by fiscal constraints among relevant government authorities, especially the Department of Agriculture (DA) and the National Agricultural Insurance Trust Fund. National government funding has been constrained not only by the recession caused by the COVID-19 pandemic but also by the impact of climate-related disasters (both natural and climate change-induced). Between October 2019 and February 2020 the country experienced a series of typhoons, including Typhoon Kammuri, which caused widespread damage to infrastructure and agriculture. The eruption of the Taal Volcano near Manila, with total damage assessed at USD 283 million, also had an impact on agricultural production in the region. In general, national government funding has been directed towards larger infrastructure projects, rather than the capacity-building and technical assistance that are essential for transitioning to CRA and reaching scale. Other development assistance is likely to be small relative to this type of "soft support", but investment tends to be modest and more geared towards pilots.


---
**Page 168**
---

### Country ownership

The project is fully aligned with and directly contributes to the country’s agriculture and climate change policies and priorities. In the 2021 nationally determined contribution (NDC), the Philippines commits to a projected GHG emission reduction and avoidance of 75 percent for 2030-2040, which includes commitments in the areas of land use, land-use change, forestry, agriculture, waste, industrial, transport and energy sectors. The nationally determined contributions also include commitments on adaptation measures, including climate risk information services to generate and use climate science for risk-informed development. The government has been active in setting out policies and high-level plans for addressing climate change, such as the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP), the Philippine Climate Change Act of 2016, and the Chairmanship of the Cabinet Cluster for Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation and Disaster Risk Reduction. The Department of Agriculture (DA) and the Office on Women and Gender Equality and Empowerment recognize the impact of climate change on women, as does the NCCCAP.

Country ownership of the project has been further strengthened through the addition of the government’s National Agricultural Modernization Fund (NAMF), PAGASA, and FAO. The GCF funds will flow from the AE through the Government of the Philippines’ BfS to FAO and PAGASA. The NAMF is a multi-year fund established by the DA in 2017 for the implementation of agricultural technology data generation, and USD 18.9 million (made up of USD 8.26 million in co-financing from the World Bank) has been allocated to the project. The NAMF is one of several funds which are committed to providing a more climate risk-informed approach to expenditure through its support for the National Agricultural Modernization and Industrialization Plan (NAMIP) for 2020-2025, the One Department of Agriculture strategy, and the Philippine Development Plan (2017-2022), which all highlight the need for climate-resilient agriculture.

The NAMF provides a vision for the agribusiness sector, reorienting major banner programmes to focus on climate change adaptation and mitigation through their respective work streams. This emphasizes the shift to CCA as a key development strategy in the country, involving government expenditure in support of sustainable agriculture. For more information about the NAMF, please refer to the DA’s website for DA successful experiences in the context of the urgency of the climate crisis.

The Climate-Smart Agriculture Office (CSA), which oversees the design of this project, was set up by the Department and is responsible for implementing climate-related legislation, in particular the Climate Change Act and Disaster Risk Reduction and
