### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

- **Of the project’s total proposed budget of USD 39.25 million**, 67 per cent (USD 26.27 million) is proposed to be a GCF grant, and 33 per cent (USD 13 million) as co-financing component.
- The project will be implemented at two levels: it is being piloted in one sense because the project is providing goods that are public in nature, such as agrometeorological services. It is also being piloted in another sense because it is demonstrating the capacity of resource-poor small-scale farmers to form self-sustaining collective enterprises that can then be scaled up.
- The aim of a demonstration model that can be replicated nationwide. The project will cater to small-scale farmers who are vulnerable to climate shocks and other adverse weather events, such as the onset of various weather shocks (e.g., droughts, floods, typhoons) which negatively affect their agricultural production and income.
- Smallholder farmers in this region have insufficient knowledge about climate change adaptation options, as well as low access to credit due to their remote location, lack of credit rating, or absence of collateral.

#### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

- **Of the USD 26.27 million GCF grant**, USD 10.63 million is proposed to be provided by the DA in addition to this official project co-financing.
- In addition to this official project co-financing, the DA will provide parallel finance to the value of USD 18.64 million to support the implementation of the project’s activities and to strengthen their capacity on CRA practices and enterprise development.
- The other €5,000 direct beneficiaries, the CRA enterprise farmers, will receive less than originally planned due to the reduction in the number of CRA enterprises from 23 to 18. This is because smallholder farmers need to apply CRA practices as an essential element of the original project design in terms of the theory of change.
- It is therefore important that the parallel financing of USD 10.63 million be provided by the DA as reported on in the project’s expected performance report to the GCF, along with the official co-financing.

### In response to a query from an independent TAP, FAO confirmed that there was no co-financing for this project.
- **Finances of USD 10.63 million**, approved by the DA, will be spent through FAO as an EE (technical assistance support costing about USD 4.5 million plus USD 14.8 million in CRA enterprise development and other activities using various GCF tools.
- Those mentioned in the funding proposal are FAO’s big data lab; climate risk assessment tools such as climatic suitability and vulnerability assessments, crop yield estimating tools such as PyeTREZ, remote planning, mapping, and ground data collection tools; and spatial tools such as EarthMap using Landsat, MODIS or Sentinel data; BarraInvent and CollectMobile tools.


---
**Page 170**
---

### The project would also leverage parallel finance in the form of credit from key project partner LandBank, the Agriculture Credit Policy Officer and other rural financial service providers.

The total amount of loanable funds available for the proposed interventions is estimated by the AUS at $187.2 million. If the projected 45,000 target farmers/500 CRA enterprises were to borrow or invest in these CRA solutions, the borrowing rate would be at 80 per cent of the project investment package. This conservative estimate of borrowing rate for each CRA enterprise is well within the LandBank's ceiling and lower than current borrowing rates.

### The economic and financial analysis for the project (see annexes 3 of the funding proposal) shows that the proposed interventions are financially viable, with a 20 per cent down payment coming from a farmer, with 12 monthly repayments, 1 year of grace period, and an interest rate of 8.5 to 15 per cent (depending on the CRA solution adopted - see production programmes). Annexes 3 of the funding proposal shows the viability of borrowing for investing in CRA options. LandBank has confirmed its support to the APA project beneficiaries through a loan facility at a maximum of 20 per cent down payment, with commercial programming interest rates ranging between 8.5 and 15 per cent as mentioned above.

### The proposed interventions also have a cost benefit analysis of the seven climate adaptation crop production packages show that the expected financial revenues from adoption of the proposed CRA solutions in the selected agroecological zones will outweigh the costs. The financial results of the proposed interventions are presented below (see annexes 3 of the funding proposal) on an individual (i.e., per hectare) and aggregate terms, assuming seven representative CRA enterprises per intervention zone. The expected incremental financial revenues from proposed interventions range from USD 1,400 to USD 15,700, depending on the proposed intervention scenario adopted. The expected incremental financial costs of the proposed interventions are between 30,000-50,000 hectares per intervention, and financial NPVs range from USD 50 million to USD 427 million per intervention zone. These results confirm that the proposed interventions are financially sustainable, which bodes well for farmers to adopt and maintain proposed CRA solutions.

### The economic analysis results were based on the same assumptions as the financial analysis. The project's overall net economic impact is positive at a discount rate of 10 per cent (assuming lower carbon prices) or 20 years at 10 per cent discount rate, are USD 500 million (assuming lower carbon prices) and USD 524 million (assuming higher carbon prices), respectively. The project's overall net economic impact is positive at a discount rate of 30 per cent for the same time horizon, with USD 681 million (assuming lower carbon prices) or USD 794 million (assuming higher carbon prices). At a discount rate of 33 per cent and 34 per cent, respectively. These results confirm the economic viability of the proposed interventions.

### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

#### a. The independent TAP believes this project from the Government of the Philippines, supported by FAO, is an important one that can do much to reduce the vulnerability of the country and its people to climate change impacts while also significantly contributing to reduced GHG emissions from the sector.

- Ensure that an agreement is reached so that the parallel funding of USD 10.63 million to be provided by the DA to support vulnerable households to apply CCA practices, which will be reported as a component of the project’s annual performance reports to the GCF, along with the official co-financing.
- Develop or customize a monitoring tool capturing all the necessary information to make sure that the project is achieving its intended emission reductions, ensuring clear reporting to the GCF on the extent to which the anticipated mitigation benefits are met.

# EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

## Gender Parity in the Philippines

1 The Philippines has been ranked as one of the top ten countries in the world in terms of making considerable progress in advancing gender equality, including gender parity and wage equality in recent years. Its laws and policies aspire to attain gender equality. Literacy rates are high at over 96% for both women and men. With respect to education, net enrolment and cohort survival rates are higher than the global average. Women have access to financial services and credit facilities that are equally divided between women and men. Proportionately more women have access to the internet than men. However, despite these achievements, women in the Philippines continue to lag behind men in terms of their livelihoods. The rural poor, who make up a large proportion of the population, have not shown great interest in gender. Climate change policies pay far less attention to gender equality and women's rights. Women are often excluded from practical guidance on climate concerns. Gender mainstreaming in development has been overemphasized by Philippine Commission of Women, but the efficacy of the efforts is yet to be analyzed.

2 Country rankings are dependent on a small set of officially widely available national statistics, which may not reflect the true picture of gender equality and underprivileged whose existence may be entirely informal. What they highlight is the plight of each social stratum, in particular the upper class, who have access to education and health services. The lower classes, particularly women, are at a disadvantage when it comes to political empowerment. Net income and wealth equalities are high, suggesting a huge inequality among women between women and men of the same socioeconomic circumstances.

3 The role and status of women differ greatly among the indigenous peoples in the Philippines: from the egalitarian Agta, slightly more gender differentiated peoples through Cordillera plus some in Mindanao. In terms of political power, in terms of gender parity, non-indigenous Filipinos in the rural areas today could be said roughly to have the same rights as men for those who used to assign traditional and prestigious roles to women.

4 The Philippine women overall participate well in decision-making at all levels, including the local level, where they are more likely to be elected than men. As men. The norm is to pool the incomes and decide on the spending jointly. Non-indigenous households in the rural areas and indigenous peoples in Mindanao have a different pattern of income sharing. In the case of the Agta, it is the husband's opinion that prevails, according to the farmers who were interviewed for project formulation. Household work and caregiving are entirely on the women's shoulders, and the women in the household are not involved in decision-making.

5 Although the Philippines has made substantial progress towards gender equality, including closing gaps in economic participation, challenges for obtaining gender equality include: restrictive laws and socio-cultural norms that continue to limit women's access to education, reproductive rights, and gender-based violence. Legal marital divorce is not permitted, except for cases of adultery. In 2013, the Catholic Church was reported to have excommunicated 54,789 people, including 6,000 Christian women more than Christian men because of social stigma. The country is the 12th highest in the world in terms of maternal mortality ratio (MMR). The MMR for rural areas is three times higher than urban areas due to poor access to reproductive information. Early childbearing is more pronounced in the rural than in urban areas: Significantly less than a third of births among teenage mothers were reported as

8 The official statistics imply that the managing responsibilities in agriculture lie with men, while more women are involved in livestock and poultry farming. In contrast to the situation among non-indigenous households in Cordillera, the division of agricultural work is not very strict, although any task involving animals or plants is usually done by a woman. Women from these communities are considered more adept than men at negotiating and managing finances and are given corresponding responsibilities. They also have better knowledge about farming techniques, in most cases even more so than Mindanaoans who are the agricultural specialists. Women are generally more open to new ideas than men; men need to be convinced of the utility of the new knowledge so that they can accept it. Women are responsible for all aspects of animal husbandry, including feeding and animals that take care of, irrespective of participation in farming. Female farmers are active in both indigenous and non-indigenous farmer organizations and have been given posts of president, treasurer and the like.

9 Indigenous varieties and breeds are endowed with characteristics that make them hardy under the local climate, even with climate change. They also do well on, even better without synthetic inputs. It is not surprising then to find that indigenous varieties and breeds are preferred by women farmers. Women tend to have vast knowledge of plants because of their role as cooks, gatherers, gardeners and healers. Indigenous varieties and breeds are more adapted to the local climate in the Philippines. Women are recognized worldwide as better caregivers to animals, except when taking large animals to the pastures. In the Philippines, indigenous women's knowledge in farming is also proving useful for the introduction of new technologies. However, the participation of indigenous farmers in Cordillera has declined with the introduction of non-traditional rice varieties and accompanying agricultural inputs. The reason is that these varieties require more intensive care than enough construction work for men; agriculture has become mostly women's work. Overall the majority of indigenous women are still involved in farming but their knowledge about organic and farming are still fairly strong among the people in Cordillera Administrative Region.

10 The young are competent in digital technology, the skills that are considered indispensable in agriculture for efficient communication and cash-income generation, but the parents discourage them from taking up agriculture as they consider the livelihood synonymous with poverty. On the other

# Women and Climate Change Adaptation: A Guide

## 13 The Role of Male Farmers

Male farmers interviewed by the project formulation mission team have noticed that seasonal rainfall patterns have become irregular and extreme; average ambient and water temperatures are increasing; and there is a greater frequency of droughts, floods, and landslides. They observed: higher mortality and lower quality of crops; higher number of livestock deaths; increased incidence of plant diseases; and an increase in the rate of change in rainfall patterns, need for shade for the animals; and higher animal mortality from disease.

Indigenous ways of life require great skills in foreseeing the changes in natural conditions, which will be useful to farmers. Indigenous women have a greater understanding than men about climate change and men-in-dates. As coping strategies, the farmers listed: application of increased amounts of pesticides and fertilizers; planting of crops such as cassava, sweet potato, maize, early planting for drought is expected; vegetative cultivation for food security; and control of soil water to prevent clogging of irrigation canals.

## 12 Other on-farm coping strategies in the Philippines include: planting of root crops and others around the house; planting of vegetables such as tomatoes, eggplant, and squash in the backyard; and vegetable cultivation on the upper back portion of the rice field. These are mainly tasks considered by women because they have more time to do them than men. In addition, some indigenous and indigenous women provided supplementary income. Some indigenous farmers in Cordillera engage in reforestation of watershed and riverine areas as well as synchronized rice planting as an offset for the loss of rice production.

## 13 Due to the custom of registering family land under a man's name, women are at a great disadvantage when it comes to taking out formal credit or insurance, whose importance is expected to grow in the future. In most cases, women have no access to financial services; however, the husband's signature or co-signature is usually demanded for financial transactions, which makes limited-headed households, already one of the poorest in the rural areas, even more vulnerable.

## 14 Microfinance institutions are flourishing in the Philippines, but little analysis is available on the impact of these institutions. The most common microfinance schemes involve loans to buy fertilizer and seeds can be used to encourage adoption of climate change adaptation strategies, but their target group is usually men. Women have limited access to credit because they face a number of economic difficulties such as borrowing money from the so-called 6%, the village money lenders who charge 20% interest. Some farmers consulted made use of microfinance schemes, which only required participation in training and submission of a loan application for purchase of insurance.

### Women and Adopting Agriculture to Changing Climate

## 15 Women are on average better educated and more open to changes than men. Indigenous women have the knowledge and skills that would form the foundation of climate resilient agriculture, while young indigenous women have the technical and entrepreneurial skills necessary for commercial and commercial information needed for integrating climate resilient agriculture in livelihoods.

Although research has shown that women's participation is essential for successful adaptation made by men, especially the strategic ones which include adoption of new agricultural methods.


---
**Page 183**
---

16 The Gender Action Plan (GAP) for the proposed project is based on the project formulation principles of: female farmer as agents of change; higher parity in strategic decision making; working with nature; indigenous peoples as guardians of traditional approaches; self-organization as climate change adaptation strategy and rapid communication. The GAP adheres to the project implementation principles of: provision of ecosystem, health and livelihood services; sharing the driver's role in the project formulation process; ensuring that women are at the center of action for the indigenous peoples; and building on the strengths of women, indigenous peoples and youth. The Plan touches upon indigenous peoples and youth, while maintaining the primary focus on women.

# PART I: GENDER ASSESSMENT

## INTRODUCTION

### 1.1 Project Introduction from Gender Perspective

1. Climate change is estimated to put at risk the sectors that are responsible for 85% of the Gross National Product of the Philippines. *Agriculture* is the source of employment and main livelihood for 39% and 20%, respectively, of the population in urban and rural areas. Agriculture accounted for about 10% of the national economy in 2020. Although the average income of agricultural households is unknown, the mean income from non-agricultural sources and activities was about 6 times higher than that of agricultural households. The majority (85%) of women who are economically active and engaged in agriculture are vulnerable and thus they are unlikely to withstand the vagaries of climate change without adequate support.

2. The proposed project aims at creating an environment that enables rural female and male farmers to thrive under the changing climate. To that end, the project will improve their access to climate information and climate resilient agriculture (CRA) services – such as seeds, water and credit crisis-resistant technologies, improved livestock management practices, and improved farming practices, to generate cash income and new livelihoods. The capacities of the government at the national, provincial, municipal, and barangay levels are also strengthened in order for them to adapt to climate change while transforming the Philippine agriculture towards resilient and low emission development pathways.

3. While gender-based division of labor among tasks for animals exists to varying degrees in the Philippines, the degree of contribution of female farmers is incontestable as that all crops or animals are taken care of by both women and men. They constitute two groups engaged in different facets of agricultural production. However, the role of women in agriculture has not always been taken into account when we consider agriculture. Although the Philippines has consistently ranked as one of the top countries with respect to gender equality, female farmers are not registered as farmers themselves under the Registry System: Basic Sectors in Agriculture, but only mentioned together with their husbands who are farmers.

4. In areas where local off-farm opportunities for cash income generation are available, the agricultural sector is often considered a last resort occupation for men on occasions when they need to physically demanding work.* The proportion of female-headed households has been steadily increasing in the past decades, reaching one fifth of all households in 2013. *As in most parts of the

5 Women on average are more literate than men in the Philippines,* they have as much basic skills as men or more to cope with the changing world. The main obstacles for women remain the system that is centered around men and pays scant attention to the strengths and constraints of women in their roles as heads of households, mothers, wives, daughters-in-law, sisters, grandmothers and youth, two other attributes that disadvantage an individual and may overlap with gender.* The Gender Assessment Action Plan** of the proposed project, while focusing on women,** touches upon these two groups.


### 1.2 Objective of the Assessment


#### 1.2.1 Rationale

6 Agricultural operators – persons who take the technical and administrative responsibility of managing the holdings – were overwhelmingly male in 2005.* Approximately 3.2 million female who were not agricultural operators engaged in farming activities, while only about 487,000 female were not agricultural operators and did the same.* In households with agricultural operators engaged in farming activities three out every five members were women.* These statistics imply that, officially at least, the managing responsibilities in agriculture lie with men,* while more women were engaged in farming than men.


7 Climate change as a negative shock impacts the vulnerable members of the society, which include women and children.** Women are disproportionately affected by climate change because they are 14 times more likely to die during a disaster,** whose frequency and intensity are increasing due to climate change.* At the same time, inclusion of these members in projects and policies is known to improve their outcomes.


8 The assessment examines the situation of rural female farmers with respect to climate change* to find the most effective ways to reduce the vulnerability of the weak members of the society and allow them to adapt to the changing environment.


9 The Philippines has ratified international agreements and goals related to the gender,* most notably the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women.* In 2009,** the government further signed a framework of rights for women* based directly on international laws known as the Philippine Women’s Agenda (PWA).** The PWA is a comprehensive policy that addresses all facets of life.

10 The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) recognizes that gender equality is key to its mandate to achieve food security for all. As evidenced by FAO Policy on Gender Equality* and Regional Gender Strategy for Asia and the Pacific**, "the Organization further sees rural women as agents of change whose engagement is indispensable to meet any of the 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in agriculture, rural development and food security."

11 Indigenous peoples and the youth also represent important disadvantaged groups, half of which are women. While many of the hurdles that women face are common, others are distinct enough for them to be considered separate issues. The rapid changes taking place in production systems provide the foundation for climate resilient agriculture and the technology savviness of the youth can help agriculture to benefit from digital information and connections.

1.2.2 Methodology

12 The Gender Assessment for the Green Climate Fund (GCF) project*, "Adapting Philippine Agriculture to Climate Change" studies the socioeconomic conditions of women and men targeted by the proposed project to shed light on gender-specific constraints and needs for a strategic approach to the project, as well as other dimensions into the project. The Assessment also briefly examines the indigenous peoples and youth in the region as part of the background information provided under the Gender Action Plan.

13 The underlying theory of change is that the project will strengthen the capabilities, constraints, and needs of women and men so that their potential as agents of change is best realized for achieving the best outcomes possible under the proposed project. It will necessarily entail changes in access to and distribution of resources and decision-making power between women and men in the targeted communities. The same applies to indigenous peoples and youth as their capacities complementary to others' have not been fully taken advantage of in agriculture.

2. METHODOLOGY

14 The Gender Assessment focuses on the situation of rural women in the regions targeted by the proposed project: Cordillera Administrative Region, Region II (Cagayan Valley), Region IV (Bicol), Region X (Northern Mindanao), and Region XI (SOCCSKSARGEN).

15 Consultations were the primary means to determine the key issues pertaining to the lives of farmers in the regions targeted by the proposed project. A total of 3,734 farmers in the target regions were consulted, of which 1,176 were women. Women's views on agriculture related issues were also sought through focus group discussions (FGDs) with 205 women farmers; institutional meetings were held at national and regional levels. At the national level, nine institutions and organizations were consulted, and at the regional level, the mission team had meetings with nine. The issues identified during these consultations are summarized in this document. The detailed analysis elucidated risks and opportunities for female farmers and their families in the targeted regions.

16 To the best extent possible, the assessment is based on official statistics and published research results pertaining to the target regions and field consultations conducted in those regions. Where relevant information could not be found, it relies on the input of the rural areas as the whole nation.

3. LEGAL, ADMINISTRATIVE AND SPIRITUAL FRAMEWORK

### Protection of Women and Gender Equality

17 The Philippines has ratified the Universal Declaration of Human Rights, the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), International Covenant on Economic, Social and Cultural Rights, and the Convention on the Rights of the Child. These treaties include provisions that protect women’s rights to equality with men in all spheres of life, including their rights as women and girls that are equal to men’s and boys’. The UN Resolution on Child, Early and Forced Marriage was adopted in 2014 with the broad-based, cross-regional co-sponsorship of 116 countries. It calls for States Parties to take all necessary measures to eliminate child marriage by working with civil society; to enact, enforce and uphold laws and policies to end the practice; and develop and implement national action plans to address child marriage. The Philippines has also worked closely with stakeholders, including civil society; to promote and protect the human rights of all women and girls, including those who are vulnerable or marginalized, such as women living in poverty, women related to their sexuality’ The Convention on the Rights of the Child, ratified by the Philippines, sets a minimum age of marriage of 18, and the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women, ratified by the country, obligates the signatory states to ensure free and full consent to marriage.

18 In 2013, the ASEAN Declaration on the Elimination of Violence against Women and Violence against Children was adopted. This declaration includes which which includes the Philippines, to reaffirm the goals and commitments of the organization to eliminate violence against women and monitor their progress, as in the Declaration on the Elimination of Violence Against Women in the ASEAN Member States. The Philippines has also ratified the Sustainable Development Goals resolution which Goal 5 is to end all forms of discrimination against women and girls, including early marriage.

19 By tradition, the Philippines is a matrilineal and egalitarian society, which is reflected in legislation that aim at closing gender gaps’. As the same time, the signatory three centuries of

Spanish rule, in the form of patriarchy and strong influence of the Roman Catholic Church, continues in the family, civil and penal laws.* Under the Spanish regime, only adult males were obligated to labor for 40 days per year, performing colonial work duties. Both women and men were required to work in the colonial plantations; but only men were paid and women were considered as the unpaid family members. The Philippine Constitution of 1935, under Article 2, Section 14 asserts that “The State recognizes the role of women in nation-building, and shall ensure the fundamental equality before the law of all citizens.” This was a response to a regulation for equal pay known as Republic Act No. 7016, the Magna Carta of Women,* which is an anti-discrimination against women by recognizing, protecting, fulfilling and promoting the rights of Filipino women in all spheres of life, including their right to equality before the law, freedom from discrimination and participation in the country.* It consolidated and strengthened the existing laws and policies on women under one legislation.* Other regulations for protection of women and gender equality include the ones in Table 1 below.

beliefs." The lack of budget, capacities and political will are said to be major barriers in increasing access to reproductive health care and improving maternal mortality.

22 In 2008, the city of Manila issued an executive order, which declared that the city would take "an 'affirmative stand on pro-life issues.'" The UN Committee on Elimination of Discrimination against Women conducted an inquiry, in response to a joint written submission from non-governmental organizations and the Catholic Church, regarding the implementation of this order. In the report published in 2015, the Committee stated that the order resulted in a systematic denial of access to reproductive health services and information, including family planning and services." It led to unplanned pregnancies, unsafe abortions, unnecessary and preventable maternal deaths and increased exposure of women to HIV/AIDS." The Committee observed that "the lives and health of women and girls are at risk because of the executive order. This is particularly true for adolescent girls, as well as women in abusive relationships." The Committee concluded that the Philippine government's actions violated its obligations under international human rights law, including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women. The Committee recommended that the Philippines "take all necessary measures to ensure access to information, education, and sensitive government representatives towards eliminating ideological barriers that limit women’s rights." These recommendations do not appear to have been implemented.

23 The Responsible Parenthood and Reproductive Health Act of 2012 stipulates that reproductive health services are a basic human right and fundamental social service," but guarantees universal and free access to nearly all modern contraceptives." According to the law, government agencies must provide "free or low-cost family planning services and other forms of reproductive care to all women and families." It also mandates sex education in schools and provision of reproductive health services by companies to their employees." The act took more than 13 years before it was signed into law. It was passed on June 28, 2008, and signed by President Gloria Macapagal Arroyo on July 15, 2009.

The act was challenged in 2011 before the Supreme Court, delaying its full implementation." A year later the Supreme Court ruled that the law is constitutional. The court removed provisions that allowed minors to access reproductive health services without the written consent of a guardian and penal measures for government officials who did not implement the law."

24 In 2013, a further temporary restraining order was issued by the Supreme Court and prevented the Food and Drug Administration of the Philippines (FDA) from procuring, distributing or issuing new certificates of product registration on more than 50 different contraceptives. The order was launched after the FDA registered contraceptive products with prices far above those charged in other countries. As a result, the Health Department could resume distribution of family planning supplies to regional health offices in 2014 and national distribution by early 2015. However, the government had already planned for this in 2013. In 2018, the Philippine president, Rodrigo Duterte, signed an executive order for the full implementation of the reproductive rights law. This was a significant legislative measure, with an official calling it "pro-life, pro-women, pro-children and pro-economic development." The act is still not yet in effect, however, and no sexual education curriculum has been implemented.

### 3.2 Agriculture, Climate Change and Indigenous Peoples Policies on Gender

#### 3.2.1 Gender and Agriculture Policy

The main objectives of agricultural policies in the Philippines are food security and poverty alleviation achieved through a stable supply of food at affordable prices as well as protection of the domestic market from foreign competition. However, these policies have had mixed results and have negatively affected the welfare of rural landowners and urban poor households.17 While the like female-headed households: most, while it has been instrumental in protecting the smallholders, for example, women's contribution to agriculture is not officially recognized as on par with that of men.

The policies do not address the issue of female farmers explicitly, and by setting aside the issue of gender differentiation in agriculture, have unintentionally affected female farmers. For example, women are more likely than men to grow vegetables such as tomatoes, eggplants, asparagus, broccoli, green onions, garden peas, lettuce, radish and cauliflower, whereas women traditionally cultivated corn, potatoes, garlic, onion and cabbage.18 The government's focus on non-traditional crops has led to the decline of rice cultivation in the Cordillera Administrative Region (CAR) and consequently eroded the culture built around rice cultivation which women have been involved with for centuries. Women are also not included as beneficiaries under the Rice Sector Support Program (RSSP), but are mentioned after the husband's name who is the official farmer; women's contribution to agriculture is not officially recognized as on par with that of men.

#### 3.2.2 Gender and Livestock Policy

27 Poultry recorded 78% growth between 1990 and 2014, making it the fastest growing animal sector in the country. Chicken and ducks are not only valuable for their meat, but also for their eggs; it is the woman who takes care of the small animals.32 including poultry, no bio-polity or analysis refers to the role of women in the sub-sector, as is the case with crop agriculture. The number of goats increased from 10 million in 1985 to 47 million in 2014.33 It is the woman who takes care of them.34 It was nicknamed the sunnie industry for its potential, but no reference has been made to women who are the caretakers.35

### 3.2.3 Gender and Climate Change Policy

28 Being a relatively new policy topic, the latest advancements on gender in the country have been integrated into existing policies and plans on climate change; the poor, women and children are identified as groups who need special attention.

29 The Climate Change Act of 200736 and its Amendment 201237 recognize women and children in the rural area as the most vulnerable to "potential dangerous consequences of climate change and global warming." They are more likely to be affected by changes in rainfall patterns, increased frequency or severity of droughts, fires, floods and storms, climate-related illnesses and diseases, damage to infrastructure, loss of livelihoods, and displacement of people.38 The act also recognizes women and children as key stakeholders for the environment, society, culture, and economy.39 They see the necessity of gender mainstreaming and identification of differential impacts of climate change on women, men and children.

30 The National Disaster Risk Reduction and Management Law of 201040 defines vulnerable and marginalized groups in disaster risk reduction as "women, children, elderly, differently-abled people, and ethnic minorities" and stipulates reparations for the victims.41 It also states that women are more likely to be affected by climate change than men.42 It is declared that it shall be the policy of the state "to develop and strengthen the capacities of vulnerable groups in disaster risk reduction and management with a view to reducing the effects of disasters." This law has been praised for taking a global lead in empowering the local governments in climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction as well as for its pro-poorness.43

31 The National Framework Strategy on Climate Change (NFSCC) adopted in 2010 states that to mitigate "adaptation measures should be based on equity, in accordance with common but differentiated responsibilities; special attention must be given to ensure equal and equitable protection of the environment."44

of the poor, women, children and other vulnerable and disadvantaged sectors.”** The National Climate Change Action Plan 2011-2028**, born out of the Framework, enlarges the definition of vulnerable groups and sets the objective of its National Strategy Priority on the Human Security Agenda as reducing vulnerability of men and women and other vulnerable groups (children, elderly and persons with disabilities). It states that “gender equality and the elimination of discrimination against women and girls, including violence against them, is an integral dimension of the development, disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation agenda.” The plan also states: “ensuring that the concerns and experiences of women and men are an integral dimension of the design, implementation and monitoring of all policies and programs to ensure that women and men benefit equally and inequality is not perpetuated.” It further clarifies that “the term ‘gender’ refers to the socially constructed roles, behaviors, activities and attributes that society considers appropriate for men and women, as well as the hidden power structures that govern relationships between them. Gender is a ‘term used to emphasize that sex inequality is not caused by the anatomical and physiological differences that characterize men and women, but rather by the unequal and unequal treatment socially accorded to them.”


32 During a session on the Philippines’ report on the Status of Women in 2011, the Philippine delegation, through its resolution entitled: “Mainstreaming Gender Equality and Promoting Empowerment of Women in Climate Policies and Strategies,” which highlighted the need to ensure women’s full and equal participation in environmental decision-making at all levels. It was adopted by the commission’s member states.


### 3.2 Gender and Indigenous Peoples


93 The indigenous peoples in the Philippines are officially recognized by the Philippine Constitution as well as by the landmark Indigenous Peoples’ Rights Act of 1997 (IPRA).** The act further recognizes the rights of indigenous communities to their ancestral lands, territories and resources through Certificates of Ancestral Domain Titles. The rights of indigenous Cultural Communities to define their development priorities through their own Ancestral Domain Sustainable Development and Protection Plans are also recognized, in relation to managing and utilizing the natural resources within their traditional lands.


94 The act is a copious of the Universal Declaration of Human Rights including the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women. It further states that “the [s]tate shall guarantee the fundamental human rights and freedoms as enshrined in the Constitution and relevant international instruments are guaranteed also to indigenous women” and that “[p]rovisions of laws, policies and programs for the protection and promotion of the enjoyment of rights and privileges already recognized and accorded to women under existing laws of general application shall be extended to these services.” The act states that “[g]ender discrimination will not be a right and special needs of indigenous women, elderly, youth, children and differently-abled persons.”


---
**Page 194**
---

35 The act recognizes the indigenous concept of ownership for ancestral domains (which includes lands, island waters, coastal areas and other natural resources associated with the space)42 and ancestral lands. The act further details that "[t]he [Indigenous] concept of ownership generally holds that ancestral domains are the ICCP43 purely private community property which belongs to all generations of its members and cannot be sold, disposed or destroyed. It likewise covers customary traditional resource rights."44

36 Although the Climate Change Act of 2016 and the American Recovery and Reinvestment Act of 2009 do not specifically mention indigenous peoples, the Philippine Commission on Women's Contribution to the UNCCD in 2015 indicates that the country pursues "[i]nstitutional reforms factoring sustainable and responsible use of natural resources, respect for, protection, promotion, and fulfillment as well, at all, the full enjoyment of human rights by women and girls, including their equal access to land, credit, technology, education, and equal participation of women, intergenerational equity, biodiversity conservation, food and water security."45

### 3.2.5 Mechanism for Gender Mainstreaming

#### Regulate Act 9780: The Magna Carta of Women
Regulate Act 9780 makes a direct reference to the country's governance structure in legislating gender mainstreaming as a strategy for the government departments, including their attached agencies, offices, bureaus, state universities and colleges, governmental bodies, local governments units, and other public service delivery institutions.46 Specifically it urges these institutions to eliminate gender inequality in their systems, structures, policies, programs and processes and to base planning and budgeting on sustainability principles.

#### The Magna Carta also designates the Philippine Commission on Women (PCW) as the primary policy-making and coordinating body of the women and gender equality concerns under the Office of the President.47 The PCW is not a regulatory agency, but it has the authority to demand any government agency and instrumentalities to report on the subject. To that end, the commission developed a Gender and Development (GAD) approach to provide Philippine governments with a framework for integrating gender equality into their policies and programs, addressing gender concerns into development programs and projects.48 The GAD guidelines require the definition of a gender-sensitive policy or program, which includes the identification of gender gaps, assessment of accountability and monitoring mechanisms.49 Specifically, the government agencies and national government instrumentalities are required to submit an annual Gender Equality Assessment (GEA) accomplishment report, but compliance appears sporadic and the quality of submitted documents variable. The PCW has also issued guidelines for elaborating a GAD approach which includes a gender assessment in service delivery plans, budgetary plans and budgets. Various legislations on

3.3 Agriculture, Climate Information and Indigenous Peoples Services on Gender

### 3.3.1 Gender and Agriculture Extension Services

The exact number of persons engaged in agriculture extension services is unavailable because many of these positions are not officially recorded. However, it has been observed that "only in countries such as the Philippines have women field staff been deployed in sufficient numbers and with sufficient resources to become effective agents of change among women farmers." In a study in 2015 that compared Leyte and Eastern Samar provinces in the Philippines, only one out of every five extension workers were female, respectively.

As of 2006, more than six million pesos per year was budgeted for agriculture extension employing approximately 38,000 people at Local Government Units (LGUs), Department of Agriculture, Department of Agricultural Reform, and Commission on Higher Education. Given that the proportion of women in extension services has stayed relatively constant over time, about one extension worker of some kind could have been hired to serve 130-140 farmers, or one female extension worker for about 250 female farmers.

The regional differences appear to be large, as ratios of female extension workers vary from 6% in the Visayas to 87% in Mindanao. None of the study widely available examines the context of extension work or its general representation.

Female farmers immediately reported that it was women who attended the trainings, as they are more associated with housework and daily community obligations than with field work. In Southeast Asia, female farmers are generally much more active and receptive to new information, and in part due to this, they are more likely to be involved in decision-making regarding their own husband.

Nonetheless, the presence of male farmers in trainings and meetings is considered necessary for the successful implementation of extension services. The role of male farmers is often assumed by those who are to be introduced to the work that they are responsible for. In Northern Mindanao, men were more likely than women to attend training sessions.

42 The agriculture extension services provided by the government used to consist of "the diffusion among the people, of useful and practical information on agriculture, soil conservation, livestock, fisheries, forest conservation, public lands and natural resources laws, home economics and rural life."109 Extension workers were responsible for providing this information through field visits, meetings, conferences, publications and other means on imparting information; 110 and extension workers were graduates of the College of Agriculture or had completed a course in agriculture.111 They worked with farmers, livestock owners, fishermen, foresters, forest conservationists, public lands managers, poultry and livestock farmers. Since the devolution of the Bureau of Agricultural Extension into LGU Extension Service in the early 1990s, agricultural extension has become highly political; increasingly, the application of extension services is based less on technical qualifications but on political affiliation or loyalty.

3.2.2 Gender and Climate Information Services

43 The gender differences in the perception of and coping strategies for climate change are slowly gaining attention. A recent study by the University of Nevada – Reno Americans, northern ranchers and farmers – in Nevada reported that women ranchers and farmers demonstrated greater concern and more scientifically accurate knowledge about climate change than men.112 In a study in rural Nepal, women were found to be more aware of climate change than men; however, this was not the case for all women.113 The association between gender and higher education was stronger than that between geography and perceived climate change.114 A study by the University of California – Davis found that perceptions of gender, age and economic status.115The differences in information interpretation or immediate action have not yet been investigated; and no country appears to have integrated such consideration in generation and diffusion of weather and climate information.

In 2008, two indigenous girls in the rural areas of Mindanao, aged 13 and 14 years, were gang raped on their way to school. The girls were forced to pay a payment to soldiers in exchange for their word that they would not lodge a formal complaint.142

46 Special Protection of Children Against Abuse, Exploitation and Discrimination Act of 1992, Republic Act 7850, punishes the recruitment of children; facilitates agreements with armed groups to enhance protection of children in conflict situations; establishes a national mechanism for monitoring and response system for grave child rights violations in situations of armed conflict.143 The government has yet to submit its review on the act for preventing recruitment of children or having been recruited or used in hostilities. Claps in implementation of the act has been observed.144

It is also the near best in the region on perceived wage gap for comparable work.105 The country had a gender pay gap of 23%, which is lower than that of all professionals were women in the Philippines, but earned 8.5% less on average than their male counterparts.106 In terms of female representation among professionals, women accounted for 53% of total employment, but earned 11.3% less than men.107

49 Among the 17 administrative regions of the Philippines, the gender equality in various indicators are comparable: labor-force participation rate; professional and technical jobs; leadership positions; and wage gap. On literacy rates and educational attainment, gender parity exists in all of the administrative regions except for Central Luzon and Caraga.108 The incidence of violence high throughout the country and it was extremely high for Autonomous Regions of Muslim Mindanao. His profile shows that the incidence of violence is highest among women aged 25–49 years old. The gender difference was the widest in leadership positions, with Central Luzon and Caraga scoring high inequality and others almost equally divided between medium and low inequality.109 McKinsey Global Institute found that the Philippines had the lowest female representation rate (37%) of all regions in different regions; maternal mortality mostly high; and violence against women mostly medium level.110 The country also has the highest general rape for all regions, indicating that the selective abortion or female infanticide was negligible.

### Literacy and Education

The basic literacy rates among Filipino women and men aged 15 and above were 96.8% and 96.0%, respectively, in 2013. The functional literacy rate among the population of age 10 to 64 nationwide was 90.3%. The rate for females was higher than among males, 92.0% and 88.7%, respectively. Female functional literacy rate is consistently 3 percentage points above that of male in each region targeted by the proposed project, sometimes close to 7 percentage points as in Bicol (Table 3).

connectivity as at 2016. Less than 80% of the applicants for teaching positions passed the licensure exams in various fields in 2016, and less than 50% of the faculty members at higher education institutions have at least a master's degree.**4.3.4 Poverty and Wealth**

57 The poverty incidence among women was 22.5% for the entire country, 20.6% for Cordillera Autonomous Region (CAR), 16.2% for Cagayan, 36.5% for Bicol, 27.8% for SOCCSKARGEN, and 36.7% for North Cotabato. The poverty incidence among men was 19.4%, with the highest in CAR (20%), followed by Cagayan (25.7%) and SOCCSKARGEN (25%). Poverty among the farmers is wider spread, except for Cagayan among the targeted regions: 34% (nationwide), 38% (CAR), 19% (Cagayan), 26% (Bicol), 20.7% (SOCCSKARGEN) and 25.7% (SOCCSKARGEN). The number of female-headed households has consistently been increasing: 14% in 1993, 16.8% in 2006 and 18.6% in 2013.**The potential contributing factors were: stronger participation of women in the labor force, especially among those who are married or whose husbands work at higher income levels; female-headed households fared better in economic terms while it was the case that male-headed households experienced more severe food insecurity and/or chronic hunger during the past three months before the Annual Poverty Indicators Survey was conducted in 2013. The rate was close to 7% for the bottom 30%. Data segmented by region or gender is not available.

58 The Philippines has a large wealth gap as measured by wealth Gini index in 2016.*Credit Suisse's report on global wealth categorizes the Philippines as having a wealth per adult of USD 5,000-10,000.**Considering this poverty rate also ties into a huge wealth gap in the country.

1083. Code of Muslim Personal Laws of the Philippines, applies instead, prohibiting the wives from managing communal property or personal business without the husband’s consent.264 although the husbands are not bound by such restrictions.265 At the same time, each Muslim spouse is allowed to own and administer exclusive property, and a Muslim woman does not lose ownership and administration of her property upon marriage.266 The Code also provides for equal division of conjugal partnership is the same for the wife and husband, except for responsibilities related to the household and children.267 In case of divorce, the Code provides that the father has custody and control of the children; the Muslim code gives privilege to mothers, maternal grandmothers, sisters and aunts in the event of guardianship of minor children.268 The Muslim code also provides for guardians as guardians of marriage of children.269 While the country does not have legal provisions for divorce, the Family Code allows legal separation without the right to remarry as well as annulment of marriage.270 The Muslim Personal Laws are applicable only in Muslim communities.

The United Nations Human Rights Committee recommended in 2017 that the government revise the minimum age of marriage for girls in the Code of Muslim Personal Laws. The most common reasons for breaking up a marriage in the Philippines are infidelity or extramarital sexual relationships, often initiated by the husband (in cases where the wife is younger than her husband) on the wife or the children; or sexual abuse inflicted by the husband (in some cases by the wife on children). In 2017, the Supreme Court ruled that a Muslim woman cannot be divorced based solely on the woman's and hence the lack of legal provisions for divorce burdens women more than men.

### 4.15 Religion

63 Starting in the early 16th century, the Spanish missionaries promoted Christian ideas of premarital virginity and fidelity within a society where unmarried girls were usually free to engage in sexual activity. The Catholic Church's stance on premarital sex was not well received by the nonelite. The missionaries were intrigued but yet repelled by the male penis adoration, which they attributed to the "carnal lustfulness" of women and used to justify their proselytization activities in the Philippines.

64 The missionaries made great efforts to undermine the elderly women who were priestesses, possessed ritual objects and presided over animal ceremonies, because such women were related to witchcraft. They also sought to eliminate the practice of "spiritual marriage," which was a union between men who were transgender women who were assigned male at birth, but identified – and were socially accepted as – women. The Catholic Church's stance on transgender people is similar to that of Hinduism, which recognizes a third gender, but not in Northern Luzon, the mountainous areas of Mindanao and Sulu, where there are many examples of spiritual unions and symbols with Christian equivalents as a means to degrade the position of the priestess. The Catholic Church recruited to locate sacred items and then urinate on them or perform other acts of desecration. Men dressed in women's clothing were also considered as such. The Catholics molded the population into a binary sex and gender framework.

65 The influence of the Roman Catholic Church remains in contemporary family, civil and penal laws, most prominently in marriage and reproductive health, as discussed above. "Filipinos are generally more religious than Americans," according to a 2017 Pew Research Center report considered unconstitutional and the church opposes anti-discrimination policies and occasionally seeks to

66 Presidential Decree 1083, Code of Muslim Personal Laws of the Philippines, is based on Sharia law.279 applicable only to Muslims, and is allowed to prevail over the civil legislation of the Philippines in matters pertaining to marriage, divorce, inheritance, and other personal status issues. A Muslim woman is allowed only one husband. The wife cannot acquire any property by gratuitous title without the husband's consent. She can inherit only if her husband dies intestate. The husband has the right to own and administer them after marriage.280 The wife may take up any occupation, occupation or engage in lawful business, as long as she consents and she observes Islamic modesty and virtue.281 The Code does not provide for conjugal partnership, cohabitation, or joint property ownership between husband and wife. It is based on the conjugal partnership of husband and wife, and it provides for custody of children, marriage of children differ between wives and husbands. The Code also reflects traditional Muslim family values and religious teachings and cultural perceptions of women and men. For example, the obligations of Muslim husbands are on the conjugal dwelling, while those of Muslim wives are on household affairs.282 In terms of annulment of marriages, the Family Code only allows legal separation without any right to reentry.283 But the Code of Muslim Personal Laws has a provision for divorce.

### 4.2 Labor Division, Decision Making and Autonomy

#### 4.2.1 Crops

67 Many of the tasks related to crop agriculture are carried out by both women and men. Land preparation is usually done by men using tractors or other motorized equipment; however, in some mechanized operation are in most cases taken care of by one man. One survey in 1999-2003 indicated that there was a significant difference between the roles played by men and women in land preparation and seeded preparation, land leveling, seeding, and irrigation systems.284 The participation of both groups in the same task does not necessarily mean that their contributions are equal. The survey showed that women were more involved than men in tasks such as planting rice, weeding, pruning, crop establishment and harvesting.285 The tasks of women as hired hands were pulling and bundling of seedlings, planting of rice, weeding, pruning, and harvesting of crops like corn, cassava, bananas, pineapples, and mangoes; women were employed most for cold room sugarcane and leet for coconut production.286 Women also participated in the tasks related to the preparation of land and harvesting but also participated in land-clearing and earthing-up.287 It was also noted that women spent longer time in the fields than men and that they were in charge of finance-related activities although their labor contribution is not always recognized.

## 68 The consultations on the ground in 2019 showed that almost all tasks were done by both, while following the general pattern above where only one gender was involved. Small variations were observed across regions. Fertilizer may be applied for men or by both women and men (Bicol) or by women alone. One anomaly was in Banaue village of Ifugao province where women were not allowed to apply synthetic fertilizers, but organic ones could be added by women in all villages consulted, aided by men who undertook the physical aspects of sale, such as carrying heavy sacs and driving the vehicle to trading centers. The participation of children after school hours was also noted in some areas. In Bicol province, women's groups continue to gather food from the forests, especially when there is shortage of cultivated food. The task is mainly given to men in some tribes and to women in others.

### 68 The most common livestock in the Philippines are: water buffalo (carabao), cattle, hog, goat, chicken and duck.* Other animals mentioned during the field consultations were: turkey, rabbit, tilapia, mud crab and milk fish for Bicol and dogs for meat in Ifugao. According to the farmers consulted, women are responsible for feeding livestock, especially buffaloes and pigs. Women also take care of cattle exclusively by men in some areas of Bicol for the reason that they could get aggressive when they are not fed regularly. In other areas, however, both men and women participate in gardening and livestock raising, which provide most of their daily provisions and even for special occasions as in weddings or funerals. The involvement of women and children in carabao management was overall moderate, but high in feeding, tethering, and cleaning the animals. Children were responsible for bringing their buffaloes to the watering place.

### 70 Household gardens are critical for household subsistence, and serve as an important source of food security during times of economic hardship. The importance of these gardens increased related to the pandemic, as it provided a source of food during market disruptions, reduced food costs, and lockdowns that could sell excess vegetables. In addition, such gardens are also considered an important tool for strengthening resilience against climate change, as they could otherwise have particularly strong adverse impacts on food security in times of drought or flood. These gardens are usually small-scale, but highly climate-resilient and organic practices within household gardens, including also promoting diverse production systems.

4.2.3 Household and Care Work

71 Unpaid household work – cooking, laundry, cleaning, mending and procuring clothes, and caring for children, the elderly and disabled – is distributed unevenly between girls and boys around the world, and the pattern persists over their lifetime.[198] The total time spent on paid and unpaid work in most countries is more than 40 hours per week. In many developing countries, this work has a direct effect girls’ access to playing, schooling, training, and other public activities,[199] leading to lower social and cognitive development.

72 The women covered by a survey conducted in 2013 in three countries – the Philippines, Uganda and Zimbabwe – spend more time than men on care in any situation, on average 6.5 vs 6.5 hours a day on household work as a primary activity and an average of 11 to 12 hours a day for total.[200] Even when women are not working outside the home, they still spend more time than men: Ugandan women, for example, report that they spend, on average, 6.5 vs 8 hours longer per day than men.[201] Nine percent of men in the Philippines reported no time spent on any kind of household care the day before the survey, which was lower than 32% in Uganda and 47% in Zimbabwe. In Uganda, women spend an average of 59 minutes a day more per week on household work compared to men.[202] Women in the Philippines reported spending 34-75% more time than men on household work.[203] The time wives spent on household work was negatively associated with their husbands’ earnings, and women who earned less were more likely to spend more time on household work, suggesting that higher household income allows employment for household work, bulk of which is done by wives.

73 In another survey of female rice farmers in Indonesia, Myanmar, the Philippines (Southern Luzon) and Vietnam, women spent an average of 10 hours a day on household chores during planting and harvesting seasons, when they needed to work in the field for 3 to 2 months in groups from early April until mid-July.[204] Women in Indonesia and Myanmar described household work as “Most female farmers in Southern Luzon described these seasons as exhausting and tiresome, and have prioritized farm work over household work during the peak seasons.”[205]

### 4.2.4 Supplementary Income

74 Cash generating activities off the farm constitute an important element in the farmers’ livelihood strategy. The income earned by women in the bottom 30% with respect to ranking reflecting the weak contribution of family farms to cash income: wage and salary (47%), entrepreneurial activities (25%), and family sustenance activities (8%).[206]

75 Women grow vegetables, raise poultry and small livestock, engage in aquaculture and apiculture, and sell their produce at local markets. They also engage in informal trade and work in other’s fields.[207] Men are usually engaged in construction work,[208] hunting or fishing.[209]

Construction jobs are preferred to work as hired hands on the farm, because the wage is higher. In dire cases, they migrate overseas for work.

76 Many female farmers expressed their wish to earn more through off-farm work to meet the household needs,128 mostly by engaging in what they like to do – such as making preserves and crafts – and selling the products. Their lack of access to marketing knowledge appeared an obstacle in making this happen. The women were not aware that they could learn how to make a product, how to read market trends; find appropriate markets; control quality; present the product in an attractive manner; and utilize the income for direct sale to consumers.

### 4.2.5 Decision-Making and Autonomy

#### Overall
When a family member’s earnings are high, she has larger autonomy and influence on household decisions.129 While “wives’” headship does not grant them autonomy and finally in decision making, it ensures that they are involved in decision making.

78 According to the National Demographic and Health Survey in 2013 revealed that very few men in the Philippines controlled the incomes earned by their employed wives aged 15–49.130 In terms of the education level of the wife, those with a secondary school or higher education had more control over care they had no education.131 The rate constantly decreased as the wives acquired more education.

79 In contrast, less than 10% of men were the main decision-makers on spending of their own earnings, regardless of the relative importance of their incomes compared to their wives’.132 The income earned by women was used for household expenditures. Women controlled about half the households for the wives’ earnings and by more than60% for the husbands’ earnings.133 Some were responsible for deciding on the sale of crops and livestock. In such cases, they kept the earnings.

80 With respect to the type of expenditures, day-to-day decisions on household expenditures (food, clothing, health care, education, etc.) were made by women, while major expenditures were jointly decided upon with their husbands.134 Women’s control over income and expenditure and husbands could not be reversed was it was the husband’s opinion that prevailed.135 The priorities for the expenses were: agricultural inputs, children’s education, daily household needs, food and transportation.

81 Assets: Decisions on the purchase and sale of land, house or major family assets are made jointly by wives and husbands, regardless of the formal asset ownership.* However to make final decisions appears to rest with men.

82 Farming: The vast majority of the persons who take the technical and administrative responsibility of managing agricultural holdings, or agricultural operators, were men in 2009.** The agriculture survey data also indicate that women are not involved in decision making on farming.*** The discussions with the farmers indicated that the decisions are jointly made by wives and husbands, only when there is a need to make an urgent decision.**** In the case of rice production, it is said that if the first born child who grows up to make the decisions for the family, all the while the title of the head of the family follows a patriarchal system.* In Ifugao, it is a woman of prestige in each village who rituals and ceremonies are performed on her behalf in her sickness.*** Moreover she lasts for a day to mark the beginning of the rice planting season.****

83 Health: Slightly over half of the women currently married and aged 15-49 years old reported that they were involved in decision making about their health, according to the National Demographic and Health Survey in 2013.* Only 3.3% of the women reported it was the husbands who made the decisions on the women's health.* Among four issues surveyed, the issue most frequently discussed by wives with their husbands was the purchase of major household purchases; own health care – the health issue had the second most participation of women after purchases of daily household needs.

84 *Note: It is important to note that the proportion of women who participated in decision making on their health increased with age.* Women's cash earning status had little impact on their participation in decision making about women's health.*** There was virtually no difference between the urban and rural areas regarding this issue.****

85 Violence: The proportion of women aged 14-59 who were subjected to physical or sexual violence in the last 12 months was 7.1% for the Philippines in 2013.* As most countries do not have the relevant data, it is difficult to compare this figure with other countries.*** According to the study by McKinsey Global Institute concluded that violence against women was mostly at medium level in the country.* As of 2016, 17% of Philippine women were subject to physical or sexual violence by their intimate partner in their lifetime.****

86 Among the Philippine girls aged 15 to 19 years, about 10% had experienced any physical violence since age 15 and about 4% experienced any physical violence in the 12 month prior to the survey in 2005-2013. In recent years, the Philippines has become a global hub for child pornography online and major destination for global child sex tourism. About 6% of girls aged 15 to 19 years ever reported forced sexual intercourse or any other forced sexual acts, according to a survey in 2004-2013.

87 The National Demographic and Health Survey in 2008 and 2013 found that women’s attitudes toward violence against women were more positive than those of men. In 2013, 59% currently married women aged 15–49 agreed that their husbands are justified in hitting or beating them if the wives burned the food, argued with him, went out without telling him, neglected the children, or refused to have sex with him. This is a significant decrease from 70% of women who agreed in 2008 (N=313) and it is considered that it their husbands hit or beat them if they neglected their children. The lower prevalence of this attitude was also found among women aged 45–49 years old (56%) and men (8%). There was very little difference among the age groups in thinking that the use of violence was justified for each of the above five cases.

88 Currently married women aged 15-49 and living in the rural areas were more tolerant of partner violence than those in the urban areas, more than twice in terms of proportion.### 4.3 Agricultural Resources and Services: Access and Ownership

#### 4.3.1 Land, House and Livestock

Republic Act 6057, the Comprehensive Agrarian Reform Law (CARL) of 1988, and Republic Act 9713, the Magna Carta of Women together give equal rights to women and men in land ownership and management. However, despite these laws, women still face many challenges in accessing their share of the land that they cultivate as a family. In many cases, the prevailing perception that men are the primary landowners, coupled with lack of awareness of these rights and weak administrative practices have limited women’s access to land. The Comprehensive Agrarian Reform Law (CARL) and other related laws, which supersede the Civil Code, requires wives to obtain their husband's consent during the course of transferring or alienating any property. This means that if a woman wants to sell her share of land she was to be granted land under the Certificate of Land Ownership Award (CLOA) of the Comprehensive Agrarian Reform Program, they cannot have any source of income other than farming, must have been farming

the land in question for five years or more, and are responsible for all taxes and surveys on the land.[287] These conditions may be difficult for a woman alone to satisfy.[288]

90 Land ownership statistics stratified by age and gender reveal that less than 1% of married women aged 15-19 owned land by themselves and slightly over 1% owned land jointly in 2018.[289] As women age, the sole and joint ownerships both increased.[290] More married women owned land in the rural areas (6.3%) than urban areas (4%).[291] In the case of married women who own land as a married woman owned land by herself, suggesting the existence of a class akin to landed gentry.[292] These native statistics also reveal that 85% of all landowners are men and 15% are indigenous women.

91 Under the lack of ownership among women, decisions on land use are made jointly, and those over land utilization rights or sale are not apt to be excepted.[293] It is important to note, however, whether the voices of wives and husbands carry equal weight, or who has the final say when agreements cannot be reached. In some cases, the husband's decision prevails in a case of disagreement between the spouses over use of marital property[294] while in other cases, the wife's decision prevails.[295] The latter scenario improves with conferment of land rights as, the rights open door to agricultural credit, which in turn encourages them to assert themselves when dealing with government offices.[296]

92 As of 2006, 11.2 million people were engaged in agriculture, of which 8.5 million were landholders and 2.7 million were tenants or sharecroppers.[297] The majority of the latter are women, in favor of the landowner.[298] Only the male farmers are paid, but the entire family mobilized to complete the work,[299] as was the case during the Spanish colonial period.

93 Housing follows a similar trend by age groups as with land, progressing from 95.6% of married women aged 15-19 owning a house to 20.8% of married women aged 45-49 in the same condition.[300] The urban-rural difference was reversed from the land ownership.[301] The higher the wealth, the more likely it is that one owns a house alone or joint, pointing[302] suggesting absentee house owners or female-headed households.

94 Official statistics on livestock ownership is unavailable, but appears to largely follow the pattern of other assets, namely joint management.[303]

relevance, contrary to the officers who were transferred to the LGUs from the Department of Agriculture in accordance with the devolution and are diminishing in number as they retire.123 Many government and non-government entities are involved in extension services, leading to redundancy.124 If not strategic confusion, but at the same time, the number of officers on the ground is considerably less than what it was before devolution.125 The result is that there may be as many as eight governmental extension workers servicing 1,000-1,500 farmers.126 The upland farmers seemed left to themselves and were not able to access the services offered in the areas. The majority of the agriculture extension officers met by the mission team were women.

96 In many regions, it is the women who participate in most agricultural meetings organized by the local extension office.127 Men prefer to work in the field.128 Considering such meetings part of social activities, they are not considered as important as other activities.129 However, since local extension services are not considered important enough to warrant the presence of men, who are also the farmers’ leaders and decision makers, it is left for women to attend these meetings.130 This is not always the case; in some areas, men are more open to new ideas than women.131 In other areas, however, women are more open to new ideas than men.132 The Department of Agriculture see this gender divide on extension services a problem.133 But men become increasingly left behind in new developments in agriculture. In Northern Mindanao, the opposite situation is observed: women are less open to new ideas and are not as willing to learn.134 Women have got a great amount of work to do at home, and consequently women remained untapped.135

97 The little information that exists on livestock extension services in the Philippines imply that there are no gender differences in livestock management workers on crops. Women and children who are engaged in livestock management often obtain necessary knowledge mostly by trial and error as well as from the previous generation in the family.136

### 4.3.2 Climate Information Services

98 Women in general are less educated than men in the Philippines, including the rural areas, and there is a gender-based obstacle in terms of information acquisition. Agriculture is managed jointly by women and men, and no gender differentiated climate information service exists. According to one survey carried out in Southern Luzon, although almost all women and men reported usefulness of the climate information services, only 20% of farmers were able to provide an opinion on how much the information boosted the agricultural production.137 Other gender differences that may
