4.3.4 Agricultural Credit

9\. A variety of subsidized direct credit programmes continues to be offered by different government agencies to smallholders. They are essentially of one kind; the main difference is in the social and economic characteristics of the target group. Most of these programmes have been fully or almost fully implemented through the Land Bank, and cater to the same strata of farmers with almost the same terms and conditions. The majority of the programmes target women of agricultural households who are engaged in income generating activities.

100\. Farmers mostly rely on the so-called S-56, which gives way to up-to 20-day pre-interest for a 100-day borrowing, for ease of access and speed of obtaining the cash needed. The second most popular programme is the Agricultural Credit Corporation (ACC) loan, which has a longer term but stipulates that the poorest farmers still do not have access to formal credit and that documentation requirements should be lessened and processes simplified to reach the poorest.

101\. Credit is used mostly for agricultural inputs and credit decisions are taken in mutual agreements between the farmer and the lender. The most common issues related to household expenses as well as the tasks that they spend more time for, and men about the needs for the tasks that they engage in and the amount of money needed to be spent on them.

102\. Women are not registered under the Registry System for Basic Sectors in Agriculture (RSBSA), and Agricultural Credit Corporation (ACC) loans. The law does not require the husband’s consent or need to authorize the wives to borrow. Many banks prefer to have the signature or consent of the husband in financial transactions.

103\. Some still demand the co-signature of male partners for financial contracts, which is a violation of the 2004 Civil Code that states that women are equal to men, expressly confirming that women have the same capacity to enter into contracts as men. The bank notes that women who do not have their own land or other assets, are disadvantaged compared to men.

104\. Finally, Article 2590 of the Civil Code stipulates that a married woman may guarantee an obligation without the husband’s consent, but shall not thereby bind the husband. In the Philippines, however, many indigenous peoples, people are undocumented and hence, automatically do not have access to formal credit.

### 4.3.5 Agricultural Insurance

102 The Philippine Crop Insurance Corp (PCIC) was established in 1978 by Presidential Decree 1647 as an arm of the Department of Agriculture to provide insurance protection to farmers against losses from natural calamities, plant diseases and pest infestations of crops. PCIC offers insurances for rice, corn, livestock, fisheries, non-crop agricultural assets, and more than 50 high-value commercial crops. The crop insurance program is based on a risk-sharing arrangement between the farmer, ground, cabbage, carrot, cassava, coconut, coffee, commercial trees, cotton, garlic, mango, mung bean, onion, potato, soybean, sugarcane, sweet potato, tea, tobacco, tree nuts (almond, cashew, macadamia), white potato, etc.) and runs a scheme on accident and dismemberment security scheme. The insurances for rice, corn, livestock, fisheries, non-crop agricultural assets are subject to the occurrence of natural disasters (typhoon, flood, drought, earthquake and volcanic eruption), plant disease, pest infestation and accidental fire. The livestock insurance is for cattle, carabao, horse, swine, goat, poultry and game from domesticated animals. The fishery insurance covers all types of fish and shrimp. Crop insurance protection against losses in fish, fishery and aquatic products caused by natural calamities and fortuitous accidents.

103 Many farmers consider the documentation requirements before and after the event beyond their capacity, and cited as the main reason for not purchasing a POC insurance. Some farmers purchase a crop insurance because it is required for taking out loans. A study found that the level of education, income, and membership in a credit union or other financial organization are significant factors in farmers' decision to subscribe to a crop-insurance policy.

104 The microfinance institutions recognized the economic fragility of their clients and the need for insurance protection against natural disasters and plant diseases. However, they were not able to provide formal insurance coverage because of high costs. The results were proliferation of informal insurance and financial risks. The establishment of a regulatory framework on microinsurance and a National Strategy for Microinsurance in 2009 diffused the risks, and by 2015, the number of insured farmers through PCIC microinsurance counted 30 million people who had been covered by formal risk protection in 2014 and 2015.

105 A study of Bangladeshi farmers showed that women were more reluctant to take out any weather-indexed crop insurance compared to men. The difference could not be explained by gender differences in risk and time preferences or decision-making power, but for lower levels of trust in microfinance institutions.

4.3.5 Mobile Phone and the Internet

106 According to the International Telecommunication Union (ITU), the Philippines ranked 12th among 38 countries in the Asia and the Pacific with respect to ICT Development Index in 2017, slightly below the world average.* A good majority of the households had a mobile phone in 2017 with largely unlimited access to the Internet. The country has one of the highest penetration rates globally. It is reported that virtually the entire country is covered by 2G mobile connection and that over two-thirds of the population is covered by broadband mobile connection.* The Philippines is one of the few countries that recorded a significant increase in the number of fixed broadband subscriptions per 100 persons in Asia and the Pacific in 2017.*

107 The Inclusive Internet Index (the IIX) of the United Nations, Unit placed the Philippines at 68th among the 95 countries surveyed in 2017, based on availability of infrastructure required for access and levels of internet usage), affordability (cost of access relative to income and the level of competition in the internet marketplace), relevance (existence and extent of local language content) and sustainability (Especially access to the Internet, including skills, cultural acceptance, and supporting policy).* 

108 A survey carried out in 2019 indicated that, among the Filipinos aged 18 and above, 46% used the internet at least once a week. This was up from 35% in 2006.* In the same year, the proportion of internet users in the urban areas was 54%, while it was only 29% for rural areas (people living without any kind of connection still existed).* 

109 The gender gap in mobile internet use is considerably wider than the mobile ownership gap in regions where women are especially disadvantaged. For the least developed countries, where one in seven women is using the internet compared with one in five men.* With literacy and education, Philippine women are less likely than men to have internet access at home. According to the mission of the UNDP, only 20% of Filipino women had a mobile phone for their use, but some had 36 or later standard.* All households had access to the functionalities of the 3G or 4G phone, as at least one member of the family (usually a youth) had a smart phone and a Facebook account.* It has been observed in the Philippines that women are less likely than men to use the internet more than once per week.*

### 43
#### 4.3.7 Other Sources of Information

The media in the Philippines is among the oldest and the least restricted in Asia [10]. There are radio programs that allow listeners to call-in to voice their concerns about their neighborhood with the goal to prompt government officials to respond and take action.[10] However, as commercial entities, the media is focused on profits, which leads to intense competition with one another for audience share. This competition often results in biased reporting that does not benefit the interests that control the media.[10] The content is also highly partisan, as people with interests and means are able to buy airtime or space to promote their views. Furthermore, the media often engage in self-promotion and self-censorship.[10] The People Power Revolution in 1986 – the overthrow by the people of Ferdinand Marcos’ regime – was a watershed moment for the media, as it was the first time that the press had been allowed to operate freely. Environmental and women’s issues, science and technology, civil society and many other areas that had been largely ignored started to attract more attention on the media.[10] The digital revolution has also changed the landscape of the media; it is still rarely covered by local[10] national media, except during disasters and other calamities.[10]

The national newspapers are in English and owned by prominent families with powerful connections within the society. Their messages are generally conservative.[10] Some of the thirty dailies in the Greater Manila area broadcast news in both Filipino and English; three in Chinese; while seventeen tabloids are in Filipino and two in English; the broadcasters cater to the English-educated

### 113 Regional newspapers are in English or the local language and mostly cover local events and personalities. Several popular tabloids are written in Filipino, and hence are more widely accessible, but very few Filipinos read them regularly. The only national newspaper that is available on a daily basis is available only in provincial capitals and no major leftist periodical is in print. Feuding politicians employ their own newspapers to attack each other. Some of the most popular radio stations have journalists who worked for local radio, television or newspapers. In 2018, Philippines was ranked the fifth by the Global Impunity Index, which calculates the number of unsolved journalist murders as a percentage of each country's population. The country currently has 46 unsolved cases.

115 The latest Functional Literacy, Education and Mass Media Survey from 2013 indicated that more than 80% of households owned a mobile phone and little under 80% owned a television.107 The print media is the most influential on a national scale, and the radio plays an important role locally.108 Radio is also used for news and information in rural areas.109 The programmes are generally in the vernacular and on local issues with little coverage of foreign news.110 This interest in local news is reflected by the fact that 75% of respondents said they would turn to Facebook for information on natural disasters.111 Television, on the other hand, is the main source of entertainment.112 Most television programs are in Filipino although regional stations also broadcast in their local languages.113 The most popular television program was a news magazine that had an average of 40 percentage points won as posters or as information sources" (Figure 1). The choice of media did not appear to be related to age.

116 Despite the fact that the oldest local newspaper was established by Ibaloy, a tribal minority in the Cordillera region in 1947, only some of the indigenous peoples, who constitute 20% of the population, have access to local newspapers or radio.114 The majority of them rely on sporadic and often misconceived reports from the national media.115

### 4.3 Access to Information

117 Women in general tend to lack mobility because of their responsibilities related to household chores and caregiving, and hence, have less access to markets than men. Although the Philippine women are bound by the same tasks, women accompany men to the markets and it is the woman who makes most decisions about what will be purchased.116 Women also play a key role in negotiation.117 Men are relegated to the tasks of driving and carrying the heavy loads, which acknowledges their greater physical strength.118 The division of labor between men and women can be quite different if the merchandise is produced by women alone; involves intensive processing by women; or requires a standard staple or semi-staple food, e.g., crafts and processed agricultural products. No analysis appears to have been carried out on this difference.

### 4.4 Water and Sanitation

118 About 70% of the population had access to basic drinking water in 2015, and the country met the Millennium Development Goal (MDG) on access to safe drinking water.119 However, it was ranked 33rd


---
**Page 219**
---

out of 148 countries according to the five aspects on water security advanced by the Asian-Development Bank - household water security economic water security urban water security environmental water security resilience to water-related diseases - which may be due to lack of data.202 Despite the rich water resources and numerous programs and projects, some areas need additional water sources and improved infrastructure.203 It is yet to be achieved; the sector is fragmented and no apex body exists to oversee and coordinate.204

119 Rural water supply has received less attention compared to urban water supply.205 In 2017, 95% of rural households had access to improved drinking water sources as opposed to 98% of urban households.206 About four-fifths of Filipino households had water on the premises: 91% of urban and 73% of rural households. Three percent of households traveled 30 minutes or longer to fetch water.207

120 The most common source of drinking water was bottled water or a well/spring system.208 Boiling was the most common method for treating drinking water, but only one-third of urban and less than half of rural households used that method regularly.209 Only 15% of the households made use of an appropriate treatment method for their drinking water.210 No gender segregated data or analysis is available on water supply.

121 The MDG target for access to basic sanitation was not met in the Philippines, but among the households around the urban areas, 98% had access to improved sanitation systems and 5% still did not have access to a basic sanitary toilet facility as of 2015.211 The government announced subsidy for establishing sewerage systems in barangays with less than 300 households and for providing water supply services up to 600 households.212 Thousands of schools do not have sanitation facilities,213 which could be discouraging girls from attending school.214 Urban areas had a higher sanitation coverage rate (85%) compared to rural areas (79%). The urban sanitation coverage rate for girls was slightly lower than that for boys. Urban sanitation is considered in worse shape than rural sanitation.215

122 Over three-quarters of households used improved toilet facilities, which are non-shared facilities that prevent people from coming into contact with human waste. Shared toilet facilities of acceptable quality were available to 40% of the population and shared facilities of unacceptable quality to another 3%.216 Five percent of the households did not use any toilet facility.217 The mortality rate attributed to exposure to unsafe water, sanitation, and hygiene was estimated at about 5 per 100,000 population; in 2014, placing the Philippines 15th out of 21 countries in the Western Pacific region.

123 It has been reported that the improved and equitable delivery of basic infrastructure for water supply, sewerage and sanitation is hindered by inadequate financing, low technical capabilities of small service providers, lack of institutional capacity to manage water resources, weak regulatory framework for water supply and wastewater treatment facilities; and institutional challenges (such as lengthy approval processes).

# 4.5 Health

## 4.5.1 Commonwealth

The latest available statistics indicate that the health of an average person in the Philippines has more in common with that in the countries with lower incomes. The mental health of the population appeared in a much better shape. The suicide mortality rate, 3.2 per 100,000 in 2016, was the lowest among the 45 countries included.

Awareness on health or access to health services is low: Sixty-six percent of the population was covered by second-dose immunization of measles-containing vaccine before the national recommended age of two years. The ratio of physicians and dentists per 100,000 population and midwifery personnel per 1,000 population among the 27 countries included in the World Health Organization (WHO) data, the latest available data from 2007–2016. Natural disasters, which are very likely to increase in the Future because of climate change, have been a major cause of death and disability in the Philippines. The country had the third highest average death rate due to natural disasters per 100,000 population in 2012–2016, after Nepal and Saint Vincent and Grenadines.

The World Health Organization (WHO) applied the Sixty-six percent of the population was covered by second-dose immunization of measles-containing vaccine before the national recommended age of two years. The ratio of physicians and dentists per 100,000 population and midwifery personnel per 1,000 population among the 27 countries included in the World Health Organization (WHO) data, the latest available data from 2007–2016. Natural disasters, which are very likely to increase in the Future because of climate change, have been a major cause of death and disability in the Philippines. The country had the third highest average death rate due to natural disasters per 100,000 population in 2012–2016, after Nepal and Saint Vincent and Grenadines.

The World Health Organization (WHO) applied its approach to the provision of health services at the devolution of health services to the local level. However, the availability of health facilities and services differs greatly throughout the country, and local governments are not always able to provide adequate services. The Philippines has a national health insurance Act of 1995, in which only 54% of the total health expenditure was out of pocket expenses and 46% from public sources. This is lower than the average for low- and middle-income countries in countries which are in the same league as the Philippines is 5% or more of GDP (2016) the national health expenditure in the country was 4.4% of GDP in 2016.

Philippines ranked the 42nd highest among 149 countries in gender ratio related to health and survival, which means that women are better off than men in healthy life expectancy. Other statistics, such as child nutrition status and mortality rates, show that girls are slightly better off than boys and that the lack of service affects both equally (Table S).

2017 The rate for adolescent women declined from 74 to 63 in rural areas, but increased from 40 to 52 in urban areas between 2003 and 2011. Women in urban areas and educated women tended to have their first children later.

### 131
Fifteen percent of adolescent Filipino women aged 15-19 in 2017 and 2% of women less than age 15 in 2017 ever had sexual intercourse. Teenager from the poorest households were more than four times likely to be pregnant, those with three or fewer children were 17% versus 44%.

### 132
Among adolescent females aged 15-19 who had a child by age 20 in 2017, The proportion of women who had begun childbearing was lower in urban than in rural areas for both age groups, 15-19 and 15-19. Teenage childbearing is most common in Davao (18%), Northern Mindanao (16%) and Cebu (14%).

### 133
Contraception was used by 37% of married adolescent women in 2013, up from 26% in 2003 and 2008. The methods used were almost equally divided between traditional and modern, while the adolescent girls who had never been pregnant were more likely to use modern methods than those lower than the other, compared to other women of reproductive age. The married adolescents had higher unmet needs for contraception than other age groups. Nearly one-third of recent births to women aged 15-19 were unintended.

### 134
Only 23% of adolescent women could correctly identify a woman's fertile period. The proportion of spontaneous abortions among households engaged in continuous pesticide application was almost double that of those who did not use pesticides (6%) compared with non-users (3%). Birth defects was nearly four times higher than among IPUH households.

### 135
The high maternal mortality rate of the country indicates women's and girls' unsatisfactory awareness and access to basic and reproductive health care. It has been reported that the government's practical information on reproductive health exists on the internet, but difficult to find.

## Coping with Negative Shocks

### Social Protection
During the past decade, the design and delivery of social protection have improved thanks to the adoption of a social protection strategy and operational framework by the Philippine Statistics Authority.


---
**Page 223**
---

National Economic Development Authority. The objectives of the policy respond to the needs of the population, covering short-term difficulties from disasters in consumption and income, among others. The Philippine Development Plan 2017-2022 states that social protection is for building the socioeconomic resilience of the poor and those recently escaped from poverty. It is with the Department of Social Welfare and Development (DSWD) that the government has been working for implementation of the largest social protection transfer program, community-driven development program on disaster risk reduction and recovery.

127 In 2008, the DSWD was evaluated that the overall level of effort and institutional development on social protection is high in the Philippines with a coherent architecture around the three typical pillars (social assistance, social insurance and interventions). They also acknowledged the continuous improvement in the quality of service delivery. The government agencies were involved in 65 social protection programs and projects. Each social protection program was also individually evaluated by the DSWD. The number of people covered under these programs has been increasing in recent years, but as 43% of GDP in 2017, it was still quite lower than the world average of 27% for that same year. The social safety net expenditures were dominated by social pension program.

138 The administrative structure that made the DSWD responsible for disaster relief as well as the definition of social assistance programmes allowed the department to integrate disaster relief into the safety net system. This was also reflected in the 2015 World Bank report. It was exemplified in its rapid use of the national conditional cash transfer programme for the poor (Pantawid Pamilyang Pilipino Program) in the aftermath of Typhoon Haiyan, which hit some of the poorest provinces in the country, and affected the main sources of livelihood: agriculture, fisheries, and tourism. In 2012, the average household income in the severely affected provinces was only PHP358 per month (US$7.9). The economic recovery from the disaster was slow as the region was largely dependent on agriculture and remittances from overseas.

The Republic of the Philippines: Social Protection Review and Assessment.


---
**Page 224**
---

139 Social protection programmes, including those for sustainable livelihoods, can be expanded at a short notice to cover large numbers of additional beneficiaries in response to disasters. Moreover, social care or social services programmes respond immediately to individuals and families in crisis situations, including disasters. They are fairly small scale and operated by many nongovernmental agencies (NGOs) and community-based organizations (CBOs). These NGOs also provide a wide range of other programs that support the poor, or provide direct transfers to households: community-driven development; microfinance; water, sanitation, and hygiene infrastructure; by communities; housing assistance programmes; and scholarship programmes.

140 Borrowing Cash Farmers encounter economic hardships when the unfavorable natural conditions result in crop damage. They try to overcome the shortage in cash by selling livestock, borrowing from informal money lenders or taking up part-time jobs in teaching, driving, motorcycle renting, food and meat processing, etc. The most common informal money lender is the local moneylender (e.g., Banco Santiago de Libon (BSL)), Center for Agriculture and Rural Development (CARD), a non-governmental organization (NGO) based in Bangladesh. The interest rate charged by the lenders varied from 2%/month in the case of BSL, 10%/month if a lack of fee for borrowing 1,500 somos in case of traders, to 20%/year if it's a loan from CARD. Farmers who borrowed money from these informal lenders had to present an interview and a photo submission and that a loan is bonded with an insurance. Others who were present at the mission consultations (28 January-6 February 2019) reported that many small farmers seemed unaware of such credit arrangements and complained about the onerous paperwork required by formal credit institutions.

141 On Farm Changes in farming were noticed by all farmers that the mission team consulted. The most common changes were: seasonal and rainfall patterns, which have become irregular and extreme; higher ambient and water temperatures on average; water shortage at the tail end of irrigation canals; increased soil erosion; reduced crop yields due to drought; increased plant mortality and lower quality of crops; higher number of familial pests; emergence of new pests; impossibility of planting certain crops (e.g., bananas, papayas); and increased incidence of sheep animal mortality from disease. The farmers reported coping with such situations by: application of increased amounts of pesticides; planting of coconut trees as windbreakers and for shade; early planting of root crops to avoid the heat; use of plastic bags to cover seeds or seedlings to avoid waste to prevent clogging of irrigation canals. Some indigenous female farmers have been in search of organic pesticides.

142 Other common coping strategies include: planting around the homestead of root crops (tapioca, sweet potato, taro or gabi) and others such as banana, mango or chayote, coconut trees, okra, papayas, jackfruit, banana, lemon, pili nut, Gmelina and coconut); foraging of root crops and other

indigenous food items, animal raising in the backyard (chicken, pig, ducks), vegetable cultivation on the upper bond portion of the rice field (tomato, bitter gourd or ampalaya, pepper, koh choy or periau, coconua).** In Camarines Sur, craft making by both indigenous and non-indigenous women is widely spread as income supplement.** The use of native seeds (which can be of export quality), but the yield is low (usually less than USD$2 per day) and the plants are more susceptible to pests and mosaic diseases.** Similarly, Agta women make brooms from coconut-folns, and collect and sell the leaves of banana trees for weaving mats.** The use of native seeds in rice production has been adopted and riverine areas as well as synchronized planting of rice for integrated pest management have been adopted so some indigenous villages.

### 4.7 Indigenous Women in Agriculture

Approximately 18 to 37 million indigenous peoples, **[About 13-17% of the total population]** live in the Philippines. In Mindanao and the Visayas, the societies in the Philippines are more than often characterized as patriarchal in a bratukhene**[sic]** and indigenous women treated as one group, but the conditions differ greatly by region.** The most significant difference is between the Cordillera and the Visayas which makes the next largest difference in the role of status of women, but in Mindanao, intra-regional variations are also large.** According to the indigenous women in the Philippines, their rights and crucial roles as custodians of biodiversity and natural resources have been ignored.

Indigenous knowledge contains an understanding of nature that have been tested by time, proven to be sustainable, and able to contribute to limiting the effects of hazards.** Women tend to be the ones who collect seeds from wild plants, save them in their homes, plant them, cultivate and store the best seeds and more and more men take up non-agricultural livelihood. Indigenous practices are gaining recognition on the field of disaster risk reduction but at the same time, it is under constant threat of being lost as indigenous communities are losing their traditional knowledge.** It is also noted that the indigenous communities are no longer self-sufficient in food, due to diminishing land at their disposal and increasing competition for resources with non-indigenous farmers in their traditional food production systems, as they increasingly recognize their contribution to community resilience.

145. As is the case for any indigenous peoples in the world, agriculture and forests are tightly woven together and into their socio-cultural lives. The preponderance of uplands accord special importance to the role of indigenous forest management, which is integrated together with agriculture into landscape and natural resources management. Indigenous women’s role in agricultural production and forest resource use and management has been largely overlooked or not readily available despite their strong involvement in collection of non-timber forest products.

146. The forests in the Philippines were considered command and governed by communal laws before colonialism, but after Spanish colonization, the land was divided into private holdings. It was following the Second World War, the country turned to its forests to support an industrialization programme.20 In the 1930s, the timber and forest product industry became one of the top foreign exchange earners for the Philippines. The government’s policy on forestry was to protect the forest cover while allowing selective logging. However, this policy did not work out well in practice; by 1960, the national forest cover diminished from over 4 million ha in 1950 to 3.86 million ha in 1960 and continued to decline.

147. From the late 1970s to the early 1980s, a movement was born among academics and NGOs to halt deforestation, soil erosion and declining productivity of upland agriculture based on community management.21 As of early 2009, the government has recognized under the title of community forest management (CFM) the rights of indigenous peoples and local communities over forests by concessions or other leases and indigenous peoples’ ancestral domain, in addition to multiple-use and buffer zones. The CFM programme is now being implemented on a large scale throughout the country; more than 24 million Filipinos in the early 2000s, making them major actors in the management of forests, biodiversity and watersheds.

148. Indigenous women’s contribution to forest management is largely undocumented. Their participation in forest management is estimated substantial, considering that women are usually the main foragers and that most foragers are not passive harvesters, but actively manage the environment by sowing tree seeds, ingesting grasses, burning vegetation for new plant growth, replanting roots and so on.22

### 4.3.2 Agta Women

149. The Australo people are the original inhabitants of the archipelago, comprising of some 32 ethnolinguistic groups. They live in seven islands, mainly Luzon, and the population was estimated at about 50,000 as of the early 1980s. Of this number, only a small percentage project; they reside in Cagayan, Bicol and Northern Mindanao.23 They refer to themselves as Agta, Ata, Mga, Ate, Haya or Ati. The Agta are traditionally nomadic hunter-gatherers, whose main

## Hunting targets are wild pig and deer. Both women and men engage in hunting, men usually alone and women in a team with other women or men. Women's preferred hunting method is to drive dogs and use long knives for killing. Men use bows and arrows, which may harm the dogs.

Some women hunt with bows and arrows, typically smaller than those used by men. Women sometimes hunt with spears, but this is rare. They usually hunt small game such as small animals per hunting than all-male groups or solo men, and the groups comprised of both women and men though the majority of the hunting is done by men. Some women prefer to engage in freshwater hunting because they participate in fewer ventures.

Agta women do not show innate repugnance for killing animals. They kill small game such as birds on a close range. Meat used to be exchanged with starch foods from the lowlanders.

### 150 Fishing is a dry season activity, in which women, youth and adult participate. Women are noted for their skills in spearfishing, both in rough, deep waters and shallow streams. It is the women who gather fish eggs and other edible parts of aquatic plants during the dry season by women and men.

While mollusk gathering, a rainy season activity, is usually conducted by women, it is not as important to them as fishing or hunting. Women are noted for their skills in spearfishing, both in rough, deep waters and shallow streams. It is the women who gather fish eggs and other edible parts of aquatic plants during the dry season.

### 151 No forest management rule of the Agta has been documented, but they sustainably managed the forests by harvesting only what was needed for their immediate use and never more than what was needed and to share it widely with others in the community. Their culture guides them to eschew saving or hoarding material goods. Many no longer live by the principle, as they realize that they cannot but integrate into the market economy if they wish so by virtue.

153 In Casiguran Valley, the rapidly increasing population of non-Agta people had increased the population density to over 1.3 persons/km² before the Second World War to 40 persons/km² in the mid 1960s.152 Multinational corporations bulldozed roads through their settlements and hunting and fishing grounds, opening up areas previously inaccessible for settlement. The government also opened up the areas which previously were inaccessible and attracted more extractive activities, for which the Agta are not well suited.153 In the 1970s, the government introduced a number of new technologies such as methods of towland Fibilinos (with firearms, dynamite, pesticides and electric rods), transistor radio, commercial liquor, and commercial rattan buyer exposure facilitated by a road built by the government. The Agta were also exposed to other innovations such as matches, which they took from the outsiders to use for firearms; hunting, instead of the traditional bow and arrow, in the 1970s the military commenced training them in the use of firearms.154 In the 1980s, the government built roads each to capital.156 The Agta people on the coast were displaced by tourism and infrastructure development.157 The land they farmed was distributed to immigrant families under Marcos government’s land distribution program.

154 Their strategies to live with the nature and its seasonal fluctuations – such as hunting and fishing – were rendered useless as the main means of livelihood by deforestation, population pressure and increased competition from non-Agta people.158 The Agta have been forced into cash crop farming or rattan gathering for commercial buyers. The daily wage for Agta labor in 1983 was the equivalent of $0.72 (US$).159 In the early 1980s, the Agta were paid as much as US$4 per day on their ancestral lands.160 As wage-laborers for the lowland farmers, women dominated all tasks except clearing at mid of 1980s.162 The growing importance of agriculture signifies the increasing role of women in livelihood.

155. Upper: Intensive personal interactions, loss of social status, lowered self-image, and diminishing faith in Agta culture - all taken to a toll on the health of Agta women.[38] For those living close to farming communities, the workload has also increased.[39] In the mid 1980s, an Agta woman over age of 45 had given 7.3 live births on average; half of the children were expected to die, and life expectancy was only 26 years.[40] The leading causes of death among Agta women were malaria, pneumonia, homicide, leprosy, alcoholism, and, among women, also complications from childbirth.[41] In the late 1980s, the average age at first marriage for Agta women was 25 years;[42] this is common in low-wives by non-Agta immigrants, contributing to Agta population decline.[36]

156. The influx of outsiders is exposing the Agta to more diseases and has resulted in environmental degradation, which has led to further health deterioration from consumption of polluted water and food. In 1980, the incidence of malaria among Agta women living on the island was already high,[43] and in the mid 2000s approximately 34% of adults and 17% of children were malnourished.[44] The prevalence of alcoholism is also increasing;[45] it is not only a social problem, but indirectly, but substantially, alcohol contributes to nutritional insecurity and livelihood degradation.[46]

157. Migration and social subordination of the Agta by lowlanders were widespread as of mid 1980s.[47] Women often fell sexually abused, and men were cheated and scorned.[48] Women were said to never feel safe from molestation unless Agta men were present.[49] While Agta themselves consider alcoholism a social problem, they do not have the resources or political power to address it and consider men the appropriate negotiators and decision makers.[50] During negotiations, men were more likely to be removed from decisions than women.[51] Women are removed from decisions over time; their influence continues to erode as intermarriage with non-Agta increases and an Agta man is born to a non-Agta woman.[52]

158. Although the Agta have not fared well in the conditions imposed by modern society, they have valuable knowledge for coping with natural hazards, especially typhoons, floods, storm surges and landslides.[53] One example of Agta's deep knowledge of extreme weatheres and adaptations is their ability to predict the arrival of typhoons. They are able to predict the arrival of typhoons and storm surges and manages to resist very strong winds. The frame of the house is tied together with rattan and abaca (dried bark of abaca) using special skills and without iron nails. Furnish whitewash on the walls and roof, which can be washed off by rain. They also predict the arrival of typhoons through natural signs and plant few months prior a root crop, camote

# 4.7.3 Women and Agriculture

## The Cordillera Autonomous Region (CAR)

159. The Cordillera Autonomous Region (CAR) consists overwhelmingly of indigenous peoples; 99% of the population who are not Agta. According to various statistics, such as functional literacy, net enrolment rates in secondary school, and access to health services, CAR is one of the poorest regions in the Philippines. However, the women in CAR are not any worse off than other regions. On some accounts they fared better in 2017: The median age of women giving birth for the first time was the highest at 25 years; the proportion of women who had given birth to their first child before turning 20 was lowest (at 3%); the number per live births were the second lowest; and the proportion of women who have ever experienced physical violence by a partner was the second lowest at 13%. (After 0% in Muslim Mindanao). [154]

160. The indigenous peoples in CAR still engage in some foraging, but most of the edible wild plants have been replaced by cultivable substitutes. Agriculture that they practice has become similar to that practiced by other farmers in the region, with a focus on rice farming and upland farming.[155] In Ifugao, a woman of prestige in the village ritually sows the first seeds of the planting season in a sacred place called "banao" at dawn. This ritual is performed to ensure good harvests at the beginning of the rice planting season.[156] Such rituals are in great decline, and most indigenous farmers encountered by the mission team could not recall any similar practices.

161. Despite the attachment to traditional farming strong enough to be influenced by some indigenous farmers in Cordillera, there is a general trend towards abandoning this tradition. They are increasingly influenced by the larger mainstream society, which affects their traditional resource management and puts additional pressure on them to meet the demands of the market economy while still meeting the needs.[157] The imbalance is exacerbated by increased population and demand per person for natural resources; as the indigenous peoples' traditional practices have not kept up with the changes. [158] In contrast, some traditions are in decline: the indigenous peoples have lost many elements so far. One tribe, Ibaloi of Pangasinan Province, is known for embracing entrepreneurship and innovation to adapt to changing circumstances by developing organic and sustainable agrobustro, but to date others have been less adept at adapting to external changes on their own.[159]

**Page 231**

162 Modern varieties of rice have been adopted by many, which requires synthetic inputs, but allow farmers to participate in the growing tourism industry thanks to their faster maturation. The modern rice cultivars are also more susceptible to diseases and pests than traditional rice varieties. For example, a large earthquake which weakened the terraces wall by digging tunnels through them.163 The natural forests in the ridges are the habitats of the striped shrew rat (Christomys whiteheadi) which feeds on these animals. This species is also found in the lowland areas and in the rice fields. However, such forests are in decline as are agricultural lands, because the younger generation prefers livelihoods that do not require them to be in the forest. The introduction of modern varieties of rice and non-organic farming has created negative impacts on soil and water, especially that the use of synthetic fertilizers and pesticides is increasing.164 The importance of traditional rice varieties was also recognized as the reason for infestation of earthworms and slugs in the rice paddies.165 The planting material for traditional trees in flugae (e.g., Samanea saman, Pteroporus indicus, Citrus aurantium) is still used by women to make a variety of products such as baskets, mats, and tools for food growing and widely available species (Swartzia macrophylla and Dendrocnide arborescens). No negative ecological impacts have been reported.

163 Indigenous women's knowledge in farming is strongly associated with traditional rice varieties and farming systems in CAR as well as in Mindanao. The critical role of older female farmers, especially as seed selectors in CAR, has declined with the introduction of non-traditional rice varieties; indigenous women are now more involved in other activities such as weaving or selling goods at markets, whose use requires intensive knowledge of the rice.165 As the region has opened up to tourism, there is enough demand for traditional products that typically pay better than farm work; agriculture has become mostly women's task in such cases.165

164 The traditional land-use zoning system in flugae includes mowing, which are private or clan-owned forests and usually predominate mountain peaks and mid-slopes.166 Each mowing is sized one half to three times the size of a rice field.167 The mowing is done by women who use traditional tools to minimize soil erosion.168 The flugae are well aware of the role of the forests as watersheds and their important contributions in maintaining the quality of water.169 Women also collect wood for fuel and house construction, in addition to edible fruits such as the aracea (Arecas catechu).170 The logger the woodlot, the more respect and recognition a clan of family receives.168

165 According to the Ifugao customary laws, gathering of dead branches as firewood in mowing by non-owners is allowed without a permission from the owner as long as the gatherer participates in cleaning and tending the forest as a form of payment for the wood collected. The law recognizes the crucial service provided by Ficus trees to regenerate undergrowth and afford harvesting them for timber or other uses. If the forest owner does not object, the gatherer is expected to dispose of the wood as much as possible under the belief that the spirits of their ancestors reside in these trees. In the old times, one could find a large number of Ficus trees in the forest used by the people as houses and sculpture. The forest owner decides which tree can be harvested and which person obtains the harvest; the latter is usually a close relative with two auspicious and clean areas of mowing.

166 When a child is baptized, the parents plant four seedlings in the mowing to pay for their blessing to the child, which prevents deforestation. The Ifugao people also plant trees as offerings when they are growing non-indigenous species such as Swietenia macrophylla and Gmelina arborea. Also preferred are local species such as Pinus merkusii, P. caribaea, and Cedrela odorata for timber harvesting and woodcarving. To discourage illegal hunting, trespassing on mowing is prohibited. If someone is caught while using others' mowing, the thief is taken to the tribal elders and will be severely reprimanded.

167 With respect to stand management, flugao forest owners engage in weeding to facilitate natural regrowth of trees and the trees only as needed—crooked and diseased trees for fuelwood and stray trees that are not part of the mowing. The practice of cutting down a tree in mowing is prohibited. Felling direction must be chosen so as not to damage other standing trees and nearby flora. In general, the Ifugao people do not cut down a tree or clear away the old seed plants at the same time warn the people in the vicinity of falling.

168 Forest management systems by other tribes in Cordillera are composed of similar practices that aim at sustainable use of forests, emphasizing soil and water conservation and encompassing maintenance of biodiversity. The Ifugao system is unique because it is practiced only in Abra Province, 200 km inland and tadlow in Mountain Province," and so on. The role of women in forest management constitutes a significant part of the traditional practices.

170 The lowland peoples in Mindanao organize their livelihood around flooded rice cultivation in the lowlands and swidden farming in the uplands.[1] One of the lowland peoples in Mindanao, B'laan, has a social organization different from the above two groups, with strong patrilineal traditions. The B'laan are known for their elaborate rituals and ceremonies, which include dancing, singing, and drumming. These ceremonies are often used to celebrate important life events such as weddings, births, and deaths.

171 Multinational enterprises have coveted Mindanao for its fertile land and mineral resources. Not only indigenous men, but also women have stood up against development projects, only to be ignored by the government. The Muslim population in Mindanao is estimated at 20% of the total population, with the majority being Sunni Muslims. In 2013, the island has seen armed conflicts involving Muslim groups which are not recognized as a separate ethnic group under Philippine law.

172 The Autonomous Region in Muslim Mindanao (ARMM) government allocated approximately 30% of its budget to personnel salaries, not leaving much for basic services such as education and health. The ARMM is also considered by many as not having been given autonomy in accordance with the Republic Act No.6734, the Organic Act for the Autonomous Region in Muslim Mindanao, while the public is wary of its relationship with Manila as well as its endemic corruption.

173 In 2015, a report from the United Nations Inter Agency Support Group on Indigenous Issues stated that armed groups used rape as a tool of war and terrorized indigenous communities.[2] Since the 1970s, more than 10,000 families of Teduray have been displaced.

# 4.8 Youth and Agriculture

The country has 30 million people between the ages of 10 to 24, representing 38% of the total population and the largest generation of youth in its history. Although girls and boys have equal access to education, girls may be at a disadvantage because, unlike for boys, girls are further handicapped by their lack of reproductive health care. The unemployment rate among those aged 15–24 years is three times higher than that for young adults older than the total population in 2019, and the gender gap was much wider: 3.7% versus 2%. This means that women are more likely to be unemployed.

The high level of unemployment among the youth means that the next generation is not obtaining sufficient experience and training to prepare them for their future roles as productive members of society when they enter the workforce, and this will have a negative impact on the economy. Labor participation rates follow similar trends, posing a considerable obstacle against empowerment of women.

The rate of one engagement in the Philippines is high, compared with other countries. However, young people aged 15–24 years are less likely to be engaged than male youth and rural youth much less than urban youth.

173 Occupational roles of agriculture have been long associated with very low income[20] and high costs from living in rural areas, leading farmers to discourage their children from taking up the family livelihood.[204] The young are pursuing non-agricultural professions, which bring in higher income, whether they stay in the rural areas or migrate to the cities.[205] Consequently, the agricultural sector is aging with a declining number of young people entering it. The Department of Agriculture has urged the youth public to take up agriculture and suggested as an entry point helping the older generation to pass on their knowledge and skills to them[206] and the Philippine Association of Agriculture has urged the youth public to take up agriculture and suggested as an entry point helping the older generation to pass on the digital information received on nutrient management to their parents and showed that they are willing to learn from their elders.[207] Recently stimulated by the COVID-19 pandemic, the awareness that the agriculture sector is important is rising among the young, and the Agriculture Students Association of the Philippines was created in August 2020.[208]

### 4.9 Women's Knowledge

174 Women’s knowledge of plants mainly stems from their role as cooks, gatherers, gardeners, herbalists, healers, and seed custodians,[209] and these functions are all performed by rural women in the Philippines.

175 Culinary choices and the post-harvest processes that support the dietary preferences are some of the most important drivers for knowledge acquisition on and conservation of plant biodiversity. Women’s roles as cooks and food preparers also make them key members of women’s societies, which are used as everyday food aid as well as famine food, fodder, mulch, medicine, fuel and material for construction.[210] They are also responsible for the preservation of seeds in environments ranging from wild seeds, ingesting grains, burning vegetation for new plant growth, replanting roots and so on.[211] The differences in responsibilities lead to variations in knowledge between women and men in terms of plants used and forest areas[212].

### Penetration of soil fertility may differ because of differentiated tasks

In Northern Mindanao, both women and men named the crops that each group was in charge of at all the ones that would grow well in good soil. Hence, for men they were large crops, such as maize and rice. For women, they were vegetables, but maize was also included. Maize was associated with the most with the worse soil by both groups. The association between maize and poor soil was stronger among women than among men.

### It is important to cultivate legumes, fruit trees and others around the house

Herbicides and maladies who treats illness using plants are mostly women. The knowledge of female herbicides has been increasingly recognized as the backbone of traditional medicine in the Philippines. Indigenous women have a deep understanding of the culinary, nutritional, processing and storage requirements, as it is their responsibility to cook and store the harvested crops. Women also have a good knowledge on how to preserve food for long-term use with the knowledge that men do not have the opportunities to acquire.

### Indigenous rice seed storage practices in the Philippines

Indigenous rice seed storage practices are diverse. Some of these include: (1) dry storage (in a container made from sturdy bark of dling tree which also serves as an insect repellent); baking (storage in a native basket-container for storage of rice before hauling and making of indigenous bamboo baskets); wet seed storage (in large bamboo pots), and soloy (storage in bottles especially for vegetable seeds).

### Corn has taken the farmers by storm in many parts of tropical Asia, especially on rainfed farms

Corn is now the most important crop in the Philippines. It is a major source of food and feed demand in livestock and its feed at the expense of traditional crops such as upland rice, adlai, and coffee. Adlai, also known as Job's Tears or Chinese pearl barley, is an alternative staple food to rice and a key ingredient for making various dishes such as pancit, adobo, and halo-halo. It can be used in tea, wine and beer, among other products. Above all, adlai grains can be processed and steamed into a porridge that is rich in protein and fiber. Adlai has been shown to have antinutrient and other medicinal properties that mitigate the symptoms of inflammation, allergies and diabetes. Thus, adlai grain has also proven resilient against extreme weather conditions and could be planted for human consumption (grains) and livestock feed (both grains and other parts.

### 5. SOCIAL PARTICIPATION OF RURAL FILIPINO WOMEN

#### 5.1 Rural Women's Voice in Politics

- **180** On women’s political empowerment, the World Economic Forum concluded in 2016 that the Philippines was the 13th best country measured by the ratios of females with seats in parliament over male, female ratio of national level over male, number of years with a leadership of state government near the last 50 years.[19] The Philippines ranked at position “5,” although it dropped to the 48th in overall gender parity ranking.[20] Another report by McKinsey in the same year reached the conclusion that women’s representation in the national parliament was only 7.3%, while the proportion of political representatives at the national level was 19.5%, where the global average was 23.4% and the Philippine average for the provincial and district/municipal levels were 19.8% and 21.4%, respectively.[21]

- **181** The higher representation at the national level is thought to be attributable to the domination of elites and political dynasties.[22] It is estimated that close to half of the women elected to the 12th Congress were wives of incumbent or former members of the House of Representatives.[23] For the 14th Congress in 2010, 15% of elected women were identified as wives of representatives.

185 Organized by the Department of Agriculture in the 1950s and supported by the local government units (LGUs), the Rural Improvement Clubs (RICs) are non-governmental, barangay-based organizations that seek to improve rural life and living standards through their members. Their activities include soil conservation and fishery enhancement projects, pest management, compost making, poultry/livestock/fisheries management to production and processing of fruits, vegetables, and other agricultural products.

186 In the Philippines, female farmers are active members of agricultural and non-agricultural organizations, both female only and mixed. The organizations have clear governance structures, and women have taken up important roles, such as president and treasurer. Women's representation in agricultural organizations is higher than men's, with women represented as men in agricultural organizations, and the National Coalition of Rural Women (Plaridel, 2018). The Philippine Gubatang Kalye Konsumyon or PKK—composed of organizations of female small-scale agricultural producers, fishers, indigenous peoples, and formal and informal workers in the rural areas—is in agreement.

### 6. RISKS AND OPPORTUNITIES FOR FEMALE FARMERS FACING CLIMATE CHANGE

187 For the most effective implementation of climate-resilient agriculture, all engaged in agriculture need to be familiar with the principles and methods, while sharing the risks and benefits. Many female farmers attend the trainings organized by the Department of Agriculture on behalf of the country's agricultural extension services (AES). However, women are not always involved in the final decisions after discussion with their wives. Women's leadership in decision-making is sufficiently common to be considered a normative practice in many communities.

188 The landless and/or the poorest are less likely to have the opportunities to attend trainings. They also lack access to credit and other financial services, which are critical for them to participate in climate-resilient agriculture. It is important that AES and other government agencies conduct inclusive targeting to engage poor or landless persons to engage in climate-responsive, gender-equitable and socially inclusive climate-resilient agriculture.

189 Such targeting not only includes women but also men who are less likely to be involved in agricultural practices, and regular monitoring to facilitate responsive management practices. Indigenous peoples have vast knowledge of local ecosystems, including indigenous varieties and breeds, which constitute the building blocks of climate-resilient agriculture as they are some of

The most resilient types in each locale even under climate change. Hybrid and non-native varieties require application of external inputs, often leading to negative effects on the environment and are unsustainable. Indigenous women have vast knowledge and skills related to indigenous species, most of which are not documented and hence lost as the occasion to make use of them dwindles. The communities need to be empowered with the knowledge and skills of using indigenous species by women, under climate change. Thus, it is important the project adequately promotes and enhances the role of women in agriculture. Women’s empowerment through their participation in agricultural activities, will strengthen the resilience of local men and women and the agro-ecosystems upon which they depend, leading to further general positive biodiversity benefits (e.g., through supporting seedbanks with local varieties).

190 As coping strategies in face of climate change, the Philippine farmers have resorted to: increased application of pesticides; planting of trees as windbreakers and for shade; changes in planting dates; use of chemical fertilizers; and introduction of hybrid and non-native species. And further reliance on craft making by women. Considering the extent of climate change to come, these measures will not be sufficient to ensure food security and livelihoods of farmers and their families, and could help them: the ease with which microfinance could be obtained, the usefulness of indigenous species in climate resilience; the Department of Agriculture’s programmes for climate change adaptation such as promotion of fodder crop and other indigenous crops as a sustainable source of livelihood.190

191 While some indigenous farmers are keen to continue practicing the traditional agriculture, many others have already abandoned their traditional practices and now rely on commercial farming and not sufficiently aware of the strengths of their own food production systems compared to modern agriculture; the indigenous systems are holistic in approach (agricultural rules are accompanied by socio-economic and cultural norms) and therefore more sustainable, resilient, productive and adaptable (the systems they have endured for centuries). The awareness of the indigenous peoples need to be raised on these issues. It is important that the project should not only address the changes that they face (socioeconomic and climate change) originate outside their line and are occurring at a faster pace than any other in the past, but internal and complementary information is likely to aid in strengthening the indigenous food production systems with respect to coping with these changes.

192 Climate change has already started to impact on some of the traditional agricultural practices that the farmers use to replant rice on some occasions. If the climate resistant agriculture that is best fit to the locale entails more work for women than they currently undertake, it would have its most negative effect on the health of the female farmers, because they are also in charge of the bulk of household chores and care giving.

193 Women around the world pay more attention to health issues, not only of themselves, but also of the entire family. Such interests in health issues can promote nutritional security, which is possible if women have access to information on nutrition and food safety. It has been shown that it is the women who have the knowledge of traditional medicine based on herbs. Since the medical system provided by the government is weak, women’s herbal knowledge must be encouraged.

Combined with their knowledge of the latest developments in agriculture obtained through their participation in agricultural extension services, training programmes and other activities within the agricultural sector, women are strongly qualified as the agents of change toward climate resilient agriculture. In areas where men have largely left agriculture for off-farm employment, women perform most

# Principles of Project Formulation and Implementation

## 7.1 Guiding FAO and GCF Policies and Frameworks

### 194 The following FAO and GCF policies and frameworks specifically guide the above assessment and project formulation:

- **FAO Policy on Gender Equality 2020**: Intends to achieve equality between women and men in sustainable agriculture and rural development for the elimination of hunger and poverty.
- **GCF Updated Gender Policy (2019)**: reinforces the responsibilities of GCF to the culturally diverse context, particularly with respect to addressing gender for the links between gender equality and climate change.
- **FAO Protection from sexual exploitation and sexual abuse (PSEA) N° 2015/7**: The principles of integrity and accountability are key factors for human rights and the implementation of GCF’s commitment to preventing and addressing acts of sexual exploitation and abuse (SEA).
- **FAO Policy on the prevention of harassment, sexual harassment and abuse of authority N° 2013/4**: The policy is based on the principle that zero tolerance for sexual harassment in all its forms is contrary to the United Nations Charter, the Staff Regulations and Rules, and the Code of Conduct for United Nations System Staff Members.
- **GCF Revised Environment and Social Policy and Policy on the Protection from Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (2021)** sets clear obligations for GCF projects to ensure that they are not linked with or contribute to SEA and to refrain from condoning, encouraging, participating in, or engaging in SEAH.
- **FAO Environmental and Social Management Guidelines (2015)** include general principles and specific guidelines on the following: 1) Elimination of discrimination; 2) Equal rights against discrimination practices; Equal opportunities for men and women to take part and to benefit; 2) ESS 7 – Recent Work Creation. Creation of better employment opportunities, particularly for women, in agriculture, forestry, fisheries, and other rural activities; 3) ESS 8 – Health and safety: Prevention of child labor, Forced labor, Workers’ and producers’ organizations; and 11 ESS 9 - Indigenous Peoples and Cultural Heritage. Identification of indigenous peoples, rights to land,

## 7.2 Principles of Project Formulation

### 7.2.1 Conceptual Framework of Change

195 Climate resilient agriculture will necessarily be based on agroecology, involving diverse indigenous species and landscape management for ecosystem resilience: it will take cue from indigenous knowledge systems that have been developed over centuries in the context of indigenous plants and farm animals, usually more extensive than that of male farmers. Women often have broader and more accurate interpretation of climate than men. Women possess the knowledge on herbal medicine, animal husbandry, and food preparation. They are also responsible for seed selection and storage as well, whose importance will only increase under climate change. Craft making based on indigenous plants is another area where women play a key role in the production of medicinal plants, which are important in remote areas with little access to modern medical services. The new farming systems built on agroecology will encourage cultivation of such plants, providing additional income for women.

196 The division of agricultural work in the Philippines may not be as clearly gender dependent as in other countries, but the general pattern seen elsewhere also prevails in the country: Food control tends to be a woman's domain. Women are responsible for most of the food preparation and harvesting and storing are prone to fall under women’s responsibility. The importance of water has only increased under climate change; despite its unrivalled availability, its distribution must meet the needs of both humans and animals, not just the high-value crops, but also the ones in the backyard taken care of by women.

197 The best climate-smart agricultural practices are those that build on traditional knowledge while creating new strategies. The strategies to cope with climate change already known in the Philippines include home gardens and backyard vegetable growing and animal raising as well as food foraging, which are women's tasks in the majority of cases. Additional attention can be provided through training and extension services to promote agroecological practices such as intercropping and vegetable growing and animal rearing, and foraging to strengthen food security and diversify production systems. Women also have a key role in water harvesting and management, including changing temperatures and precipitation patterns. In times of food shortages, women are often dependent on wild foods that they collect from forests and other natural resources. These activities, will have positive gender benefits in terms of climate-resilience, but also women’s health, nutrition and overall food security. Additional recommendations for empowering women through climate-resilient agriculture include:

- Promoting gender-orientation and gender awareness for men: "especially related to the roles of men and women and to support more equitable distribution of responsibilities to support women to overcome time constraints and other systemic barriers. Where possible,

**Page 243**

attention should be paid to men in leadership roles to serve as 'champions' to promote gender equality and the empowerment of women.

- Ensure CRA-related activities and community meetings are planned considering women's time constraints.
- Special attention should be paid to ensure the project and CRA activities do not negatively impact women's livelihoods or their ability to participate fully. This includes addressing the gender-differentiated constraints, contexts and priorities, and ensuring that it reflects both the needs of men and women in rural areas, including indigenous women, and men and women from marginalized households among other key beneficiaries.
- Increasing women's access to land and other productive resources is critical to contribute to value addition and the overall strengthening of incomes and livelihoods.
- Strengthening women's knowledge and ensuring adequate access to project funded activities (including training) will help increase their participation in decision-making processes.
- Ensuring space for women in climate-resilient value chains, where women should be supported as leaders to scale up best practices. This is particularly important in contexts that are typically male dominated.
- Women should be supported as leaders to scale up best practices.

198 Women are suited as agents of change as they have in general great willingness to improve their livelihoods and as their knowledge on elements of agriculture has not been fully integrated into the prevailing agricultural systems. This is particularly true for indigenous women, who are often more aware than men about the needs of their communities, including considering the differentiated needs of landless and poor women to improve their livelihoods (e.g., through small-scale livestock production). Women also have a greater understanding of gender issues and can help promote gender equality in agricultural production. This includes better representation of women in leadership positions, increased participation of women in decision-making processes, and greater involvement of women in the planning and implementation of agricultural policies.

7.3.2 Higher Equity in Strategic Decision Making

Non-trivial modifications of farming systems will require willing agents of change who are open to new ideas and practices. For example, if differentiated crops based on agroecology, farmers need to be interested in nutrition and possess knowledge of indigenous species. While women fit the bill much better than men for this role, they often lack decision-making power over their own lives and those of their families.

No crop production or water management is accomplished by men alone, and food and nutrition management is the responsibility of women; women need decision-making power, not limited to their own households but also in the community. Similarly sharing of power is also necessary with other vulnerable groups, such as youth. It is important that all members of each community attain its full potential and achieve sustainable development.

The chances are very high that climate change will increase the need for credit, which would push landless women into debt bondage. This is particularly true in the discriminatory preference of financial institutions for men as borrowers. The proposed project will engage with selected financial institutions to make sure that women have access to good quality, expensive technology and undocummented indigenous people have access to them more decision-making power. This is particularly relevant, as Versico et al (2021) noted for a case study in the Philippines that "women are the most vulnerable group in terms of financial inclusion and credit access. They are underfunded and are under strong pressure to bridge resource gaps. Hence, more women than men fall into chronic debt."


---
**Page 244**
---

### 202 Improvement in economic decision making of women will allow easier integration of their decisions with farming systems: broader observations on weather and climate, intensive knowledge on indigenous plants and animals, interests in food and nutritional security in addition to general agricultural production, and the ability to make better use of resources. In addition, communication, financial management and commercial negotiations will also contribute more effectively to the good of the community. In addition, youth empowerment will lead to better use of their capacity for innovation and creativity. The decision-making process should be such that all social status of vulnerable groups need to be endorsed by the communities concerned, most notably by the community heads/chiefs, to avoid regression.

#### 2.3.1 Women's Empowerment

203 Climate change at the current speed is unprecedented in human history; if we are to adopt a farming system that is sustainable, it must be a system that works with nature under climate change, not against. Farmers need to reclaim their capacity to work with nature, which includes their ability to read the signs of weather and climate and to adapt their practices accordingly. The reasons for various modern agriculture is not strongly based on the functionalities of nature; some traditional agricultural systems are more sustainable than modern ones because they have adapted and has disrupted some of the established relationships between observable phenomena and underlying causes.

204 While PAGASA enhances its capacity to provide information on weather and climate based on the latest technology and knowledge in climatology, their forecasts can be well complemented by the observations and forecasts by farmers based on the natural indicators. Such complementary has been noted for a long time; the more advanced the technology is, the more it depends on both types of forecasts and the actual events allows the different parties involved – PAGASA, DA and the farmers – to strengthen their capacities to understand and follow the evolution weather and climate. Climate’s particularity is that it is not only unpredictable but also has high cost-effectiveness, varies ownership; it is increasingly adopted around the world.

205 Working with nature also means that the technologies for agriculture must be adjusted, including those from PAGASA. As the agricultural sector in the Philippines is highly dependent on climate change to alter the ecological conditions progressively, farmers will need to constantly adjust their agriculture, which is most effectively done by close observation and adaptation on the ground without implementing external information, such as that from PAGASA and DA.

#### 2.3.4 Indigenous Knowledge

206 One of the salient features of climate change is that rainfall has become very uneven and untimely for agriculture. To thrive under this irregularity, flooding must be resisted and delayed, and floodwaters must be managed to ensure that they are not wasted but used in a productive manner. At the same time, farmers need to transform their farming systems into ones that “create” water to cope with drought conditions. Together it means that the systems need to be based on agroecology, which is exemplified by the indigenous knowledge of the people. The following are some examples of management of watershed management for agriculture: Since waterheds do not fall under the jurisdiction of

[1] Health Canada. 2004. Canadian Handbook on Health Impact Assessment. Volume I: The Basics. Ministry of Health, Canada. http://publications.gc.ca/collections/Collection/H3-2-04-SAE5.pdf (Accessed November 2009).


---
**Page 245**

main executing entity, the Philippine Department of Agriculture, the proposed project does not include watershed management as an activity and focuses on agriculture in the strict sense of the word. However, the project will consider watershed management of indigenous peoples, which is integrated already in their food production systems, in the framework defined by the Ancestral Domain Resources Management Plan (AD-RMP).

207 The indigenous food production systems, including weather and climate forecasting, have endured for centuries and proven their sustainability. Due to the changing environmental and socioeconomic conditions, however, these systems are under threat. Indigenous peoples' ability to sustainably have not been totally abandoned and are contributing to resilience to climate change. Many of the indigenous peoples are unaware of the gaining momentum for indigenous systems and tend to be more open to new ideas. The project will focus on strengthening the resilience of the overwhelming majority are indigenous. Their awareness in the indigenous culture and food production systems is a key factor in their ability to adapt to changing conditions.

Possible supplementary actions for strengthening the resilience of indigenous farming systems include increasing the capacity of indigenous farmers to access information about climate change and its connection with indigenous farmers; increasing the availability of effective inputs (seeds, fertilizers, water, weather information and credit); and improving the efficiency and equity of value chains.


### 7.1.5 Self-Reliance as Climate Change Resilience: Sustainability and collectibility

208 Ownership is paramount in making interventions meaningful and sustainable, and ownership is important for resilience. The project will ensure that the mechanisms are owned by the driver's seat, and their strengths, needs and limits should be well reflected in the project.

209 Resilience to climate change is not only a matter of knowing and practicing what it taught as suitable agricultural methods, but also of adjusting and developing the methods to the climate as it continues to change. The project will ensure that indigenous farmers are able to adapt without any external shock. Seed selection, storage and sharing, usually taken care by of women, are some of the activities that can be strengthened through the project. This is possible with the adoption of agroecology which minimally relies on external inputs; cultivation of legumes and climate-proof crops (e.g., root crops) in the backyard and other small areas, which is usually taken care of by women. Farmer-to-farmer knowledge sharing ability to cope and read the weather and climate on their own.

210 Many of these activities are infeasible or inefficient if the farmers do not join forces. Once acting together, they can achieve economies of scale and other benefits that give them a higher economic bargaining power than acting alone, which boosts ownership and sustainability.


### 7.3.6 Social Media for Rapid Communication

211 The people in the rural Philippines have little access to quality information, including that on government and NGO programmes which may be useful to them. The Filipinos, both rich and poor, utilize the media to get information about weather events such as typhoons and other extreme weather events, among others. The proposed project will make use of this already established mode for rapid communication of climate related information, bearing in mind that women have higher
