### 7.2 Principles of Project Implementation

#### 7.2.1 Framework of Ecological Agriculture

For agroecosystemic systems, farmers need to derive direct, sustainable and immediate benefits from the new system. In other words, a food production system should be an ecosystem, and diversification and landscape transformation should be guided by food and nutritional security, integrated pest management, and climate resilience.

#### 7.2.2 Sharing the Owner's Seat with Farmers

Application of agroecological principles results in the most positive socioeconomic outcomes when responsibility for land use is shared between farmers and other stakeholders, which points to the necessity of multi-scale and multi-stakeholder approach. Some of the most important actors in this approach are farmers, both female and male, and the proposed project will ensure the largest possible participation of women in all aspects of the project.

Climate and climate-resilient agriculture (CRA) information needed and voiced by women and men may vary because of their different roles and concerns at household and community levels. This will be taken into consideration in developing climate information services (CISs) for CRA training materials and training packages and CRA for Men, Women. No crop cultivation and sale are possible


---
**Page 247**
---

without women’s involvement, and gender parity will be sought in all participating parties: technicians/officials, and farmers. Women-headed households, if female farmers and farmer organizations/cooperatives led by women or with many female members will be prioritized in the identification of target beneficiaries for CCA enterprise training, development and investment planning. The percentage of female farmers and/or female-led organizations/cooperatives that will be farmers or technicians, will be 10%, 15% and 25%, respectively, to reflect the relative population size of each group. If the project focuses on one of these groups, the target for the technicians and farmers to be of that group is 100%.

### 218 Under this project, female and male farmers will participate in meetings that are customarily for government technicians or officials alone if the subject ultimately concerns the farmers. Such meetings will include a presentation by the project team on the project’s objectives, followed by a review on national agro-meteorological system and its applications. Trainings on basic functionalities, operation and maintenance of the systems at the district, sub-district, and provincial levels and Provincial CDS Centers will be given to female and male farmers alike so that their ownership of the systems and willingness to participate in their maintenance are strengthened.

### 219 Farmers will also participate in formulation of all production plans whose users are farmers themselves. This is done with the aim of ensuring that they can avoid unrealistic expectations on both sides and provide the farmers with what they do need and can use effectively. The engagement of women, poor women, indigenous women, and landless women is essential for each workshop, conducting each study and review, etc., for which the technicians/officials, private sectors, banks and financial institutions, and other stakeholders will be involved. This includes understanding their differentiated needs and constraints as well as their differentiated needs and constraints. This includes understanding the differentiated needs of and contexts landless women, poor women, and indigenous women are taken into account. Meetings will be held between relevant technicians/officials, the private sectors, banks and financial institutions, and other stakeholders to ensure that they are aware of each other’s strengths, as well as their differentiated needs and constraints. This includes understanding the differentiated needs and contexts landless women, poor women, and indigenous women are taken into account.

All field level trainings will be conducted under the framework of Farmer Field Schools, which build on farmers’ capacity and strength of adult learners and has proven to increase their capacity beyond the subject in question. In the same vein, interventions on weather/climate comprehension, seed procurement and finance will focus on strengthening of farmers’ capacities, particularly what women need to know about these issues. The trainings will be organized as a series of workshops and exchange; and fund-raising and management. For trainings that do not target farmers but others, such as CDA members, project staff, technicians/officials, etc., pre-training meetings will be organized with the aim of ensuring all parties have common understanding of the issues to be addressed and how to address them.

221 Farmers and Farmer Organizations will be encouraged to establish networks among themselves, and link with the AMA Village network for information sharing (weather/climate and others), agricultural material exchange, fund pooling and other types of mutual aid; women are known to be more proficient than men in networking establishments.

222 The proposed project promotes gender empowerment, Good inclusion and higher parity in decision-making. It will also add value to climate resilient agriculture through involving them in the preparatory meetings for each sub-activity discussed above. While Philippine women in general are not yet fully empowered, it is important that they be included in these discussions when men are also present. In order to boost women’s point of view and give women an opportunity to articulate their thoughts, a meeting among women only will be organized prior to the preparatory meetings for each sub-activity discussed above. The project team, including national technical/officials will also be present in the meetings for female farmers so that connection between the two groups can be made. It is expected that the views and suggestions of the participants in the plenary meetings will be women. Enhanced gender parity is also expected to reduce general gender inequality in the community. The decision-making process for the project will be made for indigenous peoples and youth. Farmer fields school for the indigenous peoples will include sessions on the Theater of the Oppressed to empower them as decision makers in the wider Philippine society.

223 As the project is expected to leverage more funds in the near future, use of household methodology to further enhance gender parity as well as more extensive use of Theatre of the Oppressed will be included in the proposed project. Such a methodology makes use of the full potential of women and other vulnerable groups improves the outcome of the project, the application of household methodology and the Theater of the Oppressed will organized at the beginning of each preparatory meeting.

224 Most female farmers already get by with less than eight hours of sleep per day. The manual labor for rice cultivation is quite strenuous for women, but the harsher weather brought about by climate change has to be faced by these women. The women’s share in agricultural tasks and accompanying changes for men as progress for the community. The roles and contributions of female farmers are not recognized or appreciated. This project will provide training and mentoring at all trainings so as to aid the acceptance of women as knowledgeable farmers capable of making wise decisions in agriculture.

225 Improved decision-making power of women may well make men feel unsure of their new role and perceived status. In order to assure them that the changes are good for them and the entire communities, the proposed project will work with the community heads/chiefs and Barangay committees to ensure that the changes are accepted by all members of the community and accompanying changes for men as progress for the community. The roles and contributions of female farmers are not recognized or appreciated. This project will provide training and mentoring at all trainings so as to aid the acceptance of women as knowledgeable farmers capable of making wise decisions in agriculture.

226 Female farmers who are heads of households are particularly vulnerable financially, since they do not have access to financial services which may provide the co-signature which is customarily required by many financial organizations for loans. The project will facilitate access to social schemes for female heads of households and other women farmers. These schemes include microfinance programs but they do not qualify for existing social protection schemes or loans. The project will facilitate their access to the schemes with emphasis on female indigenous persons who tend to be financially more vulnerable than male indigenous persons in modern society.

72.5 Building on the Strengths of Women, Indigenous Peoples and Youth

229 Women in general are better than men at forming groups among themselves for information exchange and sharing. They can be mobilized to form a women’s group with an information dissemination network using social media and a mutual aid system for the villagers. Farmers will also be encouraged to use social media to connect directly with urban customers and reap higher benefits thus through empowerment, they will also be trained on food production systems, risk management and finance access.

230 Usually, it is the women who are more open to the mobilization to train their household and community at the education level. They should be the first group to be targeted for any kind of finance related activities. Many women engage daily in commercial negotiations as they are considered more capable than men in this aspect; they should be further trained as negotiators at a larger scale.

231 A lack segregation in agriculture along the gender lines is minimal in the Philippines. Since it is best that both women and men have good understanding of the tasks that other gender is engaged in, farmers will be encouraged to focus on both female and male farmers with the same curricula which touches upon activities for all groups.

232 The indigenous peoples have strong foundation for climate resilient agriculture based on the indigenous food production systems and culture. They will be trained separately from the non-indigenous communities so as to focus on the strengths of the indigenous peoples and systems.

233 Youth will be trained on development of ICT products to make use of their interest and capacity in using electronic media, with a focus on mobile phone based technology for technical and financial sustainability.

72.6 Preventing increase risks of SGM and GBV

234 The engagement of women in new activities and their acquisition of new skills may upset the current gender balance and provoke SGM or even GBV. The project personnel may wield their new


---
**Page 250**
---

power that comes with the project to engage in SAEH and GBV. The community gatekeepers may use such development as something that needs to be tolerated in exchange for the opportunities: for prevention of SAEH and GBV, the project trains project-related personnel on the subject and sensitizes and mobilizes village health workers and Barangay committees for reporting and referral mechanisms. FAO’s Gender-based Violence Mechanism will be strengthened so that SAEH and GBV related grievances are adequately managed in inclusive, survivor-centred and gender-responsive ways. GBV pathways will be established to ensure access to appropriate services and referrals to survivors.

All these activities will be carried out in collaboration with UNFPA.

265 - Reporting of complaints and grievances: The project will establish a Complaint Resolution Mechanism (CRM) for FAO and project executing entities (EEs). CRM will be developed under the project and distributed to all project communities. A complaint and grievance feedback form, as well as a pamphlet explaining the mechanism, will be developed under the project and distributed to all project communities. The CRM will be available in English and local languages (as per the language pool).

271 - Project beneficiaries (including men and women from indigenous groups) will be clearly informed of the complaint and appeal channels (as described above, or as delineated through their FGD process). Women’s organizations and networks in the project area will be informed of the project and CRM, and information on the CRM provided to ensure they are able to refer cases. A detailed description of the CRM on the project’s GRM is provided in Chapter 5.6 of the ESMP in Annex 6a to the Funding Proposal.

8\.2 Consultation Questions

8\.2\.1 Core Consultation Questions for Institutions
- How are you dealing with climate change in your work?
- What is the most important gender issue in your work with farmers?

8\.2\.2 Core Consultation Questions for Female Farmers
- What are the main crops grown and women's involvement in different stages of crop production?
- What animals do you keep?
- Who owns which animals?
- How has the family situation changed in the past few years?
- Which farm tasks (if any) are performed by men? By women?
- Who sells agricultural and livestock products?
- Who keeps the cash income?
- Who decides on household spending?
- Are you engaged in vegetable cultivation?
- Do you have a mobile phone?

# PART II: GENDER ACTION PLAN

## INTRODUCTION

### 238 The Gender Assessment above included brief assessments of indigenous peoples and youth, as they represent two other important characteristics of the population that interact with gender in target regions. Gender gaps exist in these subgroups, but how these attributes interact with each other is not clear, especially given the generally more egalitarian nature of indigenous communities. In view of the importance of this issue, the proposed project will be called the Gender Action Plan (GAP). The project touches upon indigenous peoples and youth, while maintaining the primary focus on women.

### 240 Mainstreaming of gender, indigenous peoples and youth is seen from two angles: participation in activities; and the content of activities, where activities include events as well as development of events and products. Mainstreaming in terms of participation will be ensured by setting the target proportion of participants that mainly concern women, indigenous peoples and youths to be the same at the national level 50%, 12.5% and 25% for women, indigenous persons and youths, respectively. Of course in project areas where there are more indigenous people or youth than average, this will have a high majority depending on the local population. Mainstreaming in terms of content will be ensured by requiring the proportion of topics that mainly concern women, indigenous peoples and youths to be the same as at the national level 50%, 12.5% and 25% for women, indigenous persons and youths respectively.

### 241 Changes in the roles and status social of vulnerable groups need to be embraced by the communities concerned, most notably by the community heads/chiefs. Each FIS will include sensitizing sessions for community gatekeepers on social inclusion, its necessity and benefits. Since half of the person power participating in the project is women, it is expected that 50% or more of all activities and any activity will be for women/farmers/technicians. The Gender Action Plan claims half of the total project budget for gender mainstreaming. Indicators, targets and baselines are as shown below (Table B). In order to simplify the presentation of this Action Plan, each indicator is labelled, so which the Plan refers.

### 242 The day-to-day implementation of the GAP for the project will be led by the National Gender and Social Inclusion specialists, who will be recruited by the project. The specialists will work as part of the


---
**Page 257**
---

Project Management Office (PMO) and in close collaboration with the Regional Project Offices. The Specialist will collaborate with the implementing parties/sub-contractors to ensure adequate integration of gender differences as well as the need for inclusion and to leverage the complementary support that the Entities provide for the project. Specifically for gender issues, the Specialist will coordinate with the Philippine Commission on Women to achieve gender empowerment in the country in an effective manner.

[17] The number of population in Cordillera Administrative Region constituted 8.5% of the total population of target regions according to the latest available region-wide statistics (Section 4.1.2). About 99% of Cordillera inhabitants are said to be indigenous (Carino, 2013). The proportion of indigenous peoples in Northern Mindanao is unknown. Since 15-35% of total households believed to be indigenous live on the land area for 24.7% of the population aged 15–69 years and 8.5% of the population aged 0–14 years, the proportion of total population between the age of 15 and 24, 9.3% aged 20-24 and 6.6% aged 25-29 (Section 4.1.2), the youth population (persons aged 15–30 as defined by the country) is estimated 27%.

Component 3: Enabling Environment for Mainstreaming CRA

Expected gender outcome: Component 3 aims to raise awareness for CRA adoption at scale beyond the project target municipalities/provinces/regions, which is supported by strengthened capacities of Government agencies and LGAs in mainstreaming CRA into national and local policies and programmes and monitor CRA implementation. The expected gender outcomes are women from diverse social-cultural background (including indigenous women) who have access to climate responsive CRA supportive financial products. The expected gender outcome is Women from diverse social-cultural background (including indigenous women) who have maintained CRA, as well as CRA value chains and supportive financial products that improve women's access to finance and enable sustainable CRA adoption at scale.

GAP Output 3.1: Gender inclusive and gender sensitive CRA IEC materials and campaigns and national and local policies and programmes that have mainstreamed CRA.
